# Historical Newspaper Discoveries — Growing Research Ledger

**Period:** 1777–October 22, 2026 (research ranges: 1777–1785, 1786–1795, 1796–1805, 1806–1815, 1816–1825, 1826–1835, 1836–1845, 1846–1855, 1856–1865, 1866–1875, 1876–1885, 1886–1895, 1896–1905, 1906–1915, 1916–1925, 1926–1935, 1936–1945, 1946–1955, 1956–1965, 1966–1975, 1976–1985, 1986–1995, 1996–2005, 2006–2015, 2016–2025, and 2026 through October 22 only)  
**Last updated:** October 22, 2026  
**Primary archives:** [Library of Congress, Chronicling America](https://www.loc.gov/collections/chronicling-america/); [Hoosier State Chronicles](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/); [British Library's *Statesman* OCR collection](https://bl.iro.bl.uk/entities/product/2252a010-5953-4c65-8993-1be3da97d935)  
**Purpose:** Collect interesting reports published in period newspapers: unusual incidents, allegations, crimes, public controversies, science claims, disasters, and stories easily forgotten. **No entry implies a cover-up or suppression.**

## How to read this list

- **Newspaper report** means we located that claim in the digitized period newspaper. It does **not** by itself prove the incident occurred.
- **Corroborated context** means another named historical record or scholarly treatment was checked. This can confirm the context, contradict a claim, or establish that the story was a hoax.
- **Follow-up open** means later reporting has not been systematically traced—not that it does not exist.
- Printing dates are **not necessarily event dates**; papers frequently reproduced reports weeks or months old.
- OCR of eighteenth- and nineteenth-century newspapers contains errors. Uncertain proper names, dates, and precise amounts should be checked visually against page images.
- Twentieth-century full-page OCR can also interleave articles, captions, advertisements, and editorials; source genre and context are identified where material to the finding.

## Discoveries

### 1777

**HN-001 — July 22, 1777 — Alleged wind-gun assassination plot against George III**  
**Source:** *Dunlap's Pennsylvania Packet / The General Advertiser*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83021126/1777-07-22/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A London dispatch dated April 14 described information about a supposed plan to shoot George III with a “wind gun” on his return from Kew to Buckingham House.  
**Assessment:** **Reported as an allegation:** the printed text itself calls this a *pretended conspiracy*. The claim is not established. **Follow-up open.**

### 1778

**HN-002 — January 7, 1778 — Guard-ship prisoners' alleged plot and a barracks fire**  
**Source:** *Pennsylvania Packet*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83021127/1778-01-07/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** Prisoners on a harbor guard ship allegedly planned to destroy the guard; a ringleader was said to have been jailed. The same passage reports a barracks fire at Cobble Hill and suggests, without establishing, possible involvement by British prisoners.  
**Assessment:** **Two contemporary claims**, one explicitly speculative; investigation and outcomes not yet tracked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1779

**HN-003 — April 22, 1779 — American military-stores official placed in the pillory for fraud**  
**Source:** *Pennsylvania Packet*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83021127/1779-04-22/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** The paper identified Cornelius Sweyres, a deputy commissary of military stores, as convicted of fraud and forgery on three indictments, sentenced to the pillory, imprisonment, and a fine. His surname is taken from imperfect OCR and merits checking against the page image.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper report of a specific court disposition**; underlying court records not yet checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-004 — September 6, 1779 — An unnamed Franklin secretary allegedly jailed in Paris**  
**Source:** *New-York Gazette and the Weekly Mercury*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030493/1779-09-06/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A London item dated June 17 asserted that someone once employed as a secretary to Benjamin Franklin was arrested and jailed in the Bastille after allegedly obtaining goods from Bordeaux merchants with forged bills. The person was not named.  
**Assessment:** **Uncorroborated wartime allegation** republished in a Loyalist newspaper; do not attribute guilt to any individual. **Follow-up open.**

### 1780

**HN-005 — May 16, 1780 — Alleged detention of sailors and other people belowdecks**  
**Source:** *Virginia Gazette*, p. 1 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84024739/1780-05-16/ed-1/?sp=1).  
**Report:** A report about a Dutch vessel bound for Batavia described missing English sailors and around twenty other people allegedly chained in the hold.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper allegation;** independent corroboration and vessel identification remain open.

**HN-006 — October 11, 1780 — Alleged plot to kill Venice's Doge and Council**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1780-10-11/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A London dispatch dated July 4 said a Venetian representative had received word of a plot against the Doge and entire council. It claimed disclosure by a conspirator just before the planned attack and preparations for trials.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand diplomatic/news report;** Venetian records and identities not yet checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1781

**HN-007 — June 18, 1781 — Reported mutiny plan aboard the Continental frigate *Alliance***  
**Source:** *New-Hampshire Gazette / State Journal*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025584/1781-06-18/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** The Boston news column claimed that soon after departure from France, some of the *Alliance* crew intended to take over the ship and steer it to a British port. Captain John Barry reportedly confined three ringleaders in irons after severe whipping.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper account naming vessel and captain;** ship log/naval accounts should be checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-008 — August 8, 1781 — British sloop mutiny allegedly punished by six hangings**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 1 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1781-08-08/ed-1/?sp=1).  
**Report:** A London item dated April 17 claimed six sailors were hanged at the yardarm after a court-martial over a plan to seize a British sloop and take it to an enemy port. The vessel's name is unclear in OCR and must be checked visually.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper report of severe military punishment;** court-martial/vessel not independently verified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-009 — September 11, 1781 — Murder aboard the *Susannah* and an alleged oath of secrecy**  
**Source:** *Pennsylvania Packet*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83021127/1781-09-11/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** The London-to-Jamaica *Susannah* allegedly experienced a mutiny in which the captain, Ballantyne, was murdered. The report said uninvolved crew members were forced to promise silence, but disclosed the crime in Barbados; suspects were held in irons aboard HMS *Fox*.  
**Assessment:** **Specific contemporary account**, with a reported immediate outcome, but court records and ship documents not yet examined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-010 — September 11, 1781 — Portugal reopens the Távora treason convictions**  
**Source:** *Pennsylvania Packet*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83021127/1781-09-11/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A Lisbon letter announced a judgment reassessing people condemned, banished, or imprisoned under a January 1759 decision tied to the alleged attempted assassination of King Joseph I. The paper characterized the defendants very broadly as innocent.  
**Corroborated context:** [Brazil's National Archives historical Portugal collection](https://historialuso.an.gov.br/index.php?Itemid=215&catid=2064&id=5320%3Adecreto-de-d-maria-i-concedendo-a-revista-do-processo&option=com_content&view=article) documents the **May 23, 1781** rehabilitation of the Távora and Atouguia families and the controversy over the 1759 convictions. [Portugal's national archive](https://antt.dglab.gov.pt/exposicoes-virtuais-2/o_suplicio_tavoras/) documents the 1759 executions. The newspaper's all-encompassing wording should **not** be treated as a precise description of which defendants were exonerated.  
**Assessment:** **Well-established broader historical affair**, with a specific contemporaneous American newspaper report of the reversal. Further legal-judgment comparison would be valuable.

### 1782

**HN-011 — October 26, 1782 — A tale of a wrongful execution framed by the victim's daughter**  
**Source:** *New-Hampshire Gazette*, p. 1 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025585/1782-10-26/ed-1/?sp=1).  
**Report:** An article presented a cautionary tale: a son was executed for killing his father after evidence allegedly planted by his sister; years later she supposedly confessed as she was dying.  
**Assessment:** **Published moral/legal anecdote**, with no person, court, or location identified. It cannot yet be treated as a verified real trial. **Origin and follow-up open.**

### 1783

**HN-012 — December 4, 1783 — Charleston turns to a public lottery instead of an additional tax**  
**Source:** *The Gazette of the State of South-Carolina*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84026945/1783-12-04/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** The printed notice says Charleston's city council resolved to fund civic needs using a lottery after war-related financial distress. It outlines prizes, ticket prices, and deductions from winnings, explicitly describing the mechanism as a voluntary alternative to compulsory taxation.  
**Assessment:** **Primary-source ordinance/notice**, not merely a rumor. Follow-up on how the lottery actually performed is open.

**HN-013 — December 4, 1783 — The supposed cat-rat hybrid**  
**Source:** Same newspaper, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84026945/1783-12-04/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A short comic anecdote about the Earl of Rochester and Charles II describes an animal allegedly part cat, part rat.  
**Assessment:** **Clearly presented as historical wit/anecdote**, not a verified biological discovery. Kept as a curiosity illustrating what newspapers printed.

### 1784

**HN-014 — August 2, 1784 — The sensational Java poison-tree story**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1784-08-02/ed-1/?sp=3), and [July 26 preceding installment](https://www.njarchive.org/periodicals/NJA-P-P1125X6S/1784-07-26?view=transcript).  
**Report:** An account attributed to J. N. Foersch claimed that a Java tree produced a fearsome poison; experiments reportedly killed dogs and other animals within minutes, and claims extended to mass poisoning.  
**Corroborated context:** The story is a **historically documented hoax/myth**: see Tim Hannigan's research, [“Beyond control: Orientalist tensions and the history of the ‘upas tree’ myth”](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0021989418754345). The July 26 newspaper introduction itself expressed skepticism.  
**Assessment:** **Excellent documented example of sensational eighteenth-century misinformation**, not evidence of the extraordinary experiment claims.

**HN-015 — November 8, 1784 — Gunpowder-magazine explosion on Grenada**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1784-11-08/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A dispatch from St. George's, Grenada, dated September 4 attributed an explosion at Fort Frederick's powder magazine to lightning. It mentions about 150 barrels of powder, four deaths, and force strong enough to open doors and windows in town.  
**Assessment:** **Specific contemporary disaster report**; independent military or colonial records not yet consulted. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-016 — November 8, 1784 — A bizarre claim that dogs cured paralysis by absorbing it**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1784-11-08/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** In a separate piece, a Kingston report repeated a tale from France of a woman supposedly cured of palsy after sleeping beside three dogs, each of which reportedly became paralyzed and died.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified extraordinary medical tale, not credible medical evidence.** The printed text says a traveler vouched for it; identity and provenance unresolved.

### 1785

**HN-017 — March 25, 1785 — Rumored attack on a justice at a Nova Scotia birthday ball**  
**Source:** *Fowle's New-Hampshire Gazette*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025586/1785-03-25/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A Halifax/Annapolis item claimed a Captain Young organized around fifty men to kill a justice referred to as Bunhill during the Queen's birthday celebration and raid Annapolis, before being arrested.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper allegation with unresolved details.** Later local historical writing questions the person's name and whether the account can be matched to local recollections. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-018 — June 6, 1785 — Report of a bank forgery in Ireland**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1785-06-06/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** Letters from Ireland asserted that a substantial national-bank forgery had been discovered and three people had absconded. The amount is illegible in the OCR and is deliberately omitted.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand contemporary report**, no identities or court result established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-019 — June 20, 1785 — Claims about radical surgical experiments on animals**  
**Source:** *New-Jersey Gazette*, p. 1 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025471/1785-06-20/ed-1/?sp=1).  
**Report:** Reprinted London news claimed surgeons had developed a method for immediately stopping bleeding and reuniting cut arteries. It described an amputated pig leg and, in an especially extraordinary claim, a dog whose head had been nearly severed except for its windpipe yet allegedly walked around afterward.  
**Assessment:** **Extraordinary medical claim**, not independently validated. **Follow-up open.**

### 1786

**HN-020 — May 25, 1786 — Five dungeons allegedly uncovered after a Spanish convent fire**  
**Source:** *Fowle's New-Hampshire Gazette and General Advertiser*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025586/1786-05-25/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A dispatch attributed to Alcántara, Spain, and dated February 10 claimed that a February 3 fire at a convent of the Minorities exposed five underground dungeons containing human remains.  
**Assessment:** **Extraordinary secondhand report, not an established archaeological discovery.** No Spanish institutional record has been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-021 — July 20, 1786 — A seven-hour fight allegedly ends a Mediterranean pirate ship's career**  
**Source:** Same Portsmouth newspaper, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025586/1786-07-20/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A Naples dispatch dated April 24 claimed that an Algerian frigate called *Sultana* captured a 26-gun pirate vessel off Messina after seven hours. It alleged that only eighteen of roughly 200 pirates remained fit to fight.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed maritime claim republished from London papers**, but neither vessel's identity nor battle losses have been independently confirmed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-022 — September 21, 1786 — A man confesses to a seven-year-old murder after frightening visions**  
**Source:** Same Portsmouth newspaper, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025586/1786-09-21/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A London report said Jarvis Matcham voluntarily told Salisbury's mayor that he had killed a drummer named Jones about seven years earlier, after what he described as terrifying apparitions in a thunderstorm. The paper printed a letter attributed to the Earl of Sandwich stating that a drummer had indeed been killed near Huntingdon at about the reported time and urging that Matcham be detained for inquiry.  
**Assessment:** **A reported confession plus an attributed official letter;** the visions are Matcham's assertion, and no trial outcome has been traced. **Follow-up open.**

### 1787

**HN-023 — June 29, 1787 — Counterfeit-money defendants held for trial amid treason reprieves**  
**Source:** *The New-York Packet*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030137/1787-06-29/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A short item named Elisha Fobes of Southwick and Joseph French of Chester, Massachusetts, as jailed in Litchfield, Connecticut, pending trial for allegedly counterfeiting and circulating that state's paper currency. Directly afterward the page reported an additional six-week reprieve, according to rumor, for four Massachusetts prisoners sentenced to death for treason.  
**Assessment:** **Specific arrests and a separately reported reprieve**, with no guilt or sentence confirmed for the counterfeit defendants. Court and pardon records remain to be checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-024 — July 7, 1787 — A physician's premonition allegedly prevents the murder of his family**  
**Source:** *New-Hampshire Gazette*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1787-07-07/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A London account dated April 20 described a Dr. Meggs abruptly returning from the Isle of Wight after imagining that his wife and child had been murdered. His family was safe, but a servant reportedly confessed that she and an accomplice intended to kill them and rob the home; the newspaper claimed she repeated this under oath before a magistrate.  
**Assessment:** **Sensational premonition anecdote, not verified as a real prosecution.** The printed mention of an oath does not substitute for a magistrate's record. **Follow-up open.**

### 1788

**HN-025 — April 14, 1788 — The nonexistent transatlantic corn shipment**  
**Source:** *The Daily Advertiser* (New York), p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030007/1788-04-14/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A report reprinted from Britain said a London commercial house received apparently convincing letters from Philadelphia about an incoming cargo of corn. A supposed captain's arrival at Dover induced acceptance of a £700 sterling bill, which a fraudster exchanged for £400 cash and the rest in goods. The ship, captain, and cargo never materialized.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed alleged commercial fraud**, but the company and offender are unnamed and the subsequent investigation is not known. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-026 — June 17, 1788 — Schooner *Two Friends* double murder, with a documented later outcome**  
**Source:** *The New-York Journal and Daily Patriotic Register*, p. 3 — [June 17 examinations](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030566/1788-06-17/ed-1/?sp=3); p. 2 — [July 1 outcome report](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030566/1788-07-01/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** The first report reprinted statements attributed to William Cain and Richard Williams (also known as John Davis), taken before Charleston magistrate John Buchanan after the May deaths of Captain Nathaniel C. Webb and the schooner's owner, named Clode/McClode in varying print. The two accounts differed on participation. The July 1 newspaper **reported that Cain and Williams were convicted by an Admiralty Sessions court and executed in Charleston on June 16** for the murders aboard *Two Friends*.  
**Corroborated context:** Later scholarship on Charleston capital punishment also records the 1788 piracy executions, although the original trial record has not been retrieved. [Historical study of Charleston capital punishment](https://paperzz.com/doc/8148366/theater-of-death--capital-punishment-in-early---d).  
**Assessment:** **Contemporaneous accusations with a separately located newspaper court-and-execution outcome.** This story *has* a documented follow-up, so it is **not** in the unresolved-outcome category.

### 1789

**HN-027 — October 17, 1789 — A colonial letter alleges a large arson plot on Martinique**  
**Source:** *The Newhampshire Gazetteer*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023133/1789-10-17/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A letter purportedly sent from Dominica on September 6 alleged that enslaved people on Martinique planned fires in more than a hundred buildings at Saint-Pierre and attacks during the chaos. It asserted forty people were imprisoned and predicted severe executions.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified allegation in a colonial slaveholding context**, carrying evident potential for panic and bias. Neither the plot nor the threatened punishments is independently established; the article must not be used as proof that the accused committed the acts. **Follow-up open.**

### 1790

**HN-028 — February 3, 1790 — Alleged plan to explode officials' homes in Brussels**  
**Source:** *The New-Hampshire Gazette and General Advertiser*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1790-02-03/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A London reprint of a Brussels letter said a conspiracy was discovered to undermine and blow up the homes of officials identified in OCR as Dalton and Tranfmandroff, seize an arsenal and city gate, and admit political exiles.  
**Assessment:** **Political conspiracy allegation at secondhand remove** during unrest in the Austrian Netherlands. Names and outcome require independent records and page-image checks. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-029 — February 3, 1790 — A woman and accomplice arrested in a Virginia murder allegation**  
**Source:** Same newspaper, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1790-02-03/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A Winchester, Virginia, item dated January 6 said Jane Logan had been confined in the Martinsburg jail, accused of helping a man kill another person with fireplace tongs. It said an alleged accomplice was also imprisoned.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary arrest allegation, not a determination of guilt.** Criminal proceedings and the victim's identity remain untraced. **Follow-up open.**

### 1791

**HN-030 — February 26, 1791 — Alleged widespread counterfeiting of public-debt securities**  
**Source:** *The New-Hampshire Gazette and General Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1791-02-26/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A printed correspondent claimed that forged state and Georgia “general indents” had injured merchants speculating in public securities, affecting traders in New York too. One claimed loss was £20,000 sterling; others allegedly lost £1,500–£3,000.  
**Assessment:** **Specific financial-crime allegation;** the excerpt opens midstream in OCR and the quoted losses have not been matched to records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1792

**HN-031 — July 26, 1792 — Seven suspected pirates jailed in New Bern, North Carolina**  
**Source:** *The New-Hampshire Gazette and General Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1792-07-26/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A one-sentence notice asserted that seven alleged pirates accused of murdering Captain Cleary and the mate of the brig *Baillie* of Soufrière were confined in New Bern's jail.  
**Assessment:** **Specific short contemporary custody report, with no named defendants** and no accompanying trial result. The brig's spelling should be visually checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-032 — November 7, 1792 — A reported Dublin haunting loses its ghost**  
**Source:** *Gazette of the United States*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83030483/1792-11-07/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A story described a woman frightened from her home by an alleged black-robed apparition and extraordinary noises. Crowds gathered. A man agreed to watch overnight with a dog and loaded pistols; he reported hearing sounds but seeing no ghost, and the apparent haunting ceased.  
**Assessment:** **Period ghost-and-skepticism anecdote**, not evidence of a supernatural event. The newspaper does not establish who made the noises. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-033 — December 19, 1792 — Cherokee chief Hanging Maw answers accusations in print**  
**Source:** *The New-Hampshire Gazette and General Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025587/1792-12-19/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** The paper reproduced a response attributed to Cherokee leader Hanging Maw following charges made by William Cocke. The speech challenged blaming all Cherokee people for particular killings and argued that payments by settlers were prices for ceded land, not purchases of goodwill or permanent political submission.  
**Assessment:** **Important attributed Indigenous political rebuttal in a republished newspaper text.** The precise original speech/transcription and its editorial chain have not been authenticated. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-034 — December 24, 1792 — Reported assassination plot against the King of Prussia**  
**Source:** *The Patowmac Guardian and Berkeley Advertiser*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84038410/1792-12-24/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A European item alleged that a man styled L'Evesque, described as a Strasbourg scientist, had confessed to a plot against the Prussian king and to receiving an advance of 50,000 livres; the report implicated French revolutionary agents.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified wartime assassination accusation** and potentially partisan propaganda. No independent confession or judicial record checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1793

**HN-035 — January 12, 1793 — A lurid atrocity tale attributed to revolutionary Paris**  
**Source:** *The Kentucky Gazette* (Lexington), p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn82014782/1793-01-12/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A reprinted account alleged that a crowd of children attacked and killed two small children identified as aristocrats during revolutionary violence in Paris, and followed this with other horrifying claims about the September massacres.  
**Assessment:** **Sensational, politically charged secondhand atrocity narrative; uncorroborated at the level of these specific details.** The historical existence of revolutionary massacres does not independently establish this story. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-036 — October 1, 1793 — A rumored assassination attempt against French officials in New York**  
**Source:** *The American Herald of Liberty*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023134/1793-10-01/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A reprinted letter said an alleged plot against French envoy Edmond-Charles Genêt and Admiral Cambis had been discovered in New York, claimed Cambis had defended himself with a cane and suffered injuries, and connected the supposed plot to sailors on the ship *Jupiter*.  
**Assessment:** **Uncorroborated allegation of an assassination plot.** Serious unrest involving Cambis and his crew appears in later naval histories, but that does not establish the letter's proposed motive or Genêt assassination claim. **Follow-up open.**

### 1794

**HN-037 — March 11, 1794 — French officials report a plot to surrender Atlantic ports to Britain**  
**Source:** *The American Herald of Liberty*, p. 1 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023134/1794-03-11/ed-1/?sp=1).  
**Report:** A report attributed to commissioners at Rochefort asserted that papers on a vessel called *L'Apollon* showed an English-backed effort to deliver Rochefort, Brest, Lorient, Saint-Malo, Bordeaux, and other ports to enemy control. An engineer aboard was said to have killed himself when detected; the report also spoke of executions.  
**Assessment:** **Claim attributed to a belligerent revolutionary government**, printed amid severe repression. The claimed papers, individual accused, and executions have not been independently verified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-038 — October 15, 1794 — Reported executions over a plot against Sardinia's royal family**  
**Source:** *Gazette of the United States and Daily Evening Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84026271/1794-10-15/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A dispatch headed Turin, July 24, asserted that a conspiracy against the King of Sardinia had been uncovered and that three named defendants, including a captain and a count, were executed at the city's citadel, with their bodies burned.  
**Assessment:** **Specific but distant wartime report**, with names imperfectly rendered by OCR and no local tribunal records checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1795

**HN-039 — January 8, 1795 — New Haven publishes a startling annual death count**  
**Source:** *Gazette of the United States and Daily Evening Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84026271/1795-01-08/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A short statistical notice attributed **191 deaths in New Haven during 1794**, including 50 from scarlet fever and 63 from yellow fever. It listed 3,471 inhabitants in a 1791 count and noted that 1794 mortality was over one-twentieth of that population.  
**Assessment:** **Direct period statistical assertion**, not an independently audited death register. The categories are partially garbled in OCR and should be checked against local death records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1796

**HN-040 — April 21, 1796 — A captain claims Seville was devastated by an earthquake**  
**Source:** *The Patowmac Guardian and Berkeley Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84038410/1796-04-21/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** In a very brief Alexandria notice dated April 12, Captain Black of the ship *Ann*, recently from St. Ubes, purportedly corrected an earlier report: it was **Seville**, rather than Cádiz, that suffered an earthquake, and most of the city had been destroyed.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified secondhand disaster claim.** The extraordinary destruction figure is only what this newspaper asserted; it has not been reconciled with Spanish earthquake records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1797

**HN-041 — January 11, 1797 — Reprinted account of murder committed under claimed divine instruction**  
**Source:** *The Kentucky Gazette*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn82014782/1797-01-11/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** The page concludes an older-sounding narrative about a family whose children, identified in the OCR as David and John Dutartre/Durartre, were reportedly pardoned after executions of other relatives. According to the tale, one later killed another person while asserting divine command and was condemned again.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted historical/religious cautionary narrative, not a documented January 1797 crime.** The story begins on an earlier page, and its underlying date, venue, names, and court records remain unresolved. **Follow-up open.**

### 1798

**HN-042 — January 10, 1798 — William Blount's alleged explanation of a British-backed Florida scheme**  
**Source:** *The New Hampshire Gazette*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025588/1798-01-10/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** Under a heading about British intrigue, a letter attributed to a correspondent in Jonesborough claimed former governor William Blount privately described a plan involving British ships, Native American allies, and an expedition into Spanish Florida and Louisiana. The letter attributed extraordinarily ambitious demands for political power to Blount.  
**Assessment:** **An attributed, politically explosive account—not a verified verbatim confession.** The Blount affair has a documented historical setting, but this particular correspondent's account needs separate authentication. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-043 — January 30, 1798 — Witnesses claim a white-clad ghost haunted Newgate prison**  
**Source:** *Virginia Argus*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84024710/1798-01-30/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** Reprinting a purported New York account dated January 10, the paper told of a woman's apparition wandering between rooms of a Newgate jail. Several named prisoners were credited with accounts of bed curtains opening, sudden disappearances, and a bright orb bursting in the hallway.  
**Assessment:** **A period ghost narrative recording allegations by witnesses, not evidence of a supernatural occurrence.** Its original publication chain and any mundane explanation are not yet checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1799

**HN-044 — January 8, 1799 — A $100 reward for an alleged traveling physician-forger**  
**Source:** *Gazette of the United States and Philadelphia Daily Advertiser*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025881/1799-01-08/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A detailed Boston notice accused a man calling himself a physician—his name appears in the OCR approximately as **Joseph Haslett**—of forging a **$680.45** note against a Philadelphia merchant and defrauding other merchants. It offered **$100** for his apprehension and included a physical description.  
**Assessment:** **Specific public accusation and reward notice**, not a finding of guilt. Name spelling, alleged earlier offenses, and outcome still need checking. **Follow-up open.**

### 1800

*No sufficiently specific additional discovery selected from the limited 1800 preview sample on this pass; the year remains in the search queue.*

### 1801

**HN-045 — January 13, 1801 — Bank shareholder alleges a campaign to destroy a rival institution**  
**Source:** *The Republican Ledger*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023378/1801-01-13/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** An extended signed letter from a shareholder of the defunct **New-Hampshire Union Bank** accused competing bankers and the state legislature of exclusionary measures that ruined a second Portsmouth bank and harmed its shareholders. It described repeated petitions and asserted that the older bank enjoyed an improper monopoly.  
**Assessment:** **One side of a live political-commercial dispute**, not independent proof of the alleged motives or legislative impropriety. The rival bank's position and enactments have not yet been compared. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-046 — January 17, 1801 — Four condemned highway robbers barricade themselves and attempt suicide**  
**Source:** *The United States Oracle of the Day*, supplement p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025544/1801-01-17/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A European dispatch about Bourg, France, described four condemned highway robbers obtaining cutting weapons, freeing themselves from irons, and resisting their jailer ahead of an October execution. Two reportedly died in the episode; two were taken alive toward punishment.  
**Assessment:** **Sensational foreign crime report**, reprinted months after the alleged event. Names are uncertain in OCR, and the tribunal and casualty details have not been verified. **Follow-up open.**

### 1802

**HN-047 — January 19, 1802 — Report claims another planned uprising was intercepted on Guadeloupe**  
**Source:** *The New Hampshire Gazette*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025588/1802-01-19/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A Baltimore dispatch attributed to Captain Vibert of the schooner *Exchange* asserted that a planned Black uprising against the government associated with Pélage was discovered and four supposed ringleaders arrested. The newspaper repeated dramatic claims about the size and intentions of the alleged movement.  
**Assessment:** **Highly partisan colonial reporting about alleged revolt and repression.** The source's racial framing, reported numbers, arrests, and purported intentions must not be accepted as verified. **Follow-up open.**

### 1803

**HN-048 — January 5, 1803 — France reportedly threatens Algiers over fishery rights and a $200,000 demand**  
**Source:** *Alexandria Advertiser and Commercial Intelligencer*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84024011/1803-01-05/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A reprinted Constantinople dispatch about French diplomatic demands stated that the Dey of Algiers had obstructed France's coral fishery, insulted its flag, and demanded **$200,000**, after which France discussed an expedition and demanded satisfaction.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper's account of an international ultimatum and claimed extortion**, not independently confirmed diplomatic correspondence. The parties' own documents are unexamined. **Follow-up open.**

### 1804

**HN-049 — January 10, 1804 — An elaborate explanation offered for the vanished La Pérouse expedition**  
**Source:** *Oracle Post*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023351/1804-01-10/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A story attributed through the *Salem Register* to a New York paper purported to explain the disappearance of the French explorer's expedition: sailors allegedly took islanders' timber without asking, were attacked, and a handful escaped with expedition papers only to die after being rescued by a Danish vessel.  
**Assessment:** **Striking but uncorroborated explanation for a historical maritime mystery.** The page begins mid-story, and this particular purported survivor account has not been authenticated. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-050 — January 10, 1804 — Alleged $40,000 Hartford banking forgery and an absconding debtor**  
**Source:** *Oracle Post*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023351/1804-01-10/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A purported Hartford letter dated December 24 alleged that Major **Joseph Hart** left town after his brother rejected signatures used to secure discounted bank notes as forgeries. The writer said additional forged endorsements totaling approximately **$40,000** surfaced, claimed suspected accomplices were questioned, and mentioned a **$1,000** reward.  
**Assessment:** **A detailed but politically colored accusation** reproduced from a correspondent; no court case or banking ledger has been reviewed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-051 — January 10, 1804 — Survivors describe the brig *Venetia* wrecking on a reef**  
**Source:** *Oracle Post*, p. 2 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn84023351/1804-01-10/ed-1/?sp=2).  
**Report:** A first-person account from a passenger aboard the brig *Venetia*, said to be bound for New Orleans, described the vessel striking a reef, passengers scrambling into boats, shelter on a mangrove-covered island, and discovery of a fishing vessel after roughly five days. The letter praised passenger John O'Maill for abandoning his own goods so people could fit in the boat.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted survivor narrative**; the precise voyage chronology and crew records have not been independently checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-052 — January 13, 1804 — Haitian military leaders address reports of killings during revolt**  
**Source:** *The National Intelligencer and Washington Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83045242/1804-01-13/ed-1/?sp=3).  
**Report:** A proclamation dated **November 29, 1803**, bearing the names **Dessalines, Christophe, and Clervaux**, acknowledged that some inhabitants had been killed amid the upheavals in Saint-Domingue. It expressed regret and maintained that the violence was contrary to the leaders' wishes and difficult to prevent during war.  
**Assessment:** **Primary-period publication of leaders' political defense**, not independent proof of their command responsibility or lack of it. **Follow-up open.**

### 1805

**HN-053 — January 1, 1805 — A correspondent describes a short, violent Jamaica earthquake**  
**Source:** *The New Hampshire Gazette*, p. 4 — [original page](https://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83025588/1805-01-01/ed-1/?sp=4).  
**Report:** A letter reprinted from the *Kingston (Jamaica) Royal Gazette* described a single strong tremor lasting about four seconds, unusual atmospheric stillness, and agitated animals. It placed the shock at **11:05 p.m. on a Thursday the 30th**, but the original letter's publication month is not evident from the reprint.  
**Assessment:** **Dated American reprint of a first-person natural-phenomenon report**; the original Jamaican issue, date, and independent seismic record are not checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1806

**HN-054 — July 24, 1806 — A bogus banknote is used to purchase a horse**  
**Source:** *The Statesman* (London), p. 4, British Library digitized OCR — [source collection and downloadable year file](https://bl.iro.bl.uk/entities/product/2252a010-5953-4c65-8993-1be3da97d935).  
**Report:** A court dispatch described a seller accepting a purported banknote as payment for a horse, only to discover it was a fake instrument styled as a **“Debtors' Bank”** note. The newspaper reported a conviction. The accused contended that he could not read and had been deceived by whoever gave him the note.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper report of a particular court result**; the defendant's identity is difficult to read in OCR, and original court papers have not been consulted. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-055 — July 24, 1806 — Newspaper accuses rivals of manipulating peace rumors and public funds**  
**Source:** *The Statesman* (London), p. 3, British Library digitized OCR — [source collection and downloadable year file](https://bl.iro.bl.uk/entities/product/2252a010-5953-4c65-8993-1be3da97d935).  
**Report:** An editorial accused rival newspapers and interested speculators of circulating false stories about negotiations between Britain and France, including diplomatic missions supposedly underway, with stock-market consequences. It contrasted its own earlier reporting with those rumors.  
**Assessment:** **A newspaper's adversarial editorial allegation**, not verified proof of deliberate market manipulation. It is valuable as a contemporary account of information and speculation during wartime. **Follow-up open.**

### 1807

**HN-056 — December 2, 1807 — Reported mutiny by forty Canadian militiamen unwilling to fight Americans**  
**Source:** *Western Sun* (Vincennes, Indiana Territory), p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18071202.1.3).  
**Report:** A letter reprinted from a Canadian/Windsor paper claimed that men ordered into militia service resisted selection; some reportedly announced they would join Americans rather than fight them. **Forty** men were reportedly detained for mutiny pending trial.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand newspaper claim**, without Canadian militia rolls or tribunal outcomes checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-057 — December 2, 1807 — Correspondent says a fire destroyed some 200 Fredericksburg buildings**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18071202.1.3).  
**Report:** An October 21 letter from Fredericksburg, Virginia, said a recent disastrous fire had destroyed at least **200 houses and other buildings** and put losses at roughly **$400,000**, leaving residents displaced.  
**Assessment:** **Specific contemporary loss estimate in a reprinted letter**; city and insurance records have not been compared. **Follow-up open.**

### 1808

**HN-058 — October 22, 1808 — Indiana Territory reports a murder conviction and scheduled hanging**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18081022.1.3).  
**Report:** The Vincennes newspaper reported that a special court convicted **Abraham Hiley** (surname uncertain in damaged OCR) of murdering **John Coleman** (also uncertain in OCR), pronounced a death sentence, and named **October 29** as the intended execution day. It promised a fuller trial report.  
**Assessment:** **Court outcome as reported at the time**, not proof that the announced execution occurred. See the paper's later political discussion, HN-059. **Follow-up open.**

### 1809

**HN-059 — September 2, 1809 — Public defense of governor alleges powerful figures behind an earlier murder**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18090902.1.2).  
**Report:** An opinion piece discussing the convicted man named approximately as **Hiley** (see HN-058) argued that he was only a subordinate participant in a killing, while more powerful people—described as including officers and judges across the Kentucky border—were the real organizers. It defended Governor William Henry Harrison's reluctance to execute the convicted man when those figures had not been brought to justice.  
**Assessment:** **A consequential but politically charged defense and allegation**, not independent evidence against the unnamed officials. It supplies another dated newspaper discussion of the 1808 case. **Follow-up open.**

### 1810

**HN-060 — May 12, 1810 — Murder prosecution of a land commissioner triggers claims of a land-fraud scheme**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18100512.1.3).  
**Report:** A Kaskaskia correspondent described proceedings against **Colonel Michael Jones**, a land-claims commissioner, as an alleged accessory to the murder of a younger man, **Rice Jones**. The letter contended that large land claimants and questionable testimony lay behind a malicious prosecution intended to interfere with land-claim decisions, and mentioned contemplated civil actions for damages.  
**Assessment:** **One-sided account of a real reported proceeding, with severe accusations against identified opponents.** Nothing here establishes that the prosecution was fabricated or that the land claims were fraudulent. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-061 — May 12, 1810 — Liverpool church tower falls through the building before services**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18100512.1.3).  
**Report:** An English notice said the tower of **St. Nicholas's Church, Liverpool**, collapsed shortly before worship on a February morning, bringing down the spire, bells, and gallery. It said **nineteen people** were taken from the ruins; the OCR does not clearly establish a count of deaths.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted disaster report**. Its exact original date, casualties, and aftermath are open for later examination. **Follow-up open.**

### 1811

**HN-062 — June 8, 1811 — A press account alleges widespread forging of U.S. ships' papers in Britain**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18110608.1.2).  
**Report:** A reprinted *Boston Chronicle* discussion of American shipping in Napoleonic France argued that French officials were scrutinizing vessels' documentation because **American ship papers had allegedly been forged in England**, naming someone the OCR renders as “Van Sander.” The article was explicitly partisan about competing accounts of maritime restrictions.  
**Assessment:** **Political newspaper allegation**, not confirmed instances of particular forged papers or a verified perpetrator's name. **Follow-up open.**

### 1812

**HN-063 — June 2, 1812 — Reports claim a deadly mutiny between Canadian militia and regular troops**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18120602.1.2).  
**Report:** Reprinted reports claimed an attempted flogging at York, Upper Canada, escalated into a shooting and an armed clash between militia and British regulars, allegedly leaving **thirty dead** and bringing detentions and threats of transportation. The paper also described unrest around loyalty oaths.  
**Assessment:** **Dramatic, pro-American wartime rumor presented through multiple secondary dispatches.** The asserted clash, totals, and detentions have not been independently confirmed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-064 — June 2, 1812 — A ship captain reports fever and severe food shortages in the Canary Islands**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18120602.1.2).  
**Report:** A Baltimore-arrival report attributed to Captain Adams of the ship *Stafford* described an epidemic in Tenerife, hunger severe enough that people reportedly ate animals ordinarily not used for food, crowds at scarce grain distributions, and sailors requiring emergency feeding. It printed numbers of illnesses and deaths that need checking against original records.  
**Assessment:** **Captain-attributed humanitarian disaster testimony**, reproduced in an American newspaper; reported counts, precise locations, and dates are unverified. **Follow-up open.**

### 1813

**HN-065 — December 17, 1813 — Newspaper says a line of underwater torpedoes was planned near Craney Island**  
**Source:** *Western Eagle* (Madison, Indiana Territory), p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WEE18131217.1.3).  
**Report:** In naval-war correspondence, a passage claimed a line of **“torpedoes”** had been prepared for placement in the narrow channel below **Craney Island**, intended to threaten attacking ships. This was an early use of the term for underwater explosive defenses, rather than self-propelled torpedoes.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report of a claimed defensive plan**, not confirmation that the devices were actually deployed. The page's multi-column OCR is especially scrambled. **Follow-up open.**

### 1814

**HN-066 — August 27, 1814 — British occupiers demand an allegiance oath from Passamaquoddy island residents**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18140827.1.4).  
**Report:** The newspaper printed an order attributed to British occupying commanders in the Passamaquoddy Bay islands. Residents were called to declare whether they would swear allegiance to **George III**; those refusing were directed to depart within **seven days**, except by special permission.  
**Assessment:** **Published text of an occupation order**, with enforcement and individual residents' responses not yet examined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-067 — February 19, 1814 — Naval officers endorse a proposed armored steam warship**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18140219.1.2).  
**Report:** A statement concerning **Robert Fulton's** steam-propelled warship design argued that a heavy vessel with protected machinery and numerous guns could maneuver without sails and potentially defeat or deter British ships. The paper printed endorsements attributed to prominent naval officers including **Stephen Decatur** and **Oliver Hazard Perry**.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report of a technological proposal and its advocates**, not a field test or verified measure of performance. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-068 — February 19, 1814 — Sensational Rhode Island child-death allegations reach an Indiana paper**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18140219.1.2).  
**Report:** A letter attributed to a correspondent in **South Kingstown, Rhode Island**, described an inquest into a small child's death following alleged extreme abuse by an adult named **Brown**. It said an inquest returned a murder finding and the accused was jailed pending an April trial.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand and distressing accusation with a reported preliminary legal step**, not a final trial verdict. Names and outcome remain unchecked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-069 — August 27, 1814 — British ship captain reportedly attributes avoiding U.S. warship to crew mutiny**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18140827.1.4).  
**Report:** A report from Bermuda attributed to Captain **Lloyd of HMS *Plantagenet*** said that he avoided Commodore Rodgers's American warship because his crew had been mutinous for several days, with many men confined in irons. The item suggested a court inquiry and possible executions.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand explanation assigned to a named captain**; Royal Navy logs and inquiry records have not been reviewed. **Follow-up open.**

### 1815

**HN-070 — December 16, 1815 — Officials exchange letters over a killing near Detroit and Grosse Isle**  
**Source:** *Western Eagle*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WEE18151216.1.2).  
**Report:** The newspaper reproduced documents about the killing of an Indigenous man near **Grosse Isle** in the Detroit River area, including correspondence involving Michigan territorial governor **Lewis Cass** and British authorities across the border. The material describes a **$500 reward** and disagreements related to responsibility and jurisdiction.  
**Assessment:** **Published purported official correspondence**, with severe multi-column OCR mixing and no investigation file examined. It establishes that this dispute appeared in print, not who committed the killing. **Follow-up open.**

### 1816

**HN-071 — February 17, 1816 — Family killings and a reported inquest in New Fairfield**  
**Source:** *Western Sun* (Vincennes), p. 2, reprinting a letter from Danbury, Connecticut — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18160217.1.2).  
**Report:** The letter alleged that **Abel H. Platt** killed his wife and two children in New Fairfield, injured his mother when she tried to intervene, and then died by suicide. It described earlier erratic behavior and reported that an inquest returned a verdict of insanity.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed but reprinted eyewitness-style letter and reported inquest**, not a medical finding established by this research. Original inquest papers and corrections have not been inspected. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-072 — February 17, 1816 — Eight reported deaths from a Mecklenburg County house fire**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 3, reprinting Richmond-area news — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18160217.1.3).  
**Report:** A report from Virginia said **six people**, including **four servant women**, died in a fire at **Henry H. Burwell's** home; the couple reportedly died from their injuries over the following two days. The same column also reported a separate Pennsylvania house fire that killed two children.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary disaster accounts**. The totals and identities are those stated in the article, with neither coroner's records nor subsequent corrections examined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-073 — May 18, 1816 — A supposed society woman swaps a genuine banknote for a counterfeit**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 4, crediting a New York paper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18160518.1.4).  
**Report:** A short anecdote claimed that a fashionably dressed woman shopping for silver plate left a purse containing a genuine **$1,000 note** as apparent security, then substituted a counterfeit note before leaving with the goods and change. The story says she disappeared.  
**Assessment:** **Unattributed reprinted anecdote**, potentially embellished or fictitious; no merchant, police report, or victim identified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-074 — June 15, 1816 — Newfoundland fire destroys a large section of St. John's; paper alleges looting**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2, reproducing a St. John's report through the *Boston Palladium* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18160615.1.2).  
**Report:** The account said a February fire destroyed **about 130 houses** and displaced **roughly 1,500 people** during winter. Its author also accused some onlookers of stealing rescued property and failing to help; this criticism was expressed in conspicuously class-biased language.  
**Assessment:** **Published disaster estimates plus the correspondent's unverified allegation of pillage**. Neither is a systematic count or finding against specific persons. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-075 — November 2, 1816 — Report of three feet of June snow and a vessel trapped in ice**  
**Source:** *Western Sun*, p. 2, citing the *Quebec Telegraph* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WS18161102.1.2).  
**Report:** Under an “Extraordinary” notice, the paper relayed a claim of **three feet of snow** at Eskimaux Bay on June 27 and a ship so frozen in that people could walk ashore. It also mentioned ice-covered lakes near Quebec in mid-July.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand observations with unverified measurements and location spelling**. No weather log or vessel record has been checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1817

**HN-076 — October 11, 1817 — Admiralty court orders twenty seized American fishing vessels returned**  
**Source:** *Indiana Republican* (Madison), p. 3, quoting a Halifax dispatch dated August 29 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IRE18171011.1.3).  
**Report:** The paper said an Admiralty Court ordered **twenty American fishing vessels** captured in June by **HMS *Dee*** restored to their claimants, subject to costs. It also reported that their captors might appeal, with vessel owners providing bonds.  
**Assessment:** **A contemporary report of an international maritime court decision**; no original decree, appeal, or subsequent diplomatic resolution has been examined. **Follow-up open.**

### 1818

**HN-077 — August 22, 1818 — President reportedly orders military officer arrested over destruction of a Creek town**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 3, reprinting Savannah news — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18180822.1.3).  
**Report:** The newspaper reported a presidential order to arrest **Captain Obed Wright**, with a special court planned, after the destruction of the **Chehaw** town. It stated Wright was accused of murder and that Creek representatives sought reparations for the destruction.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report of an official action and accusation**, not evidence of conviction; the order, court record, and eventual disposition were not checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-078 — August 29, 1818 — Major reportedly shot on parade by a soldier seeking discharge**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 5 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18180829.1.5).  
**Report:** The paper said **Major Benjamin Birdsall** of the U.S. Army was shot in Albany, New York, by a soldier named **Hamilton**, who allegedly cited Birdsall's refusal to discharge him in exchange for a substitute. It described the soldier as awaiting sentence.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted allegation and preliminary legal status**. The soldier's identity, motive, proceedings, and outcome need independent records. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-079 — November 7, 1818 — Correspondent alleges Spain's Inquisition arrests suspected Freemasons and seizes property**  
**Source:** *Indiana Republican*, p. 2, carrying foreign correspondence — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IRE18181107.1.2).  
**Report:** A correspondent named several people reportedly imprisoned at Valladolid or Seville and claimed that those identifying as, or accused of being, Freemasons were imprisoned by the Inquisition without ordinary proceedings while their property was seized.  
**Assessment:** **An attributed contemporary accusation about state-religious repression**, not a verified legal inventory or count of arrests. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-080 — November 21, 1818 — Foreign papers describe an alleged sect-related killing in Saxony**  
**Source:** *Indiana Republican*, p. 1, reproducing a London dispatch citing journals from Bamberg — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IRE18181121.1.1).  
**Report:** The item alleged that a miller and his wife killed a visitor named **Flor** amid the influence of a religious movement associated with a traveling leader called **Kloss**. It reported their detention, broader official interventions, and Kloss's subsequent confinement in Meissen.  
**Assessment:** **Highly sensational, multilayered reprint with allegations against named people**. No Saxon court file or independent reporting has been examined; the claims must not be treated as adjudicated facts. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-081 — December 5, 1818 — Rumored plot to abduct the French king, with accusations of political fabrication**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 2, foreign political letter — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18181205.1.2).  
**Report:** A correspondent discussed an alleged plan to seize the king while hunting, move him to Fontainebleau, and press for abdication or a change in government. The same letter reported competing rumors that royalists—or even the king's own circle—had manufactured the conspiracy as a pretext to retain occupying foreign troops in France.  
**Assessment:** **Competing contemporary accusations, not proof that either plot or fabrication occurred**. The writer emphasized limited access and uncertainty. **Follow-up open.**

### 1819

**HN-082 — June 19, 1819 — Fundraising to prevent the sale of African captives, amid alleged efforts to reclaim them**  
**Source:** *Indiana Republican*, p. 1, printing a letter circulated by the American Colonization Society — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IRE18190619.1.1).  
**Report:** An agent at **Milledgeville, Georgia**, wrote that the governor had postponed the sale of a group of African captives and that he was raising funds to obtain their freedom and facilitate their return to Africa. He alleged that people connected to their capture or importation had tried to regain control of them.  
**Assessment:** **Directly published advocacy correspondence**, including claims about competing interests; neither final liberation nor the identities and conduct of purported captors have been independently established here. **Follow-up open.**

### 1820

**HN-083 — January 8, 1820 — Terre Haute warning alleges forged banknote passed in a private sale**  
**Source:** *Indiana Centinel*, p. 4, publishing a notice dated December 20, 1819 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IC18200108.1.4).  
**Report:** **Gibson Harris** publicly disputed a promissory note after a **$100 purported note on the Patriotic Bank of Washington** taken in payment allegedly proved counterfeit. The notice named a man using the name **Joseph Kingly** and warned of other convincing notes.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporaneous complainant's warning**, not a finding against the named person. The transaction occurred in 1819 but reached this newspaper in 1820. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-084 — July 27, 1820 — Buenos Aires privateer's crew reportedly kills officer and sets others adrift**  
**Source:** *Indiana Republican*, p. 1, relaying a New York maritime report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IRE18200727.1.1).  
**Report:** The account said sailors aboard a Buenos Aires private armed brig rebelled while near **Grenada**, killed a lieutenant attempting to intervene, wounded the captain, seized the vessel, and put surviving officers in a small boat with provisions. Later news said the officers reached **Margarita** after days at sea.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand maritime incident attributed partly to the captain**, with vessel name partly unreadable in OCR; ship logs or court proceedings have not been located. **Follow-up open.**

### 1821

**HN-085 — February 3 and June 2, 1821 — Bank of Vincennes shareholders demand answers, then complain of continuing uncertainty**  
**Sources:** *Indiana Centinel*, p. 3 — [February notice](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IC18210203.1.3) and [June commentary](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IC18210602.1.3).  
**Report:** A signed February notice summoned shareholders to investigate bank assets, circulation, loans, director authorizations, the suspension of payments in specie, and the possibility of surrendering the charter. A June discussion complained that results of the investigation had not been fully communicated to shareholders and feared damage from continued uncertainty or piecemeal debt collection.  
**Assessment:** **A documented public financial dispute with at least one subsequent newspaper discussion**; irregularities were questioned, **not established as criminal fraud**. Final resolutions and bank records remain unreviewed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-086 — February 3, 1821 — Man found shot indoors; homeowner reportedly held for trial**  
**Source:** *Indiana Centinel*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IC18210203.1.3).  
**Report:** A local notice said **Samuel Woollum** was shot while lying near a hearth in a house in **Johnson Township, Knox County**. An inquest reportedly found wilful murder by an unknown person, while **Moses Catty** (spelling uncertain) was said to have been arrested and jailed for trial.  
**Assessment:** **Preliminary local criminal report**. An arrest is not proof of responsibility and no trial outcome was checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-087 — February 24, 1821 — Havana military mutiny described as triggering citywide panic**  
**Source:** *Indiana Centinel*, p. 2, reprinting a December 1820 Havana letter — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IC18210224.1.2).  
**Report:** A correspondent alleged that soldiers in two Spanish regiments refused duties after their service terms expired, one bayoneted a captain, and terrified residents barricaded their premises. The letter claimed the governor promised to spare the soldier and accused supporters of Cuban independence of engineering the unrest.  
**Assessment:** **A single partisan correspondent's description and political theory**, not proof of a coordinated plot. Event date precedes newspaper date. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-088 — March 31, 1821 — Note purports to come from a bank that paper says does not exist**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18210331.1.3).  
**Report:** An anti-counterfeiting notice reproduced a purported **$30 Bank of Harmony** note signed with names including **Frederick Rapp**. The article declared that there was **no bank or banking association at Harmony** and called the instrument fraudulent.  
**Assessment:** **Documented contemporary public warning about a purportedly fictitious issuer**; no suspect, issuer records, or legal disposition were verified. **Follow-up open.**

### 1822

**HN-089 — September 18, 1822 — Bank teller reportedly uses a courier to catch a suspected forger**  
**Source:** *Western Clarion*, p. 3, reprinting Philadelphia news — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WC18220918.1.3).  
**Report:** A man allegedly sent a boy to the **Philadelphia Bank** with a forged check. The teller recognized it, handed the boy a substitute packet, and had a porter follow him; the person who collected the packet was reportedly arrested and committed for trial.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted account of an arrest, not a conviction**. Precise check amount is unreadable in the OCR. **Follow-up open.**

### 1823

**HN-090 — July 3, 1823 — Murder conviction followed by denial of a new trial in Indiana**  
**Source:** *Farmers & Mechanics Journal* (Vincennes), p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=FMJ18230703.1.1).  
**Report:** The paper printed a court account of **John Harvey** being found guilty of shooting **Thomas Casey** and said defense motions for a new trial and arrest of judgment were rejected. It reproduced a lengthy address attributed to the judge pronouncing sentence.  
**Assessment:** **Published trial reporting with an attributed judicial address**; court docket and any appeal or executive intervention have not been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-091 — July 3, 1823 — Warning that an Indiana constitutional convention could reopen the question of slavery**  
**Source:** *Farmers & Mechanics Journal*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=FMJ18230703.1.1).  
**Report:** A polemical election essay argued against calling a state constitutional convention, warning that a new convention would have broad authority and might change Indiana's existing restriction on slavery. It urged voters to consider candidates' positions before supporting a convention.  
**Assessment:** **Documented contemporary political advocacy and concern**, not evidence that a specific covert plan or a constitutional change succeeded. **Follow-up open.**

### 1824

**HN-092 — February 11, 1824 — Killing near Sugar Creek amid another reported suspect's escape**  
**Source:** *Western Register and Terre-Haute Advertiser*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRTA18240211.1.2).  
**Report:** The newspaper said an Indigenous man was killed in his camp near the headwaters of **Sugar Creek**. It referred to the rescue or escape of a person arrested in connection with another killing and urged that the perpetrators be brought before the law.  
**Assessment:** **A local report with severely interleaved and damaged OCR**. Whether the two crimes were connected, and who was responsible, cannot be inferred. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-093 — May 29, 1824 — Rumor that Portuguese minister was murdered inside a palace**  
**Source:** *Public Leger* (Richmond, Indiana), p. 1, quoting a Liverpool letter relaying news from Lisbon — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PL18240529.1.1).  
**Report:** A short report claimed the body of a royal minister had been found murdered inside a palace in Lisbon and that suspicions had reached someone near the top of the kingdom. It also rumored that the **Conde de Palmella** had fled for safety.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified multilayered rumor** with uncertain OCR spelling of the alleged victim's name. No Portuguese archival record examined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-094 — November 27, 1824 — Huge forgery allegation against an English bank partner**  
**Source:** *Public Leger*, p. 3, crediting a Leeds paper through the *National Journal* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PL18241127.1.3).  
**Report:** The paper alleged banker **Henry Fauntleroy** faced accusations of forging instruments totaling around **£200,000**, which the reprint described as about **$1 million**. It reported confinement under constant watch and said he denied involvement by his banking partners.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary accusation and description of detention**, not the final court outcome. The newspaper did not supply a full account of the case. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-095 — November 27, 1824 — Kentucky State House fire destroys legislative and legal records**  
**Source:** *Public Leger*, p. 3, reprinting a Frankfort, Kentucky report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PL18241127.1.3).  
**Report:** The report described fire spreading from the cupola of Kentucky's State House while the legislature was sitting and said the building was ruined. It estimated **more than 3,000 volumes**, including printed laws and court reports held by the state, were lost; neighboring offices were threatened.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary damage estimates**, with no independent fire inquiry or restoration inventory checked. **There is no evidence here of deliberate burning. Follow-up open.**

### 1825

**HN-096 — May 6, 1825 — Maryland murder account describes a suspect arrested after a child witness's report**  
**Source:** *Indiana Spectator*, p. 3, reprinting Baltimore-area reports — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISP18250506.1.3).  
**Report:** The paper described the killing of **Evelina Cunningham** near **Charlestown, Cecil County, Maryland**, based partly on a young child's account, and later added that a man suspected in the case had been detained between Baltimore and Washington.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand and sensational account with reported suspect detention**, not a finding of guilt; the subsequent proceedings were not checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-097 — May 14, 1825 — Report claims hundreds of sepoys killed during a refusal to march**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 3, reprinting British accounts — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18250514.1.3).  
**Report:** A report from the **Barrackpore** garrison in India said sepoys refused to march under orders associated with the British war against Burma and that **Sir Edward Paget** ordered troops to fire. It gave figures of **450 dead and 150 wounded**, while the item itself acknowledged doubts about the number killed.  
**Assessment:** **Conflicting contemporary reports of a colonial military crackdown**, with casualty figures explicitly disputed in print. No muster rolls or original official dispatches checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-098 — May 14, 1825 — Earthquake said to devastate an Ionian island city**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18250514.1.3).  
**Report:** A foreign-news summary said the city of **Santa Maura** in the Ionian Islands had been devastated by an earthquake, displacing thousands and causing **about fifty deaths** by the report's estimate.  
**Assessment:** **Brief secondhand disaster estimate**; precise death toll, place identification, and reconstruction accounts remain unexamined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-099 — June 4, 1825 — Jury verdicts and death sentences in the Fall Creek killings**  
**Source:** *Indiana Intelligencer*, p. 1, reprinting an account of proceedings in Madison — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=II18250604.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper described murder trials concerning the killing of Indigenous people at **Fall Creek**. It reported guilty verdicts against **Andrew Sawyer**, **John T. Bridge**, and his son **John Bridge**, and said the jury recommended clemency for the younger Bridge. A June 3 execution date was reported as scheduled.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed contemporary trial account**, including attributed testimony and judicial rulings. A schedule is not verification that an execution took place; official docket, clemency, and execution records are still to be checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1826

**HN-100 — January 28, 1826 — War Department suspends an officer amid complaints about clearing western waterways**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser* (Vincennes), p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18260128.1.2).  
**Report:** The paper printed a **December 13, 1825** letter attributed to Secretary of War James Barbour, saying that complaints about a contractor named Bruce's work removing obstructions from western rivers had been supported by an officer's report. It said **Major Babcock was suspended** from supervising the work pending further documents, and another officer substituted.  
**Assessment:** **Published text of an attributed official letter**, concerning contract performance and administrative suspension; this does **not** establish criminal corruption. Letter authenticity and subsequent findings have not been independently checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1827

**HN-101 — July 28, 1827 — Newspaper republishes military proceedings sentencing soldiers over an 1814 mutiny**  
**Source:** *Western Register and Terre-Haute Advertiser*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRTA18270728.1.1).  
**Report:** The issue carried an extended account of military court-martial charges against militia members for desertion, mutiny, and related offenses from **1814**. The reproduced proceedings name defendants, describe disputed orders, and record death sentences by shooting for some convicted men.  
**Assessment:** **Retrospective military proceedings published in 1827**, not a fresh 1827 mutiny. The newspaper's text supplies sentences but does not verify executions, commutations, or completeness of the record. **Follow-up open.**

### 1829

**HN-102 — June 20, 1829 — Federal fraud and forgery indictments quashed for defects in their wording**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3, reprinting the *United States Telegraph* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18290620.1.3).  
**Report:** A report of **United States v. Tobias Watkins** said Judge Cranch and the court sustained a demurrer to indictments because they insufficiently described alleged false pretenses and improperly combined forgery with another charge. It said Judge Thurston disagreed in part, and another indictment was still pending.  
**Assessment:** **Specific account of a procedural court ruling**; dismissing these indictments did not by itself resolve all charges or establish guilt. Court papers and later proceedings unreviewed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-103 — October 22, 1829 — Louisville branch bank reportedly robbed of about $25,085**  
**Source:** *Western Register and Terre-Haute Advertiser*, p. 3, summarizing affidavits supplied to the bank's president — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRTA18291022.1.3).  
**Report:** A report on the **Bank of the Commonwealth's Louisville branch** said a passerby found the front door open; inside, the money chest stood open with a key in its lock. The cashier estimated **$25,085** missing and the article suggested the vault key had been carelessly left accessible. One suspect was reportedly committed and a **$500 reward** offered.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary detailed robbery account**; the negligence suggestion is the reporter's inference, and detention does not demonstrate who was responsible. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-104 — October 22, 1829 — A stolen Egyptian mummy leads to a civil jury verdict**  
**Source:** *Western Register and Terre-Haute Advertiser*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRTA18291022.1.3).  
**Report:** The same issue said a civil suit was brought against eight people after an **Egyptian mummy** had allegedly been carried off from its exhibitor by a mob at a public house in **Rensselaerville, New York**. A jury reportedly awarded damages to the exhibitor.  
**Assessment:** **Brief reprinted legal curiosity**; the dollar figure is poorly rendered in OCR and is not relied on here. Docket and disposition beyond the reported verdict unexamined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-105 — October 22, 1829 — Five men punished for vagrancy despite insufficient proof of suspected steamboat thefts**  
**Source:** *Western Register and Terre-Haute Advertiser*, p. 3, crediting a New York paper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRTA18291022.1.3).  
**Report:** A notice said five young men were arrested on suspicion of recent **steamboat robberies**, but that positive proof of the robberies was lacking. It nevertheless reported six-month **Bridewell** sentences under a vagrancy provision.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper account of an apparently different legal basis for confinement**, not proof that the five committed the robberies. No underlying judgment or appeal reviewed. **Follow-up open.**

### 1830

**HN-106 — May 29, 1830 — Miami defendant's murder trial raises treaty and legal-jurisdiction questions**
