**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 2, printing an account attributed to Indian agent Col. Pepper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18300529.1.2).  
**Report:** The paper detailed the Fort Wayne trial of **Nowelinggua** (spelling uncertain), a Miami man charged with killing a woman named **Wishmah**. Counsel argued about Miami custom, a treaty's reservation of hunting rights, and whether Indiana criminal law applied; the judge rejected that argument. The jury convicted him of **manslaughter**, reportedly imposing two years' imprisonment and a one-cent fine, while recommending clemency.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed but culturally biased contemporary courtroom report**, including offensive characterizations of Indigenous people and disputed descriptions of Wishmah's status. The outcome of the clemency request has not been established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-107 — October 23, 1830 — Fatal steamboat boiler rupture reported with specific design concerns**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 1, reprinting the *New York Courier* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18301023.1.1).  
**Report:** The steamboat ***United States*** suffered a boiler-flue rupture near New York. The article listed several reported deaths and injuries, described a section repaired only a week earlier, and compared similar failures aboard other low-pressure boats, noting an absence of lower boiler braces.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary engineering observations and casualty reporting**, not a completed accident investigation. The page contains OCR inconsistencies in the death count and names; these need visual confirmation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-108 — October 23, 1830 — Baltimore girl allegedly awakens after being placed in a coffin**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18301023.1.1).  
**Report:** A short item claimed a family returned from one child's funeral to find another daughter apparently dead. It alleged she had been placed in a coffin, then suddenly moved, rose, and called for her mother while relatives kept watch.  
**Assessment:** **Unattributed, secondhand extraordinary claim**, printed without names, medical records, or a verifiable witness. It is **not established as fact**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-109 — November 3, 1830 — Brig crew's story of a captain's death challenged by other sailors' allegations**  
**Source:** *Corydon Press and Anti-Masonic Democrat*, p. 5, summarizing Boston papers and the *New York Post* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CPAND18301103.1.5).  
**Report:** Sailors from the distressed New York brig ***Orbit*** initially said Captain **Samuel Woodbury** had died of fever on a voyage from the West African coast. A crew member who reached the rescuing brig ***Mentos*** reportedly alleged instead that Woodbury had been murdered by an acting master, a story the paper said other crew members corroborated.  
**Assessment:** **Competing maritime accounts and reported sailor statements**, with transcribed names and the precise vessel spelling needing visual checks. Not an adjudication of murder. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-110 — November 3, 1830 — Anti-Masonic paper publicizes another arrest in the William Morgan affair**  
**Source:** *Corydon Press and Anti-Masonic Democrat*, p. 5, crediting the *Greensburg Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CPAND18301103.1.5).  
**Report:** An openly anti-Masonic column said **James Gills** (OCR spelling uncertain) had been arrested in Pennsylvania and taken toward New York for trial in connection with the kidnapping of **William Morgan**. It also speculated broadly about Masonic involvement and other possible participants.  
**Assessment:** **Partisan newspaper report of an arrest mixed with unproven accusations.** The broader claims about who knew or assisted are not evidence of guilt; court records need checking. **Follow-up open.**

### 1831

**HN-111 — May 21, 1831 — A pirate's published confession recounts multiple mutinies**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 4, reprinting the *New-York Journal of Commerce* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18310521.1.4).  
**Report:** A long narrative titled as the confession of **Gibbs the pirate** recounted service at sea, a disputed division of privateering prizes, the crew's seizure of the schooner ***Maria***, and later killings and internal violence within a pirate crew.  
**Assessment:** **Attributed first-person criminal narrative** reprinted in 1831; episodes described occurred earlier and cannot be treated as independently verified by publication alone. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-112 — August 20, 1831 — A missing possibly counterfeit $100 banknote traced to a young stage driver**  
**Source:** *Wabash Herald*, p. 4, reprinting the *Indiana Democrat* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WH18310820.1.4).  
**Report:** The Indiana state treasurer reportedly mailed a **$100 note believed possibly counterfeit**, but it never reached its recipient. A later advertisement led investigators to a stage driver between **Shelbyville and Greensburg** who had passed the note; he was sent before a federal judge on a mail-robbery accusation. The report mentioned contradictory statements and suspicion of accomplices.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary investigation and arrest report**, with no trial outcome; its speculation about accomplices should not be adopted as fact. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-113 — November 5, 1831 — Wife-murder trial tests an insanity defense in New York**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 1, reprinting the *New-York Journal of Commerce* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18311105.1.1).  
**Report:** A lengthy court report said **James Ransom** stood trial for killing his wife, **Sophia Ransom**. The defense argued that insanity left him unable to distinguish right from wrong, prompting extensive testimony and judicial discussion. The newspaper eventually reported a **guilty verdict**.  
**Assessment:** **Published trial testimony and verdict**, not a complete sentencing, appeals, or execution record. **Follow-up open.**

### 1832

**HN-114 — August 18, 1832 — Steamboat *Phoenix* explodes after catching fire on the Mississippi**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3, reprinting a New Orleans report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18320818.1.3).  
**Report:** The account, attributed in part to the ***Phoenix*'s** clerk, said the vessel ran ashore after a fire was spotted; crew efforts appeared to contain it before an explosion in the hold blew away the deck and machinery. One passenger was missing, and others were reported injured. The clerk claimed he had not known of any powder in the cargo.  
**Assessment:** **A contemporary witness-attributed disaster narrative**. The possibility of gunpowder is an inference by the witness, **not evidence of sabotage**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-115 — August 18, 1832 — Philadelphia political meeting accused of using forged signatures**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3, reprinting the *Pennsylvanian* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18320818.1.3).  
**Report:** An editorial attacking a planned political meeting claimed **more than sixty Irish signatories** had denied participating or agreeing to the meeting call, calling the signatures fraudulent and accusing unnamed organizers of forging support.  
**Assessment:** **Overtly partisan allegation**; the denials and accusation are documented in print, but original petitions, signers' statements, and any prosecution remain unchecked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1833

**HN-116 — August 17, 1833 — A former official answers forgery allegations and says he lost office**  
**Source:** *Western Sun & General Advertiser*, p. 2, an extended first-person public defense — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WSGA18330817.1.2).  
**Report:** A long signed statement concerning **John D. Woolverton**, **D. C. Johnson**, and disputed **Cottrell papers** said the speaker had been removed from office amid suspicion of writing forged materials. He denied involvement, contested handwriting identifications, and publicly accused others of manufacturing a case against him.  
**Assessment:** **One side of a public political and personal dispute**, not an impartial court finding. The author and opponents' claims need independent records and the opposite side's full reply. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-117 — December 7, 1833 — Alleged deathbed confession contests an earlier execution for murder**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 4, crediting a London paper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18331207.1.4); a similar item appeared in the *Rising Sun Times* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RST18331207.1.2).  
**Report:** An item said a dying man near **Ellesmere, England**, admitted participating in the murders of **Francis Bruce and Ann Taylor** roughly twenty years earlier. A blacksmith named **Preston** had reportedly been convicted and executed. The new claim was that Preston was involved in the robbery but did **not** personally kill the victims; two other men allegedly committed the murders.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand report of a purported confession;** the underlying case and identity of the dying speaker have not been verified. The report does **not** establish legal innocence of every offense or conclusively prove a wrongful conviction. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-118 — December 7, 1833 — Crew members allegedly plan to blow up steamboat *Freedom***  
**Source:** *Rising Sun Times*, p. 3, reprinting the *Cincinnati Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RST18331207.1.3).  
**Report:** A report said to come from Captain **Philip Richardson** and passengers described violence aboard the ***Freedom*** on a journey from New Orleans to Louisville: a deckhand named **James Algeo** was fatally stabbed in a fight, a passenger named **Barns** was subsequently shot, and six men were detained when a supposed plan to use a slow match on **two kegs of powder** in the hold was uncovered. The men were reportedly handed to authorities at **Mills Point**.  
**Assessment:** **Named-source newspaper allegation involving multiple crimes and a possible explosive plot**. No court record or independent determination of the plotters' intentions examined. **Follow-up open.**

### 1834

**HN-119 — May 17, 1834 — Doctor reportedly preserves a worm longer than thirty feet**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18340517.1.3).  
**Report:** A correspondent said **Dr. Stewart** in **Roxbury** showed him an intestinal worm removed from a young man, measured at **more than thirty feet**, and preserved in spirits for visitors to see.  
**Assessment:** **Published claimed direct observation** rather than a documented medical specimen examination. The length, species, and patient's history have not been independently checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-120 — May 17, 1834 — Brief report prematurely predicts conviction in the Kaspar Hauser case**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18340517.1.3).  
**Report:** A single-line foreign-news item asserted that the supposed assassin of **Caspar/Kaspar Hauser** had been arrested and would probably be convicted and confess. It named no suspect and offered no supporting details.  
**Assessment:** **Unsubstantiated and apparently premature news claim**, not evidence that a perpetrator was identified. Subsequent scholarship treats the circumstances of Hauser's fatal wound as unresolved; see the [medicolegal review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16134400/). **Do not record the alleged arrest as a verified event.**

**HN-121 — May 17, 1834 — Maryland postmaster and his son reportedly arrested for mail theft**  
**Source:** *Indiana Palladium*, p. 3, crediting the *Baltimore Patriot* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IP18340517.1.3).  
**Report:** A short notice said the postmaster and his son at **Middletown, Frederick County, Maryland**, had been arrested on a charge of robbing the mails.  
**Assessment:** **Bare arrest report without names, detailed allegations, or judgment**. **Follow-up open.**

### 1835

**HN-122 — February 20, 1835 — Congress seeks records about sales and transfers of Indigenous reserved lands**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 1, reprinting a February 2 House of Representatives debate — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18350220.1.1).  
**Report:** The paper printed a proposed request for federal executive correspondence concerning the **survey, location, sale, and transfer of Indigenous land reserves east of the Mississippi since 1825**. The ensuing argument included concerns about possible fraud and objections to casting unsupported aspersions on entire communities.  
**Assessment:** **Documented public congressional inquiry and contentious debate**, **not a finding of specific fraud**. Whether the records were produced and what they showed are not yet traced. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-123 — June 19, 1835 — Correspondent claims government clerks face monthly scrutiny of morals and debts**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3, reprinting a Washington letter from the *Journal of Commerce* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18350619.1.3).  
**Report:** A Washington correspondent claimed President **Andrew Jackson** had ordered monthly reports on federal clerks' conduct and personal debts and threatened removal for failure to pay. The writer said an unnamed observer had seen the circular and relayed rumors of resignations and removals.  
**Assessment:** **Journalistic account explicitly relying on indirect knowledge and rumors**; original circular and personnel actions not checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-124 — June 19, 1835 — Chile earthquake and sea surge devastate Concepción and Talcahuano**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3, relaying news from the ship ***Coral*** and a purported eyewitness — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18350619.1.3).  
**Report:** The article described the **February 1835** earthquake at **Concepción and Talcahuano, Chile**, followed by the sea withdrawing and returning as a destructive surge. A witness estimated water reached **25 feet above its usual level**, and said buildings, cargo, and entire neighborhoods were swept away.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary ship-carried and eyewitness-attributed disaster report.** The headline/body contain an OCR date inconsistency and the height is a claimed estimate; precise losses and times require other primary sources. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-125 — June 19, 1835 — Cincinnati's supposedly haunted doorbell defeats multiple explanations**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3, reprinting the *Cincinnati Whig* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18350619.1.3).  
**Report:** A householder allegedly experienced repeated doorbell ringing when no visitor was present. The bell-hanger and other observers reportedly tried adjusting and cutting the wire, checking for rats, and consulting people who suggested **electricity, weather phenomena, or supernatural causes**. The owner ultimately removed the bell.  
**Assessment:** **Entertaining, attributed extraordinary anecdote with competing contemporary theories**, but not proof of a haunting or of any explanation. **Follow-up open.**

### 1836

**HN-126 — April 9, 1836 — Steamboat *Benjamin Franklin* boiler explosion at Mobile**  
**Source:** *Weekly Messenger*, p. 3, reprinting the *Mobile Journal* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WM18360409.1.3).  
**Report:** The steamboat reportedly burst a boiler while leaving the wharf for Montgomery, throwing part of the vessel and passengers into the air. The report lists several people killed, missing, or badly wounded, and says witnesses feared **forty to fifty** fatalities, while acknowledging that its list was incomplete. It speculated that a delay and low boiler water contributed.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary disaster report with uncertain casualty estimate and proposed cause**. Neither the final death toll nor a technical investigation was checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-127 — May 14, 1836 — Wheeling murder suspect's alleged confession implicates a second man**  
**Source:** *Weekly Messenger*, p. 2, reprinting the *Wheeling Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WM18360514.1.2).  
**Report:** **Jesse Chrisman**, a traveling seller of agricultural equipment with a family in Illinois, was found dead in Wheeling Creek. The newspaper described four arrests and printed what it called the substance of **Bojn/John Long's** confession, accusing **Thomas Wintringer** of striking Chrisman with a brick, stealing his money and watch, and disposing of the body. The coroner's inquest reportedly attributed the killing to both men.  
**Assessment:** **Published accusation and purported confession**, not an independently examined confession or judicial conviction. The first suspect's given name is uncertain in OCR. **Follow-up open.**

### 1837

**HN-128 — January 21, 1837 — Witness recants in a Cincinnati murder case before execution**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Weekly)*, p. 1, reprinting the *Cincinnati Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPW18370121.1.1).  
**Report:** A report described the execution of **John Washburn** for the robbery and murder of shopkeeper **William Beaver**. It said a fellow suspect, **Hoover**, had testified against Washburn, later recanted, and triggered a reprieve; subsequent statements allegedly implicated **David Davis** and members of Hoover's family. The article itself observed that key aspects of the trial evidence had become disputed.  
**Assessment:** **Published retrospective account of a trial, reprieve, shifting testimony, and execution**. It does not by itself establish whether any recantation was true or whether the conviction was wrongful. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-129 — October 13, 1837 — Arsenic reportedly found after young visitor's death at Michigan City**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 2, reprinting the *Toledo Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18371013.1.2).  
**Report:** The story said **Elizabeth Swartwout**, who had traveled with the Jenkins family from Chicago, fell ill and died during a stay at the Mansion House in **Michigan City, Indiana**. Suspicious relatives allegedly secured an exhumation; physicians reportedly found **arsenic** in her stomach, and a coroner's jury returned a verdict of willful murder. The report connected the inquiry to Mr. and Mrs. Jenkins but left the allegations and subsequent proceedings unresolved.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand report of an inquest and forensic allegation**; an inquest verdict is not a criminal conviction. **Follow-up open.**

### 1838

**HN-130 — February 16, 1838 — Counterfeit notes allegedly printed for a nonexistent Wisconsin bank**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 1, reprinting the *New-York Journal of Commerce* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18380216.1.1).  
**Report:** The paper warned that a person using the name **James Brown** supposedly arranged for about **$200,000** in notes, denominated from five to one hundred dollars, for the purported **Farmers' and Mechanics' Bank of Burlington, Wisconsin Territory**. The New York source asserted that no such bank was known to exist and that forged notes were already in circulation.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper and police-attributed counterfeiting allegation**, not a verified criminal disposition or established total of notes passed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-131 — February 16, 1838 — Authentic Kentucky bank plates obtained using a forged order**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 1, reprinting *Bennett's Evening Herald* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18380216.1.1).  
**Report:** A second case on the same page described a letter purporting to come from Bank of Kentucky cashier **G. C. Gwathmey**, instructing New York engravers to print roughly **$370,000** in notes using the bank's actual plates. The engravers became suspicious of a purported collector, consulted the bank's New York agent, and learned the order was forged; the article reported arrests and recovery of the impressions.  
**Assessment:** **Specific contemporary account of an attempted fraud involving genuine printing plates and forged authorization**, with arrests reported but no trial disposition verified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-132 — April 6, 1838 — Pensioners reportedly seize officers at Philadelphia's Naval Asylum**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3, relaying a March 26 Philadelphia report attributed to a service officer — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18380406.1.3).  
**Report:** An officer was cited as saying pensioners at the **Naval Asylum on the Schuylkill** confined asylum officers, seriously injured the commander, and were subdued after naval-yard personnel were summoned. Alleged leaders were reportedly taken into custody aboard the *Sea Gull*.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand service-officer report of a disturbance and detentions**. Proceedings against the alleged participants were not traced. **Follow-up open.**

### 1839

**HN-133 — April 4, 1839 — Strange behavior and a murder near the St. Louis levee**  
**Source:** *Leavenworth Arena*, p. 2, reprinting the *St. Louis Republican* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKLA18390404.1.2).  
**Report:** A report described **George Gardner** being found mortally injured near a riverboat after a late-night attack and **Thomas Berry**, a recent arrival from the Grand River who had behaved unusually, attracting suspicion. It said Berry was committed and described his apparent mental disturbance, but the report's account of suspicion did not establish his guilt.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary arrest/suspicion narrative with speculative descriptions of mental health**; no criminal verdict was checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-134 — May 16, 1839 — Reports of convicted and accused *Braganza* pirates dying in jail**  
**Source:** *Leavenworth Arena*, p. 2, citing New York papers — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKLA18390516.1.2).  
**Report:** A roundup said **Van Bruggen**, described as convicted in connection with piracy and murder aboard the brig ***Braganza***, died by suicide while imprisoned, and that **James Davys**, reportedly acquitted of the captain's murder but still facing a piracy trial, also died by suicide in early May. It also claimed an earlier suspect had died in a European jail.  
**Assessment:** **Third-hand roundup of distinct legal outcomes and reported deaths**, not independently corroborated prison or admiralty records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1840

**HN-135 — November 26, 1840 — Former deputy postmaster fails to appear for mail-theft trial**  
**Source:** *Semi-Weekly Journal* (Indianapolis), p. 2, reprinting the *Richmond Palladium* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SWJ18401126.1.2).  
**Report:** The story said **Joshua B. Otey**, formerly a deputy postmaster, had been charged with taking money from letters, was indicted, and posted **$1,000 bail** for a federal-court appearance at Indianapolis. When his case was called, he reportedly did not appear, and the court ordered action against his sureties. The originating editor regarded the absence as proof of wrongdoing.  
**Assessment:** **Published report of indictment, bail, and nonappearance**; the newspaper's inference of guilt is **not** a judicial finding. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-136 — December 3, 1840 — Steamboat *Persian* explosion reportedly kills and scalds passengers**  
**Source:** *Vevay Times and Switzerland County Democrat*, p. 4, relaying the *Louisville Journal* via another paper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=VT18401203.1.4).  
**Report:** The paper said a boiler explosion aboard the ***Persian*** killed **five people immediately** and scalded **thirty-two**, of whom **eleven** had reportedly since died. It said other victims remained in critical condition and cautioned, in effect, that the early loss figures could change.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary casualty report, not a verified final toll**; vessel records and later reports remain to be checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1841

**HN-137 — August 4, 1841 — Bank teller reportedly admits robbing vault and destroying records**  
**Source:** *Semi-Weekly Journal* (Indianapolis), p. 1, reprinting St. Louis reports; separately reprinted by the *Vincennes Gazette*, August 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SWJ18410804.1.1); [second printing](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=VG18410807.1.2).  
**Report:** After a robbery at the **Jacksonville, Illinois, branch bank**, the reports said teller **H. D. Town** confessed to **Col. Mather** that he had taken funds over several years, destroyed bank books to conceal the losses, and hidden stolen cash and gold in three locations. Town reportedly asserted a deficit under **$4,000**, while a crowd pursued Mather after he recovered the money; Town was committed and sought reduced bail.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed report of a purported confession, recovery, and preliminary court proceedings**, carried in multiple newspapers; the second printing repeats rather than independently confirms the underlying St. Louis account. **Follow-up open.**

### 1842

**HN-138 — April 29, 1842 — New steamboat *Medora* explodes on a Chesapeake trial run**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 1, reprinting an earlier American newspaper account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18420429.1.1).  
**Report:** A lengthy disaster account described the new steamboat ***Medora***, built for service between Baltimore and Norfolk, suffering a catastrophic boiler explosion as it prepared to leave a Baltimore wharf on a trial trip. The newspaper estimated a crowd of perhaps **one hundred or more** aboard and described extensive fatalities and injuries.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary initial disaster narrative with estimated attendance and casualties**. Final victim count and technical cause need more sources. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-139 — December 30, 1842 — First sensational accounts of the U.S. brig *Somers* executions**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 1, reprinting the *New York Commercial Advertiser* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18421230.1.1).  
**Report:** On receiving news that three crewmen, including midshipman **Philip Spencer**, had been hanged aboard the U.S. brig ***Somers***, the newspaper reproduced claims of an elaborate plan to seize the vessel, murder officers, and commit piracy. The dispatch expressly admitted that some accounts conflicted about how rapidly the executions occurred and anticipated official records.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary, partly conflicting initial narrative**, printed before later inquiry. Its elaborate plot details cannot be accepted solely on this account; compare the 1843 follow-ups below. **Follow-up open.**

### 1843

**HN-140 — January 27, 1843 — A newspaper challenges the evidence for the *Somers* hangings**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 4, reprinting an opinion from the *Philadelphia Evening Journal* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18430127.1.4).  
**Report:** An extended editorial argued that testimony did **not** justify executing Spencer and two crewmen without ordinary trial proceedings, questioned interpretations of Spencer's conduct, and called for commander **Alexander Slidell Mackenzie** to face a criminal jury. The piece disputed the confident early newspaper account of a dangerous mutiny.  
**Assessment:** **Forceful contemporary editorial dissent**, **not** a court determination that the mutiny was fictional or that the commander committed murder. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-141 — March 11, 1843 — Naval court of inquiry justifies the *Somers* executions**  
**Source:** *Vincennes Gazette*, p. 1, printing the reported written opinion of the naval court of inquiry — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=VG18430311.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper published an opinion signed by **Charles Stewart** as court president and **Ogden Hoffman** as judge advocate. It concluded that a mutiny had been organized, that Spencer, **Samuel Cromwell**, and **Elisha Small** were involved, and that immediate execution was justified by necessity to protect the ship.  
**Assessment:** **Published institutional finding on the same disputed affair**, sharply contrasting with HN-140. This is a naval inquiry's position, not a substitute for complete testimony, subsequent court-martial proceedings, or modern legal analysis. **Follow-up open.**

### 1844

**HN-142 — October 11, 1844 — Newspaper admits publishing the forged “Roorback” election story**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18441011.1.3).  
**Report:** The openly partisan paper acknowledged that an item in its previous issue—presented as a passage from **Roorback's travels** and claiming presidential candidate **James K. Polk** had branded enslaved people—was a **forgery**. The editor then blamed a political opponent for the fabrication and continued to make other allegations about Polk.  
**Assessment:** **Documented contemporary admission that a specific printed election claim was forged**. The editor's proposed identity of the forger and its additional allegations were not independently established here. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-143 — December 6, 1844 — Rival political organizers disavow a forged Garland/Birney handbill**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 2, reproducing a statement attributed to a Michigan county Whig committee — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18441206.1.2).  
**Report:** A statement signed by **James Birdsall, Artemas Thayer, and Alexander P. Davis** labeled circulated material about the **Garland letter and James G. Birney** a forgery, denied knowing who made it, and listed competing theories about who might have benefited during the election.  
**Assessment:** **Published political disavowal and accusation of fabrication**; the statement itself says responsibility was unknown. **Follow-up open.**

### 1845

**HN-144 — July 30, 1845 — Great New York fire accompanied by huge blast and rival explanations**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Weekly)*, p. 3, collating New York eyewitness accounts and newspaper commentary — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPW18450730.1.3).  
**Report:** The account described a large **New York City fire**, reporting **546 buildings destroyed** and substantial financial losses. Witnesses said a tremendous explosion near stores containing **saltpeter** and other combustible materials knocked down surrounding buildings. Reports proposed different causes, including stored gunpowder or gas works; they did **not** agree on the explanation.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary catastrophe reporting with conflicting early causal hypotheses**, rather than evidence that a single explanation had been verified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-145 — August 16, 1845 — Brief alleges double murder and fire at an Ohio store**  
**Source:** *Locomotive* (Indianapolis), p. 3, “Items of News” — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LO18450816.1.3).  
**Report:** A brief said the **Peterbaugh & Allison** store in **Xenia, Ohio**, had been broken into, **two clerks killed**, and the premises set on fire on the night of August 2. It advertised a **$1,500 reward** for the perpetrators, but provided no suspect or investigation outcome.  
**Assessment:** **Single-paragraph newspaper crime claim and reported reward**, not independent proof of the sequence of crimes or identities. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-146 — August 16, 1845 — Murder conviction reported in four-day marriage poisoning case**  
**Source:** *Locomotive* (Indianapolis), p. 3, “Items of News” — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LO18450816.1.3).  
**Report:** A short report asserted that a man named **Green** had poisoned his new wife, formerly **Miss Wyatt**, four days after their marriage. It stated he had been **convicted of first-degree murder** and sentenced to be hanged in **Troy, New York**, on September 10.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper report of conviction and sentence**, not the court record or confirmation that the scheduled execution took place. Full names and proceedings still need checking. **Follow-up open.**

### 1846

**HN-147 — October 22, 1846 — Mutiny charge aboard the U.S. warship *St. Mary's***  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 1, relaying a New Orleans *Picayune* account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISSN18461022.1.1).  
**Report:** Two men aboard the American warship *St. Mary's* were reportedly tried on charges arising from mutinous conduct; one was accused of striking Lieutenant Taylor and sentenced by court-martial to be hanged. The reprinted report believed a reprieve would be granted.  
**Assessment:** **Secondhand military-proceeding report.** The sentence is not evidence of an execution, and the anticipated reprieve has not been independently confirmed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-148 — October 22, 1846 — Newspaper explicitly questions a Santa Fe troop-mutiny story**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 1, printing a letter attributed to the October 10 *St. Louis Union* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISSN18461022.1.1).  
**Report:** A letter claimed Mexican General **Manuel Armijo** had ordered regular soldiers to fire on men protesting efforts to disband them near Santa Fe. Crucially, the newspaper declined to endorse the story, saying it could not judge how much credence the statement deserved.  
**Assessment:** **Explicitly unverified wartime allegation**, not an established massacre or mutiny. **Follow-up open.**

### 1847

**HN-149 — September 28, 1847 — North Carolina volunteers confront their regimental commander in Mexico**  
**Source:** *Tri-weekly Journal* (Evansville), p. 2, reprinting a Mexican War correspondent of the New Orleans *Picayune* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TWJ18470928.1.2).  
**Report:** The account described discontent among North Carolina volunteers, a demand that their colonel resign, and resignations by subordinate officers that were later withdrawn. It also reported a soldier being wounded during the disturbance and described the immediate crisis as settled.  
**Assessment:** **Correspondent's report of a military confrontation**, not a full court-martial record; charges and the longer-term outcome require separate checking. **Follow-up open.**

### 1848

**HN-150 — February 29, 1848 — President remits Frémont's court-martial punishment**  
**Source:** *Tri-weekly Journal* (Evansville), p. 3, printing military orders and the president's decision via the *National Intelligencer* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TWJ18480229.1.3).  
**Report:** The reproduced order said Lieutenant Colonel **John C. Frémont** had been convicted by court-martial of mutiny, disobedience, and conduct prejudicial to military discipline and sentenced to dismissal. **President James K. Polk** approved the findings but remitted the punishment in consideration of Frémont's prior service; the newspaper further reported that Frémont resigned.  
**Assessment:** **Printed formal military disposition plus newspaper-reported resignation**. The conviction and the remission must be read together; simply calling him dismissed would misstate the printed result. **Follow-up open.**

### 1849

**HN-151 — March 24, 1849 — Doctors dispute signs of corrosive poisoning in a husband's death**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 2, lengthy testimony in the **Mary Smithers** investigation — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISS18490324.1.2).  
**Report:** Mrs. Smithers was examined in connection with the death of husband **James Smithers**, allegedly caused by sulfuric acid. Medical witnesses described early diagnoses involving disease, while a post-mortem revealed damage they considered consistent with a caustic substance; the account also exposed limits of the chemical evidence.  
**Assessment:** **Published preliminary criminal and medical evidence, not proof that the wife poisoned him.** The final judicial disposition remains untraced. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-152 — May 9, 1849 — Newlywed allegedly hands husband a forged $10,000 note**  
**Source:** *Madison Daily Courier*, p. 2, reprinting a New Orleans anecdote — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKMDC18490509.1.2).  
**Report:** An unnamed French woman was said to have married in New Orleans, passed her new husband a **$10,000 note** that later proved forged, and traveled north with him before the problem was discovered.  
**Assessment:** **Sensational, anonymous newspaper anecdote**, without named parties, court record, or independent confirmation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-153 — September 1, 1849 — Crew of California-bound ship *Memnon* reportedly mutinies off Montevideo**  
**Source:** *Wabash Courier*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WACO18490901.1.4).  
**Report:** The *Memnon*, sailing from New York toward California, reportedly encountered a refusal to work and violent confrontation between seamen, officers, and passengers near Montevideo in May. The vessel diverted to port, and the report said several crewmen were detained and handed over to the U.S. warship *St. Louis*.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted shipboard disturbance and detention account**; the later maritime or criminal proceedings have not been established. **Follow-up open.**

### 1850

**HN-154 — November 22, 1850 — A Cincinnati séance makes the newspapers**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 4, reproducing a Cincinnati account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18501122.1.4).  
**Report:** An observer described sitting with a woman represented as a clairvoyant, asking questions through a spoken alphabet, interpreting tapping noises as replies from dead relatives, and observing alleged involuntary movements. The piece also noted skeptics who dismissed the performance as trickery.  
**Assessment:** **Evidence of what a newspaper writer said happened at a séance**, not evidence of communication with the dead. **Follow-up open.**

### 1851

**HN-155 — March 13, 1851 — Steamboat *Oregon* destroyed in boiler explosion, *Iroquois* rescues survivors**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 3, reprinting the *Louisville Courier* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISSN18510313.1.3).  
**Report:** The steamer ***Oregon*** reportedly exploded while passing Island 82 on the Mississippi on **March 2**, with passengers just finishing dinner. The account described the bow and forward cabin torn apart, the vessel burning, and the nearby ***Iroquois*** bringing ladders alongside. It estimated **at least sixty killed or scalded** and reported that eleven injured people subsequently died aboard the rescuing vessel.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed early rescue and casualty account**, but the estimated victims killed or injured are combined and **not a verified final fatality count**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-156 — April 22, 1851 — “Spiritual knocking” debated as trickery or joint-cracking**  
**Source:** *Prairie Chieftain*, p. 2, an opinion essay discussing the Rochester rappers — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKPD18510422.1.2).  
**Report:** A contributor reviewed claims that mediums relayed messages from deceased people, then argued that alleged supernatural raps could have physical explanations such as noises from joints or sleight of hand. The writer referenced the Rochester “Knockers” and a disagreement over physicians' proposed mechanism.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary editorial skepticism about spiritualism**, not a scientific demonstration that any one mechanism caused the sounds. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-157 — July 2, 1851 — An explosive device said to have traveled aboard the *Mayflower***  
**Source:** *Plymouth Pilot*, p. 2, reprinting the *Detroit Free Press* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PP18510702.1.2).  
**Report:** A report said a boxed contrivance containing flammable material **exploded in a railway freight-depot passageway**, slightly burning a bystander. Its remnants reportedly included a card with the name **Henry French**. The original paper **inferred** that it had been placed aboard the steamer *Mayflower* at Buffalo to explode during its prior voyage.  
**Assessment:** **Reported actual depot explosion with an inferred attempted ship attack**; the purported intended target and identity of any sender were **not proven** in the account. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-158 — October 9, 1851 — New reports of deaths after the *Brilliant* steamboat explosion**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 3, dispatch datelined New Orleans, October 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISSN18511009.1.3).  
**Report:** A brief telegram said **two wounded victims** of the steamer ***Brilliant*** explosion had died the previous day. It described **seventeen injured people** brought to New Orleans aboard ***S. W. Downs***, one of whom died in transit, with other seriously injured survivors left at Plaquemine.  
**Assessment:** **Medical and casualty follow-up telegram**, not a comprehensive account of the initial disaster or final death toll. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-159 — November 8, 1851 — Three Buffalo business safes robbed and reportedly locked again**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 2, reprinting the *Buffalo Commercial* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EDJ18511108.1.2).  
**Report:** A dockside commission merchant and two nearby businesses were reportedly robbed of approximately **$2,500, $190, and $390**. In an unusual detail, the article claimed the safes or offices had been left apparently locked, delaying detection until the owners tried to retrieve the missing money.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted local crime report**, with its descriptions of entry and amounts not independently verified; no suspect or conviction identified. **Follow-up open.**

### 1852

**HN-160 — April 21, 1852 — Penal-colony revolt at the Strait of Magellan turns into ship seizures**  
**Source:** *Wabash Express*, p. 1, reprinting reports from Chile via the *National Intelligencer* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WE18520421.1.1).  
**Report:** The account recounted the **December 1851 revolt at the Fort Bulnes penal colony**, the reported killing of governor **Muñoz Gamero**, seizures of vessels including the American ***Florida*** and British ***Eliza Cornish***, and a violent struggle over their cargo. Importantly, the article **also reported a follow-up**: leader **Cambiaso** had been captured, chained at Chiloé, and some treasure recovered.  
**Assessment:** **Serialized/reprinted trans-Pacific revolt narrative containing its own later development**. Details of killings, cargo totals, and legal outcome need Chilean and maritime records. **Follow-up partially present; later judicial outcome open.**

**HN-161 — April 21, 1852 — New Orleans druggist describes an Indian-hemp self-experiment**  
**Source:** *Wabash Express*, p. 1, reprinting a *Savannah Republican* medical-interest piece — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WE18520421.1.1).  
**Report:** Druggist **B. Laplace** said he and another person took an experimental preparation of **Indian hemp**, which he called “hatchish,” then described unusual perception and excitement while considering possible medicinal value. The newspaper framed this as a novel inquiry into a poorly understood substance.  
**Assessment:** **Historical personal anecdote, not a controlled scientific finding or safety evidence**. Dose claims and physiological effects were not independently confirmed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-162 — June 25, 1852 — Audience challenges a medium to demonstrate “spiritual manifestations”**  
**Source:** *Indiana American*, p. 3, polemical report of a local spiritualist encounter — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IA18520625.1.3).  
**Report:** A writer described an invitation to a spiritualist lecturer to demonstrate phenomena before a committee under conditions designed to rule out ordinary physical explanations. In the account, the speaker did not produce the requested demonstration and said the spirits would not comply.  
**Assessment:** **A skeptic's partisan account of a public demonstration that did not occur**, not a universal test of spiritualist claims. **Follow-up open.**

### 1853

**HN-163 — August 18, 1853 — Highway killing at Vernon followed by arrests and fears of a mob**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Weekly Journal*, p. 2, dispatch dated Vernon, August 16 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CWJ18530818.1.2).  
**Report:** A short dispatch said **Frederick Alms** was killed near Vernon, Indiana, and **Patrick McGinn** (name uncertain in OCR) and three others were arrested. It added that an angry crowd was gathering in the courthouse square and threatening local shops.  
**Assessment:** **Early crime-and-unrest report**; the identities, reported mob intentions, and judicial outcomes need follow-up. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-164 — July 23, 1853 — Collision blamed for *Empire State* steamboat explosion**  
**Source:** *Wabash Courier*, p. 2, relaying a New York July 18 dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WACO18530723.1.2).  
**Report:** A dispatch attributed an explosion aboard the ***Empire State*** to a **sloop colliding with the steamer** and dislodging one of its boilers, which reportedly went overboard. It also carried an early list of people killed or drowned.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper's preliminary causal explanation and casualty list**, not an engineering finding; mechanism and final toll require corroboration. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-165 — September 1, 1853 — Wheeling wharf powder blast shatters nearby windows**  
**Source:** *Weekly Reveille* (Vevay), p. 2, reprinting a Wheeling dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WRE18530901.1.2).  
**Report:** The dispatch said about **38 kegs of gunpowder** exploded while being carted toward a boat. It reported the death of a drayman whose name appears as **Wallerston Kimberly** in imperfect OCR, serious injury to another person, and glass broken along Water Street. It explicitly said the cause was unknown.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary explosion report with an unresolved cause**. Names and the quantity of powder need checking against the scan or local Wheeling coverage. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-166 — December 2, 1853 — A Virginia ghost rumor allegedly points to a murder suspect**  
**Source:** *Terre-Haute Journal*, p. 1, short item about **Wetzel County, Virginia** — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=THJ18531202.1.1).  
**Report:** A brief related that people were telling a ghost story about a murdered man purportedly returning to demand justice. It asserted that a person identified in the rumor as the murderer had actually been arrested.  
**Assessment:** **Folkloric newspaper anecdote paired with an unverified arrest claim**, not evidence of an apparition or the arrested person's guilt. **Follow-up open.**

### 1854

**HN-167 — June 15, 1854 — Black Heath coal-mine disaster in Virginia**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 6, reprinting a Washington-area report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18540615.2.6).  
**Report:** A report from the **Black Heath coal pits** in Chesterfield County, near Richmond, said a violent explosion struck while roughly **twenty miners** were underground. A superintendent entered to retrieve bodies and said all those inside appeared dead; the paper said the cause was still unknown and men who had recently left had not noticed unusual gas levels.  
**Assessment:** **Initial mine-disaster report with an approximate fatality count and no established cause**. **Follow-up open.**

### 1855

**HN-168 — January 25, 1855 — Missing bank cashier and emptied vault, with two rival theories**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 14, Indianapolis correspondent's letter dated January 22 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550125.2.14).  
**Report:** The writer said **Frank May**, cashier of the **Farmers' and Mechanics' Bank of Indianapolis**, had disappeared and the bank's vault appeared emptied. The article offered two competing possibilities: robbers might have kidnapped or killed him and taken his keys, or he might have taken the money himself. It openly admitted the affair was unexplained.  
**Assessment:** **Documented contemporary uncertainty, not evidence May stole funds.** Attempts to search by name and bank for a later explanation met archival HTTP errors. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-169 — June 9, 1855 — Murder conviction overturned over faulty jury instructions**  
**Source:** *Daily American* (Terre Haute), p. 2, court report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDA18550609.1.2).  
**Report:** A court reportedly reversed **William H. Arrison's** first-degree murder conviction because jurors had not been allowed to weigh lesser homicide findings under the evidence and instructions. The matter was returned for another trial.  
**Assessment:** **Reported procedural reversal and retrial, not an acquittal or adjudication of innocence.** **Follow-up open.**

**HN-170 — July 19, 1855 — David M. Stocking condemned to death for John Rose's killing**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 10, publishing the judgment and judge's remarks — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550719.2.10).  
**Report:** The trial court found **David M. Stocking** guilty of first-degree murder in the killing of **John Rose** and imposed a death sentence, with an execution date initially set for **September 14**. The report discussed the evidence and the judge's remarks.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary conviction and sentence report; it must be read with HN-173 and HN-174**, because his execution was first postponed and then reported as carried out in January 1856. **Not evidence he was executed in September.**

**HN-171 — September 13, 1855 — Kankakee land sale allegedly turns into robbery and murder**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 9, reprinting the Kankakee *Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550913.2.9).  
**Report:** A sensational account alleged that buyers of a farm killed the seller to recover **$150**, invented a story that he had departed for Canada, and hid his body in the **Iroquois River**. The report named several suspects and claimed the body was later found and some arrests followed.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted criminal allegation with the period source's racialized descriptions omitted here.** No convictions or reliable identification of the unnamed victim have been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-172 — September 13, 1855 — Michigan farmer reportedly predicts his death that morning**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 9, brief attributed account from **Brockway, Michigan** — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550913.2.9).  
**Report:** A man identified as **J. Westbrook** was said to have told his son he would die that day, requested a final ride around the farm, and died soon afterward despite seeming healthy that morning.  
**Assessment:** **Extraordinary anecdote presented as local hearsay**, with neither medical explanation nor independent corroboration. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-173 — September 13, 1855 — Governor postpones three executions pending appeals**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 9, reprinting a *Sentinel* report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550913.2.9); the July [Stocking trial coverage](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18550719.2.10) also uses **Driscoll**, whereas the January 1856 account spells the name **Driskill**.  
**Report:** The newspaper said that once the Supreme Court records arrived for **Abraham Rice, Timothy Driscoll (Driskill in later coverage), and David M. Stocking**, Governor **Joseph A. Wright** delayed their executions because the court could not hear the matters until its November term. The newly listed date was **January 11, 1856**.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary follow-up materially qualifying HN-170**. This September article reports a postponement, but **a January 1856 report (HN-174) subsequently describes the executions as having occurred**. The appellate history has not been reconstructed from court records.

### 1856

**HN-174 — January 17, 1856 — Follow-up: Rice, Driskill, and Stocking reportedly executed in Lafayette**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 3, reprinting a first-person *Lafayette Journal* account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18560117.2.3).  
**Report:** The extensive eyewitness-style account described **Abram Rice, Timothy Driskill, and David M. Stocking** being hanged in connection with the murders of **Cephas Farenbaugh** and **John Rose**. It recounted their last conversations, their statements disputing or qualifying responsibility, and the execution ceremony. The reprinted writer said it had happened “yesterday”; the exact day depends on the date of the original *Lafayette Journal* issue, not the January 17 reprint.  
**Assessment:** **Substantive published follow-up to HN-170 and HN-173.** The earlier accounts render **Driskill** as **Driscoll**, a spelling discrepancy still to reconcile. The report supersedes the earlier uncertainty over whether their death sentences were carried out, but does **not** by itself validate the trial evidence or fix the precise execution date. **Underlying case files remain unreviewed.**

**HN-175 — January 24, 1856 — Religious sect member reportedly confesses to New Haven-area killing**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 13, reprinting an unidentified earlier account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18560124.2.13).  
**Report:** A report concerning the death of **Justus Matthews** near New Haven, Connecticut, said **Samuel Sly** had been arrested and confessed to a brutal attack. It linked his stated motivations to a spiritualist sect called the **Wakemanites**, associated with his sister, **Mrs. Wakeman**, and quoted extraordinary claims she reportedly made about a prior journey to the spirit world.  
**Assessment:** **Reprinted report of an alleged confession and religious beliefs**, not verified trial testimony or proof of supernatural explanations. The court outcome has not been traced. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-176 — April 17, 1856 — Nine sailors arrested for mutiny counterclaim brutal treatment**  
**Source:** *Daily State Sentinel* (Indianapolis), p. 3, reprinting maritime news from New York — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DSS18560417-01.1.3).  
**Report:** Nine crewmen of the Liverpool packet ***Underwriter*** were reportedly arrested in New York and brought before federal authorities for alleged mutiny. The article said they had arrived emaciated after confinement in irons and that the men accused officers of severe flogging as an explanation for their actions.  
**Assessment:** **Competing shipboard allegations reported in the same newspaper account**; neither the mutiny charge nor the cruelty complaint is resolved here. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-177 — April 17, 1856 — Sailor's letter alleges kidnapping for the Chinese indentured-labor trade**  
**Source:** *Daily State Sentinel*, p. 3, reproducing an attributed shipboard letter and an explicitly political editorial introduction — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DSS18560417-01.1.3).  
**Report:** The letter described Chinese people allegedly abducted, confined on vessels, sold to labor shippers, and guarded by armed crews to prevent escape. It claimed the writer's vessel was carrying hundreds of such passengers, who were being sent into harsh bonded work. The editor used the material polemically in a comparison with American slavery.  
**Assessment:** **First-person-attributed accusation of forced labor and human trafficking**, reproduced through a partisan newspaper; its particulars, ship identity, and numbers require independent evidence. **Follow-up open.**

### 1857

**HN-178 — February 20, 1857 — Burdell murder inquest examines marriage claim and identifies a suspect**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 9, a New York February 10 inquest dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18570220.2.9).  
**Report:** In the investigation into **Dr. Harvey Burdell's** murder, a witness reportedly said that he heard a cry and saw a man leaving or opening the house on the fatal evening; he later identified **Eckel** in an arranged identification. A business ledger and a bank director's testimony were also discussed in relation to a disputed marriage claim.  
**Assessment:** **Preliminary witness and documentary testimony**, not a finding that Eckel committed the murder or that any marriage was proved or disproved. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-179 — September 9, 1857 — Fatal blasts at du Pont's powder works on the Brandywine**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 9, an August 23 Wilmington, Delaware dispatch with later casualty update — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18570909.2.9).  
**Report:** At **du Pont's powder mills**, a heavy box was reportedly moved against the wall of a powder-house and friction ignited material. The account described a second explosion as **Alexis du Pont** investigated the fire. It reported that du Pont, foreman **Edward Hurst**, and **Anthony Dougherty** died, with further injuries.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary disaster report with a proposed ignition sequence and named casualties**. The technical cause and complete victim list were not independently checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-180 — December 2, 1857 — Distant report claims a thousand killed by lightning-triggered magazine blast**  
**Source:** *Jasper Banner*, p. 15, relaying a translated Persian letter printed in a South Asian paper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPBN18571202.2.15).  
**Report:** Under a sensational headline claiming **one thousand lives lost**, the article described lightning striking a ruler's ammunition magazine at a place rendered by OCR as **Loudpore**, devastating a fort, nearby houses, and people inside them. The source itself was a chain of transmission from a letter translated and reprinted abroad.  
**Assessment:** **Unverified, highly amplified foreign disaster report**. The headline's casualty claim is **not treated as an established death count**, and both place-name and event require original-language checks. **Follow-up open.**

### 1858

**HN-181 — February 25, 1858 — Gas explosion wrecks Cincinnati Methodist church during revival**  
**Source:** *Marshall County Republican*, p. 2, reprinting the *Cincinnati Commercial* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=MCR18580225.1.2).  
**Report:** A large explosion at the **Sixth Street Methodist Church** in Cincinnati damaged the church basement and shocked a city already aware of large revival gatherings there. Early rumors were alarming, but the firsthand newspaper account emphasized that **nobody had been killed outright** in the blast and described an injury list.  
**Assessment:** **Early local disaster coverage**; the absence of immediate deaths does not establish that no victim later died. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-182 — April 17, 1858 — Mutineers seize New Bedford whaler *Junior* on Christmas night**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Review*, p. 1, reproducing a January report attributed to a Sydney, Australia, newspaper — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CVR18580417.1.1).  
**Report:** The New Bedford whaler ***Junior***, near the New Zealand whaling grounds, reportedly suffered an armed crew revolt around **Christmas 1857**. The account described killings of senior officers, serious injuries, and mutineers later leaving the ship in boats near **Twofold Bay**. It named **James Plummer** as a leader, although the numbers of participants are not entirely clear from the reprint.  
**Assessment:** **Long, secondhand maritime-crime narrative with names and geography**, but without the logbook or a final trial outcome. **Follow-up open.**

### 1859

**HN-183 — February 9, 1859 — Belgian murder supposedly located through a girl's dream**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Gazette*, p. 3, summarizing an unnamed Belgian journal — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLGZ18590209.2.3).  
**Report:** The extraordinary reprinted story said a **ten-year-old girl** dreamed her father, uncle, relatives, and their driver had been murdered, identified two possible attackers, and directed authorities to a forest where several bodies were allegedly found.  
**Assessment:** **Sensational transnational dream-and-crime anecdote**, without named original publication, documented investigation, or verification of prophetic ability. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-184 — October 19, 1859 — Partisan editor alleges counterfeit election tickets in Jasper County**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Gazette*, p. 17, lengthy Republican partisan opinion — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLGZ18591019.2.17).  
**Report:** A partisan writer accused a rival printer of preparing **Republican-labeled election tickets** with a concealed candidate substitution and distributing them in **Jasper County, Indiana**, to mislead voters. The article named local political opponents and made sweeping personal accusations.  
**Assessment:** **Documented published allegation of ballot deception, not a verified finding of forgery or proof against the named printer**. The disputed tickets, election results, and any proceedings were not independently examined. **Follow-up open.**

### 1860

**HN-185 — March 9, 1860 — Imprisoned man's reported confession to Vincennes post-office robbery**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 2, reprinting local correspondence — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EDJ18600309.1.2).  
**Report:** A man named **John Winter**, already imprisoned with **Frank E. Riley** and **Adam Snyder** on robbery charges, reportedly told a visiting sheriff that the group had broken into the **Vincennes post office** in October 1859, rifled mailbags, and discarded them in a river; he also attributed other burglaries to the group.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper's report of a purported prison confession**, not a separately documented conviction for each offense mentioned. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-186 — April 16, 1860 — Express agent acquitted at preliminary robbery hearing**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EDJ18600416.1.1).  
**Report:** **James Reynolds**, the Vincennes agent of **Adams Express**, had been arrested in connection with a robbery of that company's office. The paper reported that a preliminary examination **acquitted him**, and described public criticism of the company superintendent for initiating the arrest. This was an **express-office case, distinct from HN-185's post-office robbery**.  
**Assessment:** **Published favorable preliminary disposition**. The word *acquitted* describes the paper's report of that hearing, not a reconstructed full case history. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-187 — December 31, 1860 — Eight sworn statements dispute simple explanation of steamboat explosion**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 1, printing signed and notarized witness statements — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EDJ18601231.1.1).  
**Report:** After the ***W. V. Gillum*** exploded on **December 24**, the paper printed affidavits from its captain, firemen, and other crew. Several insisted there had been **adequate boiler water immediately beforehand**; one crewman also recalled an earlier incident in which boiler metal became red-hot. A separate notice offered the wreck for sale.  
**Assessment:** **Unusually detailed contemporaneous evidence from witnesses with possible interests in the investigation**; their agreement does **not** settle the engineering cause. **Follow-up open.**

### 1861

**HN-188 — March 30, 1861 — Balloonist plans transatlantic experiment and is mocked by a newspaper**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Review*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CVR18610330.1.2).  
**Report:** A short item ridiculed **Professor Thaddeus S. C. Lowe** for announcing ambitious balloon voyages, including a projected Atlantic crossing based on supposed upper-atmosphere winds, without yet completing them. It described a proposed flight from Cincinnati toward the coast as preparation.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary skeptical commentary about a proposed experiment**, not an accomplished ocean crossing. Compare his later military balloon reporting in HN-191 and public flight in HN-200. **Follow-up partially present.**

**HN-189 — May 2, 1861 — Burning oil well causes mass casualties in Pennsylvania's new petroleum fields**  
**Source:** *Plymouth Weekly Democrat*, p. 1, reprinting a *Buffalo Courier* correspondent — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PWD18610502.1.1).  
**Report:** At a well on **Buchanan Farm**, near **Tidioute, Pennsylvania**, a sudden surge of oil or gas ignited and engulfed nearby drilling works and spectators. The paper named several reported dead and badly injured people, described **roughly three dozen wounded**, and printed a grim account of one victim who died after trying to make a will.  
**Assessment:** **Graphic initial petroleum-industry disaster account**, with an uncertain final toll and no independent determination of ignition mechanics. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-190 — July 1, 1861 — Spy accused of carrying arsenic toward a military spring**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 3, Washington dispatch of June 28 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EDJ18610701.1.3).  
**Report:** The dispatch stated that Rhode Island soldiers arrested a purported spy carrying **several revolvers and about a pound of arsenic**. It surmised that he intended to poison a nearby spring used by troops.  
**Assessment:** **Report of an arrest and alleged possession, followed by the newspaper's inference of intent**. There is no proof here that the water was poisoned or the prisoner convicted. **Follow-up open.**

### 1862

**HN-191 — June 3, 1862 — Battlefield reports transmitted from Lowe's tethered balloon**  
**Source:** *Daily Evansville Journal*, p. 3, dispatch from the fighting near Richmond — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DEJ18620603.1.3); [Library of Congress historical context](https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/civil-war-in-america/april-1862-november-1862.html).  
**Report:** The article said **Lowe's balloon** observed a battle near **Fair Oaks/Seven Pines** from roughly **2,000 feet**, with **Parker Spring** operating a telegraph wire to send observations about Confederate movements to **General McClellan**. The correspondent heralded this as a landmark wartime use of an airborne telegraph station.  
**Assessment:** **Period account of an early military observation-and-telegraph operation**, supported in broad historical context by the Library of Congress; specific claims of technological priority remain the correspondent's. **Follow-up partially present.**

**HN-192 — September 19, 1862 — Allegheny Arsenal blast kills scores of munitions workers**  
**Source:** *Daily Evansville Journal*, p. 3, Pittsburgh dispatches dated September 17–18 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DEJ18620919.1.3).  
**Report:** An explosion destroyed the arsenal's laboratory while many **women and young workers** were employed there. Early telegrams estimated about **70–80 dead**, described repeated blasts and a rapidly spreading fire, and noted the city shutting businesses in mourning. The report said the cause was not known.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary tragedy and evolving casualty estimates**, not final fatalities or a proved ignition source. **Follow-up open.**

### 1863

**HN-193 — April 30, 1863 — Soldiers accused of mutiny aboard Mississippi Marine Brigade vessel**  
**Source:** *Daily Evansville Journal*, p. 2, reprinting the *New Albany Ledger* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DEJ18630430.1.2).  
**Report:** Soldiers aboard the brigade's flagship ***Autocrat*** allegedly refused orders over complaints about rations. The reprinted article sided with the quartermaster, described confinement in irons, and said **General Ellet** threatened severe punishment if the disruption continued.  
**Assessment:** **Military-discipline account from a sympathetic newspaper source**. The soldiers' complaints, the commander's reported words, and any court-martial dispositions are not independently established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-194 — June 9, 1863 — Lit pipe allegedly ignites powder in an Evansville shop**  
**Source:** *Daily Evansville Journal*, p. 2, local eyewitness-style coverage — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DEJ18630609.1.2).  
**Report:** The paper described an explosion at a store at **Eighth and Chestnut** that shattered windows, walls, and merchandise. It attributed ignition to the proprietor allegedly **weighing gunpowder while holding a lit pipe**, and reported severe burns to him and a customer, with others injured.  
**Assessment:** **Local disaster report with a specific contemporary causal claim**, not a completed official technical investigation or a confirmed final medical outcome. **Follow-up open.**

### 1864

**HN-195 — March 17, 1864 — A sailor describes a nearly submerged craft sinking the *Housatonic***  
**Source:** *Marshall County Republican*, p. 1, reprinting a naval officer's account from the *Army and Navy Gazette* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=MCR18640317.1.1).  
**Report:** A naval participant described a **quiet, nearly submerged craft** approaching the blockading U.S. sloop ***Housatonic*** on **February 17**. The ship's crew fired at it, but an explosive charge struck the vessel, which sank rapidly. The report listed **five sailors missing** and said the unusual attacking machine was **not seen after the blast**.  
**Assessment:** **Specific firsthand-attributed account of submarine warfare**, not evidence the craft or crew permanently vanished. The attacking vessel's identity and later discovery are outside what this particular report establishes. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-196 — August 2, 1864 — Early report of Petersburg mine attack obscures its later defeat**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 3, contemporary battle dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVDJ18640802.1.3); [National Park Service battle assessment](https://www.nps.gov/civilwar/search-battles-detail.htm?battleCode=va070).  
**Report:** The newspaper vividly described the **July 30 mine explosion** under Confederate entrenchments outside **Petersburg**, a simultaneous Union artillery bombardment, and initial gains by Burnside's troops through the gap. The dispatch captured the attack's early promise. The National Park Service's retrospective account records that the Union assault subsequently collapsed in confusion and was **repulsed**.  
**Assessment:** **Period battlefield snapshot placed against corroborated later outcome**, demonstrating why an early dispatch cannot be read as the final result. **Outcome contextualized.**

### 1865

**HN-197 — April 20, 1865 — First assassination telegram misstates the fate of Secretary Seward**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Weekly Journal*, p. 1, reprinting hurried April 14–15 Washington dispatches — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CWJ18650420.1.1); [U.S. State Department's April 15 correction](https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1865p2/d206).  
**Report:** Amid the devastating news of **Abraham Lincoln's assassination**, one early dispatch said the president, **Secretary William H. Seward**, and his son had been assassinated. In fact, Seward and his son were **gravely injured in the coordinated attack but survived**. An official diplomatic report dated April 15 already described the assault on Seward as unsuccessful.  
**Assessment:** **Identifiable false early-news claim directly corrected by contemporary government documentation**. This is an ordinary example of incomplete breaking news, **not evidence of suppression**. **Correction established.**

**HN-198 — June 3, 1865 — Ordnance magazine explodes in occupied Mobile**  
**Source:** *Daily State Sentinel*, p. 2, quoting Mobile and New Orleans reports — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DSS18650603.1.2).  
**Report:** Reports described an immense explosion in **Marshall's warehouse** near **Commerce Street** in Mobile, Alabama, where Union ordnance was being stored or transferred. The blast devastated nearby buildings, scattered explosive shells, and prompted emergency orders from **General Gordon Granger**; a correspondent suggested an accidental percussion-shell detonation.  
**Assessment:** **Postwar military-storage disaster coverage**. The suggested accidental trigger and evolving casualty estimates are not established technical findings. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-199 — September 13, 1865 — Ammunition train detonates near Nashville**  
**Source:** *Evansville Daily Journal*, p. 1, reprinting Nashville *Press and Times* coverage — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVDJ18650913.1.1).  
**Report:** A military train carrying several cars of ammunition exploded roughly **seven miles from Nashville**, damaging the railroad and surrounding countryside. Early news named **three people killed immediately**, others badly hurt, and **an estimated eighty tons of explosive cargo**. The reporter suspected a carelessly packed shell but supplied no final finding.  
**Assessment:** **Early catastrophe report with provisional cause and loss figures**, not a completed railway or ordnance investigation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-200 — October 12, 1865 — Wartime observation balloon carries an acrobat above New York**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Weekly Journal*, p. 1, reprinting a New York newspaper account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CWJ18651012.1.1).  
**Report:** **Professor Lowe's** former war balloon ***United States*** ascended near **Central Park** with Lowe, a passenger, and daredevil **Harry Leslie** aboard. The article describes Leslie descending on a rope and performing acrobatics in midair before the balloon eventually landed near **Yonkers**.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed contemporary spectacle report**, without separate event records or accident log; a vivid later chapter in Lowe's aviation career, following HN-188 and HN-191. **Follow-up open.**

### 1866

**HN-201 — February 2, 1866 — Reports put the toll of the steamer Miami explosion near 150**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Daily Herald*, p. 1, telegraphic news summary — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IDD18660202-01.1.1).  
**Report:** New reports from **Memphis** concerned the explosion of the steamboat ***Miami*** on the **Arkansas River**. The dispatch estimated that the death toll might reach **150**, said only seven crew members were known to have survived, and described bodies recovered and seriously injured people hospitalized at **Helena**.  
**Assessment:** **Early disaster estimate, not a verified final casualty list.** The archive page reports the event and figures but does not provide an accident investigation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-202 — February 2, 1866 — Clipper crew arrested after alleged South American mutiny**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Daily Herald*, p. 1, San Francisco dispatch dated February 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IDD18660202-01.1.1).  
**Report:** A brief dispatch said crewmen from the clipper ***White Swallow***, recently arrived from New York at San Francisco, had been **arrested for mutiny** involving an incident off the South American coast.  
**Assessment:** **An arrest report only.** The charge, causes, evidence, and disposition cannot be recovered from the few printed lines. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-203 — May 14, 1866 — Old state-prison contract accusations revived in a partisan investigation**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 2, political letter citing an **1861 legislative committee report** — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18660514.1.2).  
**Report:** A newspaper contributor accused Indiana politician **William H. Talbott** of securing a Michigan City state-prison building contract through improper dealings. The article quoted a legislative committee as finding that competitors' bids had been suppressed, that **$1,000** was paid in connection with withdrawing a rival bid, and that the state had lost **$56,093.33** compared with an alternative proposal. The author also said the contract was surrendered after the inquiry.  
**Assessment:** **Partisan newspaper argument citing identifiable official material**, not a newly adjudicated 1866 fraud. The committee's complete record and Talbott's response have not been independently read. **Source-context follow-up open.**

**HN-204 — June 30, 1866 — Buzzards lead searchers to a missing man's body in Indiana**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Review*, p. 2, local Montgomery County reporting — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CVR18660630.1.2).  
**Report:** **Nehemiah T. Ham** had vanished after leaving a gathering in **Alamo** in late April. After an unsuccessful search, men driving cattle noticed buzzards gathering near a fallen tree, where Ham's remains were found concealed under wood and leaves. The newspaper named several people **jailed on suspicion of murder** and others held over an earlier alleged robbery.  
**Assessment:** **Documented discovery and arrest report**, not proof any named suspect committed murder. The reported corpse's discovery resolves the *physical disappearance* but not the full criminal case. **Court outcome open.**

### 1867

**HN-205 — August 9, 1867 — Indiana “ghost” promises treasure beneath an elm**
