**Source:** *Decatur Eagle*, p. 3, local report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCEG18670809.1.3).  
**Report:** Residents near the **old Ross farm**, between Pleasant Mills and Wilshire, reported noises and a white apparition that allegedly told one investigator a murdered man's hidden money would be found if his remains received proper burial. A crowd later assembled, but the apparition did not return. The editor described plans to dig and joked that success might justify summoning **Captain Kidd** next.  
**Assessment:** **A witty contemporary account of a local haunting claim, not corroboration of a spirit, murder, or buried treasure.** The paper itself signaled skepticism. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-206 — August 26, 1867 — Wife stranded after husband's disappearance at Evansville wharf**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 8, local report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18670826.1.8).  
**Report:** **Andrew Curry** (middle initial unclear in OCR), traveling with his wife from Illinois toward **Cannelton**, left her at a wharf boat in Evansville to ask about an upriver steamer and did not return. The paper described her as without money and fearful for him; **foul play was suspected**.  
**Assessment:** **An unresolved-as-printed missing-person report.** The article does not prove a crime or establish Curry's ultimate whereabouts. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-207 — November 8, 1867 — Two steamship employees disappear at sea**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 1, reprinting November 7 New York dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18671108.1.1).  
**Report:** The steamship ***Cumberland***, arriving from **Puerto Cabello**, reported that its **first assistant engineer** and **stewardess** had disappeared aboard ship during the night of October 31. The first report presumed they might have fallen overboard but said it did not know whether an accident or deliberate act was involved.  
**Assessment:** **Specific reported disappearance of two crew members; cause unknown in this dispatch.** No name list, ship's log, inquiry, or later outcome has been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-208 — December 16, 1867 — Clerkenwell Prison blast and uncertain early arrests**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Daily Herald*, p. 1, London dispatches from December 14 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IDD18671216-01.1.1).  
**Report:** Dispatches described the explosive breach of a wall at **Clerkenwell Prison**, destruction of nearby houses and rising casualties. They also reported **Timothy Desmond, Jerry Allen, and Ann Justice** being examined because they were near the scene and allegedly fled; the same report said **nothing definite had been proved against them**, and they were remanded. Commentators immediately attributed the outrage to **Fenian** activity.  
**Assessment:** **Catastrophe and preliminary judicial reports**, sharply distinguishing the attack from unproved accusations against specific arrestees. Death figures varied even within the day's dispatches. **Full inquiry open.**

### 1868

**HN-209 — May 25, 1868 — Sudden giant sinkhole reported near the Florida–Georgia line**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 1, telegram relayed via Augusta — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18680525.1.1).  
**Report:** A dispatch transmitted via **Augusta, Georgia** and attributed to **Lake City, Florida**, described a farm in **Hamilton County** suffering a sudden ground collapse: about **two acres reportedly dropped fifty feet**, filling with water, and the affected area was said to be expanding to four acres. The writer suggested nearby streams and groundwater had found a new outlet.  
**Assessment:** **Striking secondhand geological report, not a measured survey.** The Augusta relay point is **not** evidence the farm was in Georgia; Hamilton County, Florida, lies near Lake City. The newspaper's rough dimensions and precise site have **not** been independently verified. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-210 — May 25–26, 1868 — Armed robbers separate an express car from its train**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, May 25, p. 1 — [first reports](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18680525.1.1); *Daily State Sentinel*, May 26, p. 2 — [additional account](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DSS18680526-01.1.2).  
**Report:** Armed men attacked a train stopped for water at **Marshfield** on the **Jeffersonville Railroad** near Seymour, Indiana. Reports described attacks on the engine crew and express messenger **E. G. Harkness**, the robbers uncoupling the express car, fleeing with the engine and car, and forcing safes. Initial figures differed over **how many safes were looted and the amount stolen**; some headlines claimed roughly **$30,000** before an accounting was complete.  
**Assessment:** **Two contemporary accounts of the same robbery, with developing and conflicting particulars.** Neither the precise loss nor the offenders' eventual fate is established from these pages. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-211 — June 17, 1868 — Evansville merchant vanishes from his wharf boat**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 8, local report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18680617.1.8).  
**Report:** **Benjamin (“Ben”) Hubbs**, partner in **Hubbs, Patrick & Co.**, was last seen partly undressed aboard the firm's Evansville wharf boat late at night. His watchman found his room empty and searched the boats and waterfront. The paper printed three competing theories: accidental drowning, suicide during a temporary crisis, or voluntary disappearance under financial pressure.  
**Assessment:** **A disappearance with competing newspaper speculation, not evidence any particular theory is correct.** The report states that the search had not yet established his fate. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-212 — June 23, 1868 — U.S. sailors help suppress mutiny in Antwerp**  
**Source:** *Evansville Journal*, p. 1, Antwerp dispatch dated June 22 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EJ18680623.1.1); [U.S. diplomatic report establishing the Ticonderoga was at Antwerp that month](https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1868p2/d82).  
**Report:** An American clipper named ***Neru*** (OCR spelling uncertain) reportedly experienced a violent crew mutiny while docked at **Antwerp**. Officers were assaulted, local police boarded, and sailors from the nearby USS ***Ticonderoga*** reportedly assisted in suppressing the disturbance.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary transatlantic dispatch**, with independent confirmation of the *Ticonderoga*'s presence in Antwerp that month—not independent confirmation of its alleged intervention. The clipper's name needs page-image confirmation, and later prosecution or disciplinary records have not been located. **Follow-up open.**

### 1869

**HN-213 — May 20, 1869 — “Spirit photograph” seller cleared in New York fraud proceeding**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Weekly Journal*, p. 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CWJ18690520.1.7); [Library of Congress historical context](https://blogs.loc.gov/loc/2017/10/a-ghostly-image-spirit-photographs/).  
**Report:** An article described the court examination of **William H. Mumler**, who produced photographs allegedly showing deceased people beside living sitters. It reported his **acquittal**, recited accounts from fascinated witnesses, and printed Mumler's insistence that no trick was involved.  
**Assessment:** **A genuine fraud proceeding, not confirmation that spirits appeared in photographs.** The Library of Congress likewise notes that Mumler was charged but not convicted; the newspaper's speculation about inexplicable photographs is the author's opinion. **Legal result contextualized.**

**HN-214 — October 9, 1869 — Fairground steam engine explodes after machinery trials**  
**Source:** *Crawfordsville Review*, p. 1, reproducing an *Indianapolis Mirror* account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=CVR18691009.1.1).  
**Report:** An immense boiler explosion at the **Indianapolis state fair** followed competitive machinery demonstrations, tearing apart a steam-powered sawmill area. The headline reported **twenty killed and fifty wounded**, while the article collected witness observations and manufacturer **Mr. Sinker's** account of steam gauges, testing and operation. It also complained that pickpockets preyed on the panicked crowd.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed contemporary catastrophe journalism**, but the death tally and technical explanation were not a completed official inquiry. **Cause and final toll open.**

### 1870

**HN-215 — September 3, 1870 — Missing Indiana man allegedly held for escalating ransom**  
**Source:** *Terre Haute Daily Gazette*, p. 2, news from Salem, Washington County — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=THDG18700903.1.2).  
**Report:** **Randall Boling** reportedly vanished from his home near **Salem**. His father-in-law **John Ryman** received letters purporting to come from a “Seymour Vigilance Committee,” demanding first **$1,500** and then **$3,000** in exchange for Boling's life. The paper explicitly said some residents thought the kidnapping genuine, while others suspected Boling might have authored the letters himself.  
**Assessment:** **Documented ransom-letter allegation with competing contemporary theories**. The letters' provenance and Boling's fate are not established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-216 — November 4, 1870 — Nitro-glycerine magazines obliterate four workers at Fairport**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1, reprinting the *Cleveland Herald* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN18701104-01.1.1).  
**Report:** A vivid account described nearly simultaneous explosions in two nitroglycerine storage magazines at **Fairport**, near Cleveland, causing widespread shaking, shattered windows and large craters. It identified workers **Nelson Malone, Patrick Scribner and Edward Duncan**, and mentioned a fourth man near the works.  
**Assessment:** **Specific industrial disaster reporting**, with witness descriptions; a definitive ignition cause was **not** established by this account. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-217 — November 18, 1870 — Soldiers reported mutinying when families barred from transport**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1, Quebec dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN18701118-01.1.1).  
**Report:** A telegram said British troops of the **69th Regiment**, stationed in **Quebec**, attacked officers aboard a vessel after wives and children were reportedly denied passage when the unit was transferred toward **Bermuda**. The report alleged several deaths and injuries before order was restored.  
**Assessment:** **A dramatic first dispatch, not a verified official court-martial account**. The identity of the ship, casualties and stated motive need independent checking. **Follow-up open.**

### 1871

**HN-218 — August 2 and 10, 1871 — Staten Island ferry boiler blast: rapidly changing estimates**  
**Source:** *Terre-Haute Weekly Express*, August 2, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=THWE18710802.1.2); *Rensselaer Union*, August 10, p. 2 — [subsequent compilation](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18710810.2.2).  
**Report:** The ferry ***Westfield*** suffered a boiler explosion at its **Whitehall Street** landing in New York. The earlier paper printed a preliminary speculation of **150 deaths**, then a same-day dispatch identifying **31 deaths** at midnight. Witness and engineer **Henry Robinson** gave detailed, at times conflicting, statements about boiler condition, water, steam pressure and inspections. Later newspaper coverage discussed the people trapped under the shattered forward decks and questions about an earlier repair.  
**Assessment:** **Verified as a contemporary disaster report, with explicit internal inconsistency in early casualty estimates.** No definitive cause or final death count should be inferred from either snapshot. **Inquiry follow-up open.**

### 1872

**HN-219 — April 12, 1872 — Steamboat Oceanus explodes and burns on the Mississippi**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 3, Cairo dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18720412.1.3).  
**Report:** The steamer ***Oceanus***, traveling from the Red River region toward St. Louis, blew a boiler near **Brooks Point**, above **Cairo, Illinois**, early on April 11. The ruined vessel then burned to the waterline. Rescuers described people clinging to a paddle wheel that gave way and others dying from burns or in the river. The headline suggested **sixty or seventy people lost**.  
**Assessment:** **An immediate disaster dispatch containing named witnesses and surviving crew**, with an **early casualty estimate** rather than a final investigation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-220 — September 12, 1872 — Newspaper counters alleged alteration of the date of Grant's gift**  
**Source:** *Daily Wabash Express*, p. 2, sharply partisan editorial reproducing its own **February 28, 1866** dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DWE18720912.1.2).  
**Report:** The editor attacked **Thomas A. Hendricks** for publicly citing a **$100,000 gift to Ulysses S. Grant** as if it had been presented in **1869**, after Grant's election. To refute that chronology, the newspaper reprinted its **1866 Associated Press notice** of the gift and named contributors, arguing that the later date made an ordinary gift appear like an attempt to influence a president.  
**Assessment:** **A documentable date dispute embedded in political invective.** The old dispatch supports the **1866 date**; the paper's assertions about Hendricks's intent and personal responsibility for a “forgery” are accusations, **not independently established findings.**

**HN-221 — December 27, 1872 — Passenger cars fall from trestle and burn near Prospect Station**  
**Source:** *Terre Haute Evening Gazette*, p. 1, Buffalo dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=THG18721227.1.1).  
**Report:** At **Prospect Station, New York**, a broken rail on a high trestle caused passenger and baggage cars to tumble roughly **thirty feet** while the locomotive crossed safely. The crushed cars ignited, severely hampering rescue; victims and witnesses described people trapped inside. The report separately mentioned a **second, still poorly detailed Louisville-area wreck** the same day.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary investigation-stage coverage of a major railway calamity**. The headline combined deaths and injuries, and neither definitive totals nor responsibility should be assumed. **Follow-up open.**

### 1873

**HN-222 — June 6, 1873 — Polaris survivors reach Washington; suspected mutiny not yet substantiated**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1, reporting the arrival of the U.S. steamer *Frolic* and an interview with **Captain George E. Tyson** — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN18730606-01.1.1); [Smithsonian Institution's Polaris expedition record description](https://siarchives.si.edu/collections/siris_arc_216675).  
**Report:** Survivors of the U.S. Arctic expedition aboard the ***Polaris*** arrived at the Washington Navy Yard. The paper said **Secretary of the Navy George Robeson** spoke privately with Tyson about the expedition, Captain **Charles Francis Hall's** death, and the condition of the ship. Tyson attributed Hall's death to **apoplexy**, saw no serious mutiny before his party separated, and expected the *Polaris* might return. The Secretary reserved any suspected mutiny question for a formal inquiry.  
**Assessment:** **A preliminary survivor interview, not the final explanation of Hall's death, the ship's fate, or crew relations.** This early natural-death account should be read alongside the later allegations in HN-224. **Outcome not settled by this report.**

**HN-223 — September 8, 1873 — Stranded whalers endure an Arctic winter; fourteen reported dead**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 1, New Bedford telegram — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18730908.1.1).  
**Report:** The schooner ***Abbie Bradford*** reportedly brought word of crews from two New Bedford whaling vessels, the ***Ossay Taft*** and ***Ansel Gibbs***, stranded near **Marble Island, Hudson Bay**. Men were said to have lived ashore for many months, and **fourteen** reportedly died of scurvy and exposure before survivors were retrieved.  
**Assessment:** **A specific maritime survival report**, but the digitized OCR displays a **wreck year later than its 1873 publication**, an impossibility requiring page-image review. This entry therefore does **not assert the day or year of the actual wrecks**. The casualty number also awaits corroboration from logs. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-224 — October 16, 1873 — Poisoning allegation challenges first account of Arctic explorer's death**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, Dundee correspondent of the *New York Herald* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18731016.2.5); [Smithsonian's surviving expedition records](https://siarchives.si.edu/collections/siris_arc_216675).  
**Report:** A correspondent reported **William Morton's** assertion that Captain **Hall** fell ill after drinking coffee, had complained he was being poisoned, and received injections from expedition physician **Emil Bessels**. The writer said other witnesses' accounts appeared to support suspicion but also gave opposing explanations; another officer and Bessels said Hall died naturally. The article repeatedly acknowledged the possibility that the accusation was false.  
**Assessment:** **Contested testimony and serious contemporary suspicion—not proof Bessels poisoned Hall.** Compare the earlier Tyson account (HN-222); medical, forensic and naval records must be examined before deciding causation. **Dispute documented; conclusion open.**

**HN-225 — November 6, 1873 — Sixty-eight indictments in alleged railroad-bond forgery**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, digest of October dispatches — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18731106.2.4).  
**Report:** Amid news of a severe financial panic, the paper reported that **sixty-eight indictments** had been returned against **A. T. Roberts** and **J. Gleason** for allegedly counterfeiting or forging bonds of the **New York Central** and **Buffalo & Erie** railroads.  
**Assessment:** **Reported grand-jury action, not proof of guilt or a final conviction**. The bond names and defendant initials should be checked against the indictments. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-226 — November 20, 1873 — Insurance-death deception ends in a murder conviction**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, retrospective news account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18731120.2.7).  
**Report:** A Pennsylvania jury had convicted **William E. Udderzook** of murdering **Winfield S. Goss**. According to the account, Goss had first been falsely represented as having died in a workshop fire, enabling a disputed **life-insurance claim**; subsequently he lived under an alias before being killed after threatening to expose the arrangement. The article described contradictions in dental evidence offered after the supposed first death.  
**Assessment:** **A reported jury conviction in a highly unusual alleged insurance conspiracy.** The paper's prediction that Udderzook would soon be hanged is **not proof of any subsequent execution**. Complete trial and insurance records remain to be checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1874

**HN-227 — December 10, 1874 — Two medical students supposedly duel in a dark classroom**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, reprinting the *Louisville Courier-Journal* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18741210.2.5).  
**Report:** Two young men at the **Louisville Medical College**, allegedly rivals in a romance, arranged a pistol duel in a **completely darkened room**. After a first exchange missed both participants, a second left one slightly wounded in the arm. The paper said the pair reconciled afterward and the incident only later became known.  
**Assessment:** **A colorful reprinted incident, not a police or college disciplinary record.** Participants are unnamed and independent confirmation is absent. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-228 — December 18, 1874 — Mob lynches man just sentenced to life imprisonment**  
**Source:** *Jasper Republican*, p. 2, Associated Press dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPRP18741218.2.6).  
**Report:** **Charles Howard** was convicted in **Des Moines, Iowa**, of murdering **John Johnson** and sentenced to life imprisonment. Within days, masked men reportedly overpowered jail personnel, dragged Howard from his cell, and hanged him from a lamppost. His wife, described as separately indicted in the case, was present as the mob seized him.  
**Assessment:** **A contemporaneous account of lynching after legal sentencing**, not a legitimate execution. The report guessed at the mob's membership but said that point had not been established. **Follow-up open.**

### 1875

**HN-229 — March 12, 1875 — Houstonia tornado destroys buildings and scatters rail cars**  
**Source:** *Jasper Weekly Courier*, p. 6, reprinting a Sedalia, Missouri, eyewitness report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JWC18750312.1.6).  
**Report:** A tornado struck **Houstonia, Missouri**, shattering homes and businesses, seriously injuring several residents, and reportedly blowing an entire stationary train off the tracks. The account described emergency physicians arriving by special train and first-person descriptions of the storm's course.  
**Assessment:** **Substantive eyewitness-attributed disaster report**. The damage total and eventual deaths cannot be fixed from the immediate narrative. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-230 — March 23, 1875 — Cleveland powder mills destroyed in a series of blasts**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 3, quoting the *Cleveland Leader* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISST18750323.1.3).  
**Report:** Explosions at the **Austin Powder Works** near **Cleveland** destroyed many industrial buildings, shattered windows across the city and reportedly killed **three men**. The source estimated **forty to fifty tons of powder** in the affected facilities. It also noted that a smaller explosion had struck the same works in **March 1872**, making the recurrence a contemporary concern.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed industrial-disaster reportage, not a proven technical cause**. The powder quantity is a rough estimate and specific accident inquiries remain unreviewed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-231 — May 14, 1875 — Schiller wreck: early survivor reports and a newspaper geography error**  
**Source:** *Jasper Republican*, p. 2, local summary of the first reports — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPRP18750514.2.12); [Royal Museums Greenwich wreck record](https://www.rmg.co.uk/collections/objects/rmgc-object-1113370).  
**Report:** The passenger liner ***Schiller***, sailing from New York toward Hamburg, struck rocks in **fog** on May 7. The Indiana newspaper relayed a very low preliminary survivor count and mistakenly placed the Isles of Scilly off **southeastern** England. The Royal Museums Greenwich record confirms the wreck was at the **Western Rocks, Isles of Scilly**, off **southwestern** Britain.  
**Assessment:** **An authentic disaster report with a checkable geographic error**. Initial survivor and casualty totals should not be taken as final. **Location independently corrected.**

**HN-232 — November 12, 1875 — Construction-train locomotive boiler explodes in New Jersey**  
**Source:** *Jasper Republican*, p. 2, reprinting New York newspaper reports — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPRP18751112.2.4).  
**Report:** A locomotive identified as ***Centralia*** on the **Easton & Amboy Railroad** exploded while waiting on a siding near **South Somerville, New Jersey**. The blast crushed a car holding railroad workers. The paper reported **five killed**, **thirty-three scalded or injured**, and suggestions that a weakened boiler and lack of water might have contributed.  
**Assessment:** **Specific early railway-disaster account**, but the stated mechanical causes are allegations, not an official technical verdict. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-233 — December 24, 1875 — Mosel dynamite disaster: negligence theory contradicted by fraud-plot report**  
**Source:** *Jasper Republican*, p. 2, reprinting the *Chicago Tribune* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPRP18751224.2.9); [*The Spectator*, December 18, 1875](https://archive.spectator.co.uk/article/18th-december-1875/1/a-crime-the-like-of-which-the-world-has-probably).  
**Report:** A tremendous dynamite explosion beside the steamer ***Mosel*** at **Bremerhaven** destroyed a tug and killed many people on the crowded quay. The reprinted American account speculated about an improperly packed dynamite case and suspected a passenger who tried to shoot himself. But a **December 18 British report**, published before the Indiana reprint, already described a much more deliberate alleged scheme: **William King Thomson**, using the name **Thomassen**, supposedly intended a **timed bomb** to sink the liner at sea for inflated cargo-insurance proceeds.  
**Assessment:** **Major evidence against simply accepting the newspaper's negligent-handling narrative.** Contemporary reporting connected the blast to a planned insurance-fraud crime, but the precise device and perpetrator's admissions still require original investigative records. Initial death estimates differ. **Follow-up partly resolved; prosecution evidence open.**

### 1876

**HN-234 — February 17, 1876 — Boston loan scheme exposes a chain of forged endorsements**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union and Jasper Republican*, p. 2, reprinting the *Boston Globe* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJP18760217.2.7).  
**Report:** A man identified as **Winslow** reportedly forged multiple acquaintances' signatures on promissory notes, persuaded at least one victim to accept other forged notes in repayment, and obtained **$6,000** from the Maverick Bank using an endorsement purportedly from **E. D. Poland**. When Poland denied signing it, the bank president confronted Winslow, who reportedly produced repayment under supervision.  
**Assessment:** **Specific contemporary account of alleged serial forgery and a repaid bank advance.** A repayment described by the newspaper is not proof the other forged notes were valid or that criminal charges were resolved. Winslow's full name, independent records, and any prosecution remain **unverified**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-235 — March 23, 1876 — A farmer's supposed annually recurring convulsions puzzle physicians**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union and Jasper Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJP18760323.2.4).  
**Report:** An extraordinary reprinted account concerned **William Hutchison**, a farmer near Springfield in **Erie County, Pennsylvania**, who purportedly experienced violent, largely painless convulsions during a two-week period every year, beginning about **1858**. The reporter claimed sedatives and changes of climate failed to stop the attacks and that numerous doctors had witnessed them.  
**Assessment:** **An unusual nineteenth-century medical claim, not a verified diagnosis or paranormal phenomenon.** The writer repeatedly asserted its truth, but no original clinical case notes, page images establishing every figure, or independent follow-up have been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-236 — May 18, 1876 — Kansas vigilantes reportedly hang a bystander along with a suspected horse thief**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union and Jasper Republican*, p. 2, reprinting the *Kansas City Times* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJP18760518.2.56).  
**Report:** A reported vigilante party near **Fort Dodge, Kansas**, seized and hanged a young buffalo-bone collector named **Calahan** alongside a suspected horse thief after the suspect had taken shelter at Calahan's dugout. **Rev. Mr. Calahan of Topeka**, the young man's father, insisted his son had nothing to do with the theft. The editorial condemned the killings and identified a suspected leader, whose name is unstable in OCR.  
**Assessment:** **Newspaper allegation of a possible wrongful lynching**, with the innocence claim supported in this account principally by the bereaved father's investigation and statements. Do not treat that claim, the mob's membership, or guilt of either victim as independently adjudicated. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-237 — September 7, 1876 — Detectives foil a bank raid through a months-long underground tunnel**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, reproducing and comparing Chicago newspaper accounts — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18760907.2.23).  
**Report:** Burglars seeking the vault of **McCoy & Thompson's bank in Rensselaer, Indiana**, had allegedly worked for months on a concealed tunnel. Detectives led by **William Turtle** and **Captain Bull** watched the preparations, recovered elaborate tools, and arrested men identified as **James Filkins and Martin Davis**. The newspaper also described the suspected involvement and flight after bail of local saloon proprietor **M. V. B. Warner**; reported tunnel duration varies between versions.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed account of a thwarted heist, not a successful loss of the vault's money.** Much of the narrative relies on the participating detectives' version of events; final dispositions and Warner's fate were not checked. **Follow-up open.**

### 1877

**HN-238 — March 28, 1877 — A thief uses a wrapped box to reach $25,000 over a bank counter**  
**Source:** *Indiana State Sentinel*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=ISST18770328.1.3).  
**Report:** In a daylight robbery at the **Indiana National Bank**, at Washington and Meridian Streets in **Indianapolis**, a man reportedly positioned a wrapped **orange box** at the counter, climbed onto it, grabbed packages of currency, and escaped while officials and passersby reacted. The report put the initial haul at **$25,000** and said two packages of **$500 each** were dropped inside the bank. Two other men were arrested on suspicion of diverting attention, but the thief's identity remained unsettled.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary account with an unusually specific mechanism**, but the reported amount, suggested accomplices, and police suspicions are not final findings. The article appears to reuse earlier dispatch language, so its publication date should not be read as the exact robbery date. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-239 — March 29, 1877 — Newspaper mistakenly publishes a second death notice for sea captain Mary Patten**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, reprinting the *Brooklyn Union and Argus* — [1877 original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18770329.2.22); *The Civilian and Gazette* (Galveston), April 9, **1861**, p. 1 — [earlier original obituary](https://texashistory.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metapth177494/m1/1/).  
**Report:** The 1877 article stated that **Mary Patten**, celebrated for taking command of the clipper ***Neptune's Car*** when its captain, her husband, became incapacitated in **1856**, had just died aged about **41**. Yet the **1861** Texas newspaper had already published a death notice for the same **Mary Ann Patten**, widow of Captain Joshua Patten, explicitly recalling the ***Neptune's Car*** rescue and reporting her death at roughly **23**.  
**Assessment:** **A directly demonstrable chronological contradiction between two original newspaper reports.** The later story repeats distinctive identifying facts about the ship and captain; it cannot be accepted as an accurate first death report. The cause of the 1877 republication error is **unknown**, not evidence of deception. **Correction established; error mechanism open.**

**HN-240 — September 14, 1877 — Sealed bank remittance allegedly arrives stuffed with paper**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, reproducing a Mount Vernon, Illinois correspondent to the *St. Louis Republican* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18770914.2.24).  
**Report:** The **Mount Vernon National Bank** sued **Adams Express Company** over an **$8,000** package bound for the **Third National Bank of St. Louis**. According to the article, bank officers had wrapped and sealed the money with unusual care before handing it to express agents in June; at the destination the package was opened to reveal **paper cut into banknote-sized pieces**, with some inner wrapping and twine altered but the outer addressing apparently intact.  
**Assessment:** **Reported lawsuit and alleged transit theft**, not proof of which employee, stranger, or stage of custody was responsible. The reporter expressly said the culprit was unknown; the court judgment and any recovered money remain **unverified**. **Follow-up open.**

### 1878

**HN-241 — March 21, 1878 — Four young women capture a groaning street “ghost”**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Union*, p. 2, reprinting the *Detroit Free Press* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLUN18780321.2.15).  
**Report:** After repeatedly encountering a white-clad, groaning figure while returning from work at dusk, four young women reportedly arranged an ambush. They caught the figure at an opening in a fence, removed his **sheet and paper hat**, and identified him as a local youth playing a prank. The article described the young women parading him through nearby streets in his improvised costume.  
**Assessment:** **A newspaper's explicit natural explanation of an alleged haunting**; the printed story does not support the existence of a ghost. Names and independent confirmation of the practical joke remain unavailable. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-242 — August 16, 1878 — Experiments connect flour dust, airborne oxygen, and mill explosions**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18780816.2.40).  
**Report:** Reporting research prompted by the deadly **Minneapolis flour-mill explosion**, the paper described **Professors Peck and Peckham** of the University of Minnesota blowing finely divided flour and mill dust through flame, comparing bran, middlings, and other grinding byproducts. It distinguished dust suspended in air from compact material and noted that a glowing wire and some sparks failed to ignite dust under their test conditions, while direct flame could.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary experimental attempt to explain a major industrial disaster**, not final proof of the exact ignition source in Minneapolis. This account cautions against reducing a complex dust explosion to a single untested spark theory. **Technical follow-up open.**

### 1879

**HN-243 — July 26, 1879 — Newspaper claims the mystery of the lost ship *Alaska* was solved by a mutineer's confession**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Standard*, p. 2, reprinting the *Victoria (British Columbia) Colonist* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLSD18790726.2.3).  
**Report:** The clipper ***Alaska*** reportedly vanished after sailing from **China toward Burrard Inlet**, where it was to receive lumber. Years later, according to a story relayed through a sailor met in a Japanese saloon, investigators heard of a **mutiny**, the murder of officers and supercargo **Mr. Thorndike**, theft of roughly **$5,000**, and the intentional burning of the ship. The report said several alleged participants had been arrested following a confession.  
**Assessment:** **A remarkable reported solution resting on a secondhand confession narrative.** Vessel registers, original arrest records, the chronology of the voyage, and the outcome of any prosecution were **not independently verified**; do not recast an attributed tale as a proven murder case. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-244 — November 7, 1879 — Senator Chandler found dead after a campaign speech; medical explanations differ**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18791107.2.3).  
**Report:** **U.S. Senator Zachariah Chandler** of Michigan reportedly retired after a long speech in Chicago on **October 31** and was found dead the next morning in his room at the **Grand Pacific Hotel**. The account first summarized doctors' view as heart disease, then quoted an examining consultation's written conclusion of **natural causes, probably cerebral hemorrhage**. A postmortem was reportedly not performed.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary example of medical uncertainty during sudden-death reporting.** The differing early formulations do not establish an unnatural death. The article's stated medical opinion is provisional; original coroner materials remain **unchecked**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-245 — November 7, 1879 — Acquitted murder defendant seized from his home and lynched**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 3, reproducing an October 30 Keokuk dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18791107.2.3).  
**Report:** **Bill Young**, reportedly **acquitted** in a Missouri case involving the murder of the **Spencer family**, was pursued home near **Luray, Missouri**, by a large armed crowd. After a siege in which he was shot multiple times, the mob hauled him out and hanged him. The article reported that Young maintained his innocence and that the vigilantes refused to spare him when he declined to confess; it estimated the mob in the hundreds and alleged some recognizable local participants.  
**Assessment:** **Reported fatal mob action overriding an acquittal**, not a judicial determination that the lynched man committed the underlying murders. Neither the identities of all participants nor subsequent accountability have been independently established. **Follow-up open.**

### 1880

**HN-246 — March 19, 1880 — Frankfort flaxseed mill explodes, killing its entire reported crew**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, reprinting a *Chicago Inter Ocean* special — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18800319.2.3).  
**Report:** A massive blast destroyed **Rosenthal & Co.'s flaxseed mill** at **Frankfort, Indiana**, on **March 11**. The paper named **ten workers** killed and described ruins subsequently burning; several workers were absent and a late-arriving timekeeper escaped. The report referred to a **boiler explosion**, noting reused boiler and engine equipment, but admitted the complete loss of the workers prevented a definitive determination of what happened.  
**Assessment:** **Specific workplace catastrophe and provisional mechanism**, not an independently verified final engineering inquiry. Casualty names are vulnerable to OCR misreading; check page image and local death records before reusing them. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-247 — July 2, 1880 — Accused poisoner released as inquiry turns toward an accident**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Indiana news roundup — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18800702.2.4).  
**Report:** A brief item reported that the **Box family poisoning case** in **New Albany, Indiana**, had ended, at least provisionally, with the **discharge from arrest of Moses Box**. The writer added that, if the family's illness truly came from **arsenic**, investigators now believed the exposure probably accidental.  
**Assessment:** **Direct contemporaneous correction to an accusation**, not confirmation that Moses Box intentionally poisoned anyone—or that arsenic was conclusively established. Underlying testimony and medical analyses were not inspected. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-248 — November 13, 1880 — Judge calls a damaging Garfield campaign letter a forgery**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1, reporting Judge Davis's ruling in New York — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN18801113-01.1.1).  
**Report:** In preliminary criminal-libel proceedings concerning the infamous ***Morey letter***—a purported statement by **James A. Garfield** about Chinese immigration—**Judge Davis** stated that the letter was **not written by Garfield** and that evidence for the supposed addressee **Henry L. Morey** was lacking. He sharply criticized the decision to publish it and ordered accused journalist **Kenward Philp** held to answer.  
**Assessment:** **A contemporaneous judicial finding that the document was forged**, not a verdict proving Philp personally produced it or assigning criminal guilt to every political figure who circulated it. The article explicitly left Philp's guilt to a jury. **Document finding established; individual culpability open.**

**HN-249 — December 3, 1880 — Report alleges wedding feast poisoned when arsenic was used as salt**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, citing a Cincinnati dispatch to the *Chicago Tribune* — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18801203.2.9).  
**Report:** At a wedding for **Joel Hembric** and **Jane Dale** in **Roane County, Tennessee**, guests became gravely ill after eating. The newspaper reported **six dead**, many others unconscious or endangered, and attributed the deaths to **arsenic reportedly kept in the kitchen to kill crows** and mistakenly used to season chicken. Several names and the groom's surname are especially prone to OCR variation.  
**Assessment:** **Grave alleged mass-poisoning accident with a specific initial cause theory**, not a verified toxicology report or a final casualty total. Do not presume sabotage—or accept the accidental-mistake explanation as officially determined—without later inquiry records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1881

**HN-250 — July 29, 1881 — Winston train robbery: two deaths and conflicting firsthand accounts**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, quoting express messenger **C. H. Murray** — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18810729.2.6); *Rensselaer Republican*, August 4, p. 2, eyewitness interviews — [second original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18810804.2.16).  
**Report:** Masked gunmen boarded a **Chicago, Rock Island and Pacific** train near **Winston, Missouri**, in July, killed conductor **William Westfall** and passenger **McMillan**, and robbed the express safe. The messenger's detailed version described being forced at gunpoint to unlock it; an interviewed passenger described the panicked rush through the train and disputed some claims about how the messenger was treated and the number of shots.  
**Assessment:** **Multiple contemporary witness-attributed accounts**, showing how immediate details diverge while the killings and robbery recur across reports. Do not treat every alleged gang identification or reconstruction as settled. **Connected later to the 1883 Frank James trial report, HN-259.**

**HN-251 — September 16, 1881 — Another Missouri train heist, with competing claims of being Jesse James**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18810916.2.6).  
**Report:** A **Chicago and Alton** passenger train near **Glendale, Missouri**, was stopped by masked gunmen who reportedly robbed express property and passengers, including women in a sleeping car. The newspaper said **two bandits each claimed to be Jesse James**; one claimed to hold the pistol used to kill conductor Westfall during the earlier Winston robbery. A brakeman's account described narrowly flagging down an approaching freight train, preventing a possible collision.  
**Assessment:** **Reported second train robbery and practical near-disaster**, but participants' self-identifications were **claims by masked attackers**, not independent identification. Reported amounts were estimates. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-252 — October 27, 1881 — Shooting of a Wisconsin judge reaches trial after a bitter public feud**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18811027.2.22).  
**Report:** The article described the opening of proceedings against bank official **H. B. Cochran**, accused of shooting **Judge Hayden** in **Centralia, Wisconsin**, in **1879**, amid allegations that the judge had had an affair with Cochran's wife and had attacked him in his newspaper. The report described the collapse of their former friendship, conflicting community factions, and repeated delays before trial; it also alleged retaliatory destruction of newspaper offices and property.  
**Assessment:** **Reporting on an actual court proceeding concerning an earlier killing**, heavily flavored by the period writer's account of scandal and alleged motives. The alleged affair, mob threats, and newspaper reprisals are not findings of the jury. No trial verdict was established from this page. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-253 — December 16, 1881 — Vienna Ringtheater fire: initial estimates exceed later official minimum**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18811216.2.18); City of Vienna municipal archive retrospective — [official context](https://presse.wien.gv.at/2011/12/06/ringtheaterbrand-am-8-dezember-1881).  
**Report:** A reprinted account of the **December 8 Ringtheater fire** in **Vienna** painted a harrowing picture of flames behind the stage curtain, blocked passageways, failing lights, and rescue attempts with blankets. It claimed **nearly 700 deaths** and offered competing theories involving an oil lamp or machinery associated with electric light. Vienna's own historical summary says fire broke out while **stage lighting was being ignited** and identifies **at least 386 people** who died.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary catastrophe reporting corrected with a named municipal record.** The paper's rough total is not the final official minimum, and its competing origin theories should not be treated as technical conclusions. **Core disaster confirmed; first-report figures contextualized.**

### 1882

**HN-254 — September 1, 1882 — Mob allegedly shoots two prisoners while they are being taken to court**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, brief national news summary — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18820901.2.3).  
**Report:** The newspaper said **Savage and James**, two Black defendants convicted of murder in **Madison, Florida**, but granted a **new trial**, were being escorted to the courthouse when a mob stopped the transport and **shot them to death**. It explicitly placed the deaths *after* the order for a new trial, not as a lawful execution.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report of extrajudicial killing of defendants awaiting renewed proceedings.** The brief item does not supply full names, case records, or identities of the killers; racialized period phrasing does not establish the defendants' guilt in the absence of a final adjudication. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-255 — September 7, 1882 — Egyptian sailors allegedly keelhauled after mutiny**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, international war news roundup — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18820907.2.39).  
**Report:** Amid dispatches about the **1882 British-Egyptian conflict**, one terse item alleged that **three Arab sailors** accused of mutiny aboard the Egyptian frigate ***Souda*** at **Alexandria** had been **keelhauled** and died soon afterward. The assertion appeared alongside many other fast-changing military dispatches without named witnesses or an inquiry.  
**Assessment:** **Uncorroborated contemporary allegation of a severe naval punishment**, not an established finding that the ship's officers carried it out or that every detail is accurate. The vessel's log and any diplomatic or naval inquiry remain **unchecked**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-256 — October 19, 1882 — Armed crowd storms Evansville jail and lynches accused murderer**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, dispatches from Evansville — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18821019.2.38).  
**Report:** An armed group from **Posey County, Indiana**, reportedly forced its way into the **Evansville jail** despite resistance from **Sheriff Kerth** and officers, then seized **Hume Redman**, accused of killing his wife at Mount Vernon. A gun battle followed; Redman was taken out and lynched, and **David Murphy**, identified as a relative of the deceased wife, was also killed. The report described attacks on doors, gunfire, alarms, and the officers' attempts to defend the prisoner.  
**Assessment:** **Detailed press account of mob violence against a jailed defendant**, not a court judgment legitimizing the killing. The first account's descriptions of culpability, individual actors, and casualty sequence merit comparison with inquest and trial records. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-257 — November 16, 1882 — Ocean liner collides with an unidentified vessel in darkness**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, London cablegram — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18821116.2.40).  
**Report:** The Hamburg-American liner ***Westphalia*** reached **Portsmouth** badly damaged after colliding with an **unidentified steamer near Beachy Head** in heavy seas and darkness. Its officers feared the other vessel had sunk with everyone aboard; a boat launched from the liner to search for survivors, said to carry **an officer and six men**, was also initially missing. The liner's passengers and mail were landed safely.  
**Assessment:** **Early maritime-disaster and disappearance report**, not proof that no one from the unknown ship or search boat was later found. Later casualty and vessel identifications remain **untraced**. **Follow-up open.**

### 1883

**HN-258 — September 11, 1883 — Woman tied to an infamous murder case completes a separate perjury term**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 8 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18830911.1.8).  
**Report:** The *Journal* anticipated the next day's release of **Nancy E. Clem** from the Indiana Female Prison, on the **fifteenth anniversary** of the 1868 **Cold Spring murders** that had made her notorious. The retrospective recounted earlier criminal proceedings, but explicitly said her most recent imprisonment was **four years for perjury** arising from later financial dealings, reduced by lawful good-time credit. A reporter spoke with prison staff; Clem declined an interview.  
**Assessment:** **Important distinction between notoriety from an old murder prosecution and a later, specific perjury sentence.** The notice predicted release rather than documenting her arrival home, and the long retrospective mixes reported facts and courtroom theories. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-259 — September 14, 1883 — Frank James acquitted in one trial while further indictments remain**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Gallatin, Missouri telegram — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18830914.2.9).  
**Report:** **Frank James** was acquitted in **Gallatin, Missouri**; the courtroom reaction was heated and several observers condemned the verdict. The report said other charges still awaited disposition, including allegations connected to conductor **Westfall's killing at Winston** and an earlier **Gallatin bank robbery**. The jury's verdict was reported alongside criticism of the prosecution evidence and claims, not established by the article, of jury interference.  
**Assessment:** **Verified contemporaneous report of an acquittal on the charge tried**, not exoneration on every other accusation. Its links to the **1881 Winston robbery, HN-250**, must not be collapsed into an assertion of a single definitive outcome for all charges. **Other dispositions open.**

**HN-260 — November 15, 1883 — Indiana mill dam repeatedly sabotaged with dynamite**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, local/regional account — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18831115.2.66).  
**Report:** Opponents of a mill dam on the **Monon River** near **Monon, Indiana**, reportedly blew a large hole in it at night after efforts to negotiate its removal failed. Following repairs and the hiring of guards, another blast opened a wider breach and broke the mill's windows; the newspaper described the proprietor being pushed into the river during an earlier watch. No responsible party was conclusively identified.  
**Assessment:** **Reported series of infrastructure-sabotage incidents arising from a local property and environmental dispute**, but motive and perpetrator identities were inferred by the writer, not determined in court. The paper's comic language about those involved is not itself evidence. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-261 — November 22, 1883 — Organized vigilantes lynch Jacob Nelling before trial**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, supplementing Indianapolis reporting — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18831122.2.42).  
**Report:** A reportedly **59-man** masked group seized **Jacob Nelling**, accused of murdering **Ada Atkinson**, from the **Fowler, Indiana**, jail, drove him toward **Oxford**, and hanged him from a roadside walnut tree near the crime scene. The article gave a striking account of the group's planning and of Nelling's words immediately before death, and attributed a qualified admission to a prior coroner conversation.  
**Assessment:** **Reported organized extrajudicial killing**, not a lawful punishment or substitute for a completed trial. The exact mob count, alleged admission, and identifiable organizers require independent investigation. The same paper elsewhere condemned the lynching; editorial labels of guilt should not erase the missing trial. **Follow-up open.**

### 1884

**HN-262 — February 1, 1884 — Crested Butte coal-mine blast entombs dozens; first totals conflict**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, dispatch from Gunnison, Colorado — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18840201.2.17).  
**Report:** An explosion at the **Colorado Coal and Iron Company** workings near **Crested Butte** wrecked the mine entrance and ventilation apparatus while a morning shift was underground. The article referred to **57 men trapped**, **six additional people killed**, and other estimates of the total, while residents and rescue parties tried to reopen the workings. Mine inspector **John McNeil** was quoted as fearing all the trapped men had died. A firedamp ignition was suspected; officials said ventilation and inspection had been in place.  
**Assessment:** **First disaster account, not a final casualty roster or conclusive cause report.** Its figures are not internally clean; do not add them into a purported final total. Official inspection and recovery lists require further research. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-263 — May 15–16, 1884 — Wall Street panic: bank reopens, then another major house fails**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, May 15, p. 1 — [first original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18840515.1.1); May 16, p. 1 — [next original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18840516.1.1).  
**Report:** The paper described a rush on New York banks and stock-exchange turmoil after the failure of **Grant & Ward** and **Marine National Bank**. **Metropolitan National Bank** suspended on May 14; clearing-house support encouraged confident predictions that the worst was over and Metropolitan would reopen. The next issue confirmed **Metropolitan's resumption**, yet reported a fresh shock when prominent firm **Fisk & Hatch** suspended later on May 15.  
**Assessment:** **An unusually clear day-by-day record of crisis, intervention, and premature optimism.** Resumption of one institution did not establish that the wider panic had fully ended; speculative explanations and predicted creditor recoveries remain attributed statements. **Immediate sequence corroborated in two issues.**

**HN-264 — October 23, 1884 — Suspected Wisconsin shooter lynched despite arrest**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, Western news roundup — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18841023.2.8).  
**Report:** During political procession preparations at **La Crosse, Wisconsin**, club president **F. A. Burton** was reportedly shot dead by a man nicknamed **“Scotty.”** After police jailed the suspected shooter, a crowd broke in and hanged him; the **first rope broke**, after which a second hanging was carried out. The same item referred to a coroner's jury naming **persons unknown** in a related **Mitchell lynching** account.  
**Assessment:** **Reported killing followed by mob execution**, with names and precise sequence drawn from a brief summary. The assailant's full identity, coroner's phrasing, and any later prosecution need local verification. **Follow-up open.**

### 1885

**HN-265 — January 25, 1885 — Rare progressive ossification case reported in Morgan County**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 1, special from Martinsville — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18850125.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper described the recent death of **Hannah Stout**, daughter of postmaster **John Stout** near **Martinsville, Indiana**, after years of progressive rigidity that allegedly fixed her joints and spine and turned some soft tissues into bone-like material. It rejected rumors that her body had become extraordinarily heavy and reported that her family guarded the grave against unwanted medical curiosity.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary description of an exceptional medical condition**, not evidence she literally “turned into stone” or a retrospective clinical diagnosis. The page's chronology of onset and confinement is **internally inconsistent in OCR**; the physician's records and age require verification. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-266 — January 25, 1885 — Bomb blasts damage British Parliament and Tower; early arrests collapse**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 1, January 24 London telegrams — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18850125.1.1).  
**Report:** Successive blasts damaged areas of **Westminster Palace** and the **Tower of London**, injuring people including visitors and children and alarming the city. The same continuously updated news report initially described hurried arrests after witnesses saw a suspicious cab and people fleeing, then explicitly stated that **all people arrested on suspicion that afternoon had been released** for lack of adequate evidence. It also corrected a first rumor that the **White Tower** was destroyed or badly damaged.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary terror-attack reporting with corrections already embedded in its first edition**. The initial detentions were **not convictions**, and initial rumors about destruction should not survive their corrections. The actual perpetrators and later trials need separate documentation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-267 — July 15, 1885 — Jail defenders thwart an attempted lynching in Marion, Indiana**  
**Source:** *Evening Star* (Franklin, Indiana), p. 1, telegraphic report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKES18850715.1.1).  
**Report:** A short dispatch from **Marion, Indiana**, reported that an armed mob tried to enter the jail to seize a **Black prisoner identified only as Wallace**. The attempt failed after a member of the attacking group was shot; the writer warned of possible renewed violence. No underlying charge against Wallace was supplied.  
**Assessment:** **Reported unsuccessful lynching attempt**, not proof of the man's guilt, a successful escape, or a permanent end to the danger. The contemporaneous report offers no complete identity or court file for Wallace. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-268 — July 16, 1885 — Munster Bank closure sparks crowds and fears of Irish banking riots**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 1, July 15 dispatch from Cork — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18850716.1.1).  
**Report:** The failure of the **Munster Bank** drew distressed depositors to branches in **Cork, Dublin, and Limerick**, with police guarding the premises amid fears of riots. The report identified **£9,000** of municipal funds deposited in the bank, said about **200 employees** faced unemployment, and described widespread business disruption. Angry customers blamed former chairman **William Shaw**, but the article gave no adjudicated finding of wrongdoing by him.  
**Assessment:** **Firsthand-attributed banking-crisis dispatch**, not a final liquidation account, proof of a riot, or evidence that a named director committed fraud. The bank's subsequent disposition and depositor recoveries require later records. **Follow-up open.**

### 1886

**HN-269 — March 19, 1886 — Three explosions strike the Uniondale coal mine**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Connellsville, Pennsylvania dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18860319.2.18).  
**Report:** The Uniondale mine near Dunbar was struck by successive firedamp explosions. The dispatch first reported **two people dead** and **twelve badly injured**, with rescuers repeatedly turned back by flames. A blast at the adjoining workings reportedly knocked miners off their feet. An unnamed local opinion held that employing a fire boss might have prevented the disaster.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary disaster dispatch, not a completed casualty or engineering inquiry.** The proposed prevention measure is an attributed opinion, not a demonstrated cause. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-270 — April 29, 1886 — Indiana tax collector disappears, with competing theories**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, state-news roundup — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18860429.2.61).  
**Report:** **Townsend Wilson**, described as a Greencastle tax collector, reportedly left his home on April 15 and had not been seen. The account mentioned his wife and five children, business difficulties, and speculation that he had deliberately disappeared or taken his own life. No body, farewell note, witness to a death, or proof of financial wrongdoing was given.  
**Assessment:** **Verified disappearance report but unresolved fate and cause**; the reporter's suppositions must not be repeated as facts. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-271 — April 29, 1886 — Doctor accused of enlarging old debts by altering notes**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, same state-news roundup — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18860429.2.61).  
**Report:** A Carroll County judge issued a bench warrant for physician **William Cooper** of Kokomo on a **forgery charge**. The allegation was that notes from his former business had been raised in value—for example, from **$7 to $70** and **$20 to $120**—before collection suits. He reportedly posted **$1,000 bond**.  
**Assessment:** **Arrest and allegations reported, not a conviction.** Original notes and court disposition have not been checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-272 — October 21, 1886 — Missing brewery driver becomes a contested witness in the Haddock murder**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, Sioux City, Iowa special — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18861021.2.43).  
**Report:** After the shooting of minister **George C. Haddock**, suspicion fell on brewer **John Arensdorf**. A brewery driver, **Henry Peters**, had disappeared, prompting claims that he too had been murdered. Neighbors reported a quarrel and groans; Arensdorf said Peters had left voluntarily after a disagreement over horse care. The same story conceded that a late report placed Peters at his former home in Davenport, and rival witness accounts conflicted about the shooter.  
**Assessment:** **A record of rapidly multiplying and contested allegations**, not proof that Peters was killed or that a named suspect fired the shot. **Follow-up open.**

### 1887

**HN-273 — March 18, 1887 — Crowded train falls through Boston's Bussey Bridge**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Boston special — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18870318.2.15).  
**Report:** A Boston & Providence commuter train plunged through the **Bussey Bridge** between Forest Hills and Roslindale. Several packed coaches dropped into the roadway; neighbors and firemen hurried to rescue passengers. The headline declared **30 dead**, while the article's body counted **at least 25** and acknowledged that the injuries and fatalities could not yet be settled. It assigned possible responsibility to defects in ironwork before describing a completed structural investigation.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporaneous collapse and rescue reporting with conflicting early totals and a preliminary cause theory.** Final findings and the roster need the official investigation. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-274 — April 14, 1887 — A 33-year-old forgery indictment resurfaces against a California businessman**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18870414.2.44).  
**Report:** Lawyers for California resident **William K. Rogers**, also identified as **William Kissane**, sought to quash a New York **1854 forgery indictment** involving the Chemical Bank. The retrospective recalled his earlier, separate prosecution relating to the burning of the steamer *Martha Washington*, reporting that he had **been acquitted**. It also recycled an extraordinary, unverified allegation that agents had poisoned a private investigator years earlier.  
**Assessment:** **The 1887 news is the revived legal proceeding**, not a new 1887 arson or poisoning. Previous acquittals cannot be converted into guilt, and the investigator-poisoning story is an allegation of doubtful provenance. **Motion's disposition open.**

### 1888

**HN-275 — March 15, 1888 — Local thieves abandon a Rensselaer bank break-in**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, local report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18880315.2.30).  
**Report:** Night watchman **Myers** saw one person at a window of **McCoy & Co.'s bank** and another climbing out; both fled. The watchman woke neighboring shopkeepers, and the bank found **no known money missing**. The abandoned implements were two large nails and pieces of scrap iron, leading the writer to mock the burglars as amateurs unlikely to penetrate the vault.  
**Assessment:** **Specific local attempted-entry account**, not proof of the suspects' identities or a successful cash robbery. The paper's comic judgments are editorial. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-276 — July 27, 1888 — Three Chicago men accused of plotting to bomb Haymarket officials**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Chicago special — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18880727.2.37).  
**Report:** After alleged dynamite threats against **Grinnell, Gary, and Bonfield**, suspects **Hronek** and **Chapek** denied involvement when arraigned. The justice **continued the case ten days**. Hronek admitted possessing explosives left by an acquaintance but disputed the asserted murder plan; the report called **Chleboun** an informer and noted that another initial arrestee had already been discharged.  
**Assessment:** **Arraignment, police theory, and defendants' denials**, not proof of a plot or completed conviction. The report contains charged language associated with the aftermath of Haymarket. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-277 — November 15, 1888 — Frontenac mine toll first inflated, then sharply revised**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18881115.2.11); [Kansas Historical Society account](https://www.kansashistory.gov/kansapedia/coal-mining-in-southeast-kansas/19849).  
**Report:** The first dispatch on the **Frontenac, Kansas, Mine No. 2 explosion** imagined **160 miners dead**, effectively all but two men thought to be underground. Later on the **same original page**, the newspaper revised the fatality count to **37**. The Kansas Historical Society records **44 men and boys killed** in the November 9 disaster and notes subsequent safety reforms.  
**Assessment:** **A documented reporting cascade, not three competing equally authoritative final totals:** initial 160 → contemporary correction 37 → Kansas historical account 44. **Later casualty correction corroborated.**

**HN-278 — November 15, 1888 — Rochester factory fire and reports of desperate window escapes**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, same multi-disaster article — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18881115.2.11).  
**Report:** A fire at Rochester, New York's **Steam Gauge and Lantern Company** trapped workers on upper floors. The paper described people leaping from windows, and at least one man trying to reach the river near the falls. Its running account first identified **five immediate deaths**, then added that **33 people** were known to have died. The cause of the fire was said to be unknown.  
**Assessment:** **A single evolving fire report with different reporting stages**, not separate events or a verified final casualty inquiry. Original Rochester fire and coroner records remain to be compared. **Follow-up open.**

### 1889

**HN-279 — April 4, 1889 — Bank robber extorts $21,000 with a pistol and fake explosive**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, Denver report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18890404.2.43).  
**Report:** A stranger persuaded **First National Bank of Denver** president **David Moffat** to discuss a purported threat to the bank, then drew a pistol and demanded **$21,000**. He compelled Moffat to sign and cash a check under threat of detonating a bottle presented as nitroglycerine. The robber walked away. The newspaper's update says the bottle was recovered and actually held **castor oil**.  
**Assessment:** **Reported daylight armed extortion and prompt falsification of the claimed explosive**, not a verified account of the robber's capture. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-280 — August 16, 1889 — Spokane's business district burns as officials dynamite buildings**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Spokane Falls dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18890816.2.17).  
**Report:** Fire swept **Spokane Falls, Washington Territory**, burning much of the business district to the river. Firefighters reportedly lacked adequate water and ordered a dozen buildings demolished with explosives to form firebreaks, without stopping the fire. The same printed report gave incompatible starting times and losses ranging from **$11 million** to conjectures of **$30 million**, then called the highest estimate exaggerated.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary disaster report with explicitly unstable times, death reports, and property-loss estimates.** The rebuilding and final damage audit need independent review. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-281 — October 31, 1889 — Prayer service accompanies an extrajudicial execution**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18891031.2.32).  
**Report:** A mob reportedly took **Robert Berrier**, accused of murdering his mother-in-law, from a jail near **Lexington, North Carolina**. Instead of a trial, the crowd held prayers for hours, joined by a visiting salesman from New York, then hanged the prisoner while a hymn was sung.  
**Assessment:** **A newspaper account of a lynching**, not a judicial punishment; the accusation against Berrier remains an accusation, and the extraordinary ceremonial details require local-source comparison. **Follow-up open.**

### 1890

**HN-282 — January 10, 1890 — Eight Black prisoners killed by a mob in Barnwell**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Barnwell and Charleston, South Carolina dispatches — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18900110.2.15).  
**Report:** A disguised crowd overwhelmed jail staff in **Barnwell, South Carolina**, seized **eight Black men**, and shot them dead outside town. The account referred to a previous killing as the pretext for collective vengeance; its own Charleston follow-up stated the broader community denounced the violence and feared further reprisals.  
**Assessment:** **Reported mass lynching rather than the lawful disposition of charges.** The newspaper's categorical declaration of the prisoners' guilt is not a trial record; the eight individuals' identities require further work. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-283 — July 18, 1890 — Steamship *Tioga* blast: rival boiler and cargo theories**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Chicago dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18900718.2.14).  
**Report:** An explosion tore through the cargo steamer ***Tioga*** at a Chicago River dock, with **17 deaths** and about as many injured reported at first. Some witnesses blamed a boiler failure; others suggested that **naphtha fumes**, perhaps reaching lamps in the hold, ignited the blast. The first rescuers recovered bodies in conditions that made identification difficult.  
**Assessment:** **Disaster dispatch preserving competing mechanical explanations**, not a completed finding that either boilers or cargo were at fault. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-284 — October 3, 1890 — Passenger express collides with derailed coal cars in Pennsylvania**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Reading, Pennsylvania report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18901003.2.12).  
**Report:** A freight train struck a coal train near **Shoemakersville**, throwing cars across the adjoining track. Before a warning reached the **Pottsville express**, it hit the obstruction and plunged down an embankment near the Schuylkill River. The headline announced **35 deaths**, yet the dispatch said **15 bodies had been recovered** at one reporting hour and that the passenger total was uncertain.  
**Assessment:** **Well-described collision sequence with a provisional death estimate**, not a final list of people killed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-285 — December 26, 1890 — Austrian banker's supposed execution produces a grotesque financial rumor**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18901226.2.47).  
**Report:** A short report from **Bijeljina, Bosnia**, claimed residents had heard that banker **Baron Albert Rothschild** faced execution unless someone volunteered to take his place for **one million florins**. Applicants allegedly formed a pool to divide the reward and draw lots for the sacrificial participant. Crucially, the article said the **local authorities were trying to convince applicants the entire rumor was false**.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report about the spread of an implausible false rumor**, not an actual death sentence or a genuine market for substitute executions. **Follow-up open on origin, not on the supposed execution.**

### 1891

**HN-286 — February 5, 1891 — Mammoth mine disaster: shifting counts and a later federal correction**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18910205.2.35); [NIOSH technical history, p. 6](https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/11497/cdc_11497_DS1.pdf).  
**Report:** After the **January 27 Mammoth No. 1 mine** explosion in Pennsylvania, the newspaper's headline counted **130 deaths**, its first body text stated **110**, and a later passage asserted **151**. It described lethal afterdamp, recovery efforts, and competing claims about inspection and ventilation. A federal mine-safety technical review citing historical Bureau of Mines research records **109 miners killed** and associates the gas ignition with a roof fall.  
**Assessment:** **Three inconsistent contemporary figures superseded by a better-documented federal historical count of 109**, not proof of misreporting by any named worker. **Corroborated count and historical mechanism; individual accountability not established.**

**HN-287 — April 30, 1891 — Rome shaken by a powder-magazine explosion**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18910430.2.34).  
**Report:** An explosion said to involve **150 tons of gunpowder** on April 23 shook Rome, shattered windows, damaged buildings, and reportedly collapsed part of a parliamentary cupola. The dispatch recorded approximately **120 civilians taken to hospitals**, two seriously wounded officers, and **seven people known dead** at that point. It reported that King Umberto helped transport injured residents.  
**Assessment:** **First disaster report, including dramatic damage claims and an evolving fatality count**. Official Italian military and civic documentation has not been compared. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-288 — September 10, 1891 — Michigan dynamite works disappears in an explosion**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18910910.2.41).  
**Report:** A dynamite manufacturing plant belonging to **F. A. Reynolds & Co.**, near **White Pigeon, Michigan**, exploded on September 4. The article estimated **20 tons of dynamite** on site and **16 workers dead**, while alleging payroll records were destroyed, delaying identification. A church spire and nearby buildings were reported damaged by the shock.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary industrial-disaster report**, not a verified final death list, precise explosive inventory, or cause determination. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-289 — December 4, 1891 — Toledo rail collision scalds passengers when steam escapes**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18911204.2.16).  
**Report:** A **Flint & Pere Marquette** train ran into the rear of a **Lake Shore** express near a tunnel close to the Toledo Union Depot. The first report identified **six people killed** and **16 seriously injured**, many suffering from escaping steam rather than fire. The paper described how delays placed both trains close together and a flagman's warning reportedly came too late.  
**Assessment:** **Specific early reconstruction of the collision**, but assigning final operational fault requires the railroad and coroner investigations. **Follow-up open.**

### 1892

**HN-290 — May 20, 1892 — Roslyn mine disaster: company conjecture versus inquest finding**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18920520.2.20); [HistoryLink investigation and victim list](https://www.historylink.org/File/8016).
