**Report:** The newspaper placed the **May 10 Roslyn, Washington**, mine fatalities at **at least 42**, with other estimates of **45 to 50**, while one manager said the exact ignition source might never be known. Later historical research based on mining reports and the coroner's jury confirms **45 workers died**. The inquiry found an explosion involving gas and **inadequate ventilation**; company and inspector theories differed over the exact ignition mechanism.  
**Assessment:** **Later fatality tally corroborated (45)** and a documented investigative finding narrows the early managerial uncertainty. Details of the precise ignition sequence remain contested in the historical record. **Follow-up partly resolved.**

**HN-291 — October 7, 1892 — Nebraska woman charged in three alleged family poisonings**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18921007.2.10).  
**Report:** **Mary Ann Armagost** of **David City, Nebraska**, was arrested amid allegations that she poisoned a former husband, her current mother-in-law, and a stepdaughter. After initial testing on the stepdaughter was complicated by embalming fluid, investigators reportedly exhumed two older remains and found arsenic. The coroner's jury attributed **two deaths** to poisoning by Armagost; a **preliminary criminal hearing** was still to occur.  
**Assessment:** **Distinct stages: accusation, chemical testing, coroner's conclusion, pending criminal examination.** No conviction or disposition is established by the page. **Follow-up open.**

### 1893

**HN-292 — January 6, 1893 — Frozen dynamite explodes near Long Island City homes**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2, December 29 New York dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18930106.2.28).  
**Report:** The **late-December 1892** blast occurred beside tenements near a new East River tunnel project. The story at first alleged workers put frozen dynamite beside a fire, then quoted foreman **McIntre** claiming cartridges were placed in a **steam-heated box**; he could not remember the explosion. **Five deaths** were confirmed in the first account, with more feared. McIntre was said to be arrested on a criminal-negligence charge.  
**Assessment:** **Published in 1893 about an event in 1892**, with conflicting thawing accounts and uncertain final fatalities. Arrest does not establish negligence in court. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-293 — January 26, 1893 — Homestead labor leader convicted in poisoning case**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18930126.2.41).  
**Report:** In Pittsburgh, **Hugh Dempsey**, a Knights of Labor district leader, was found **guilty** by a jury of a charge related to the poisoning of non-union workers at **Homestead's mills**. The article said the jury had deliberated about **three hours** and noted that Dempsey was released under a substantial bond pending further steps.  
**Assessment:** **Reported jury verdict in a specified proceeding**, not a verified final sentence, appeal outcome, or finding implicating an entire labor organization. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-294 — September 21, 1893 — Dynamite used in a spectacular Indiana express-train robbery**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18930921.2.16).  
**Report:** Armed robbers stopped a **Lake Shore express** near **Kendallville, Indiana**, shot and injured the engineer, blasted an express car and its safe, and escaped. The article cites sharply conflicting witness accounts: one rider guessed **20–25 robbers**, whereas the express superintendent said **no more than six**. Rumored losses of **$300,000** were disputed by the company's estimate of **no more than $20,000**. Passengers were not themselves robbed in the account.  
**Assessment:** **The robbery is reported; gang size and haul are explicitly contested.** No identities or convictions established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-295 — September 15, 1893 — Lynching account misplaces Quincy in Massachusetts, ignoring later victims**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18930915.2.7); [Ida B. Wells, *A Red Record* (1895), ch. III](https://blackfreedom.proquest.com/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/ida2.pdf).  
**Report:** The Indiana paper reported a mob hanged **Benjamin Jackson**, accused of poisoning a family's well, and datelined the event **“Quincy, Mass.”** Journalist **Ida B. Wells** independently documented the episode in ***Quincy, Mississippi*** and described subsequent mob killings of **Mahala Jackson, Lou Carter, and Rufus Bigley**; she wrote that the women had even been declared innocent by an inquest before being murdered. The two texts also disagree on the episode's year, and Wells's summary count is internally inconsistent with the individual names given.  
**Assessment:** **Substantive correction of geography and of a single-victim narrative**, with the event-year and exact victim count still requiring primary local research. The poisoning suspicion was **not proof of guilt**. **Follow-up partly resolved.**

**HN-296 — November 10, 1893 — Hidden explosives destroy the *Cabo Machichaco* and Santander's quay**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2, Spanish disaster dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18931110.2.7); [Santander tourism authority's Machichaco Museum](https://turismo.santander.es/en/museo-machichaco).  
**Report:** On **November 3**, fire aboard ***Cabo Machichaco*** in Santander spread through cargo that included undeclared dynamite, causing a catastrophic blast along a crowded quay. The article alleged substantial unreported explosives and gave wildly different fatality/injury estimates, ranging from **at least 500 people killed** to much higher combined figures. The city maintains a museum dedicated to the event and preserves contemporary artifacts and documents.  
**Assessment:** **Historical catastrophe confirmed**, but do not treat the article's largest headline total or legal accusations against dead crew as adjudicated findings. **Final tally and technical inquiry open.**

**HN-297 — November 17, 1893 — A missing Wisconsin farmer found buried beneath a fallen tree**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2, Wausau, Wisconsin telegram — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18931117.2.33).  
**Report:** Neighbors searching near **Edgar, Wisconsin**, reportedly found the body of missing farmer **William Habeck** in a shallow, disguised grave under brush and a fallen tree. The report attributed an ax killing to his **19-year-old son Herman**, who was said to have confessed, with **17-year-old Otto** allegedly helping conceal the body. Both were arrested.  
**Assessment:** **Report of recovery, an attributed confession, and arrests**, not confirmation of a court-tested confession or final verdict. **Follow-up open.**

### 1894

**HN-298 — March 22, 1894 — Hundreds fall ill after a Boston wedding banquet**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18940322.2.26).  
**Report:** After wedding celebrations for **Henry Feinberg and Ida Margolies** in Boston, approximately **300 of 800 guests** reportedly developed symptoms of food poisoning. Speculation centered on food prepared in **old copper boilers**, with poultry, giblets, and pickled tongue mentioned as dishes served. Those involved disagreed over whether raw food or cooking equipment was to blame.  
**Assessment:** **Reported outbreak and contemporary cause hypotheses, not a verified toxicology result.** No food sample analysis or official investigation reviewed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-299 — May 17, 1894 — Two men named McKinley lynched in Kansas after a disputed accusation**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18940517.2.57).  
**Report:** A Kansas mob near **Sharon Springs** reportedly hanged **William McKinley and his adult son Lewis** after another son, **Fred**, was accused of killing relative **Charles Carley**. The younger son was said to have pleaded guilty and implicated his father and brother; **William and Lewis pleaded not guilty**, and the mob seized them before trial. The newspaper expressly noted their names had **no connection to the Ohio politician** of the same surname.  
**Assessment:** **Reported extrajudicial killings**, and the son's alleged confession is not proof that the two men the mob killed were culpable. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-300 — June 21, 1894 — Erastus Wiman found guilty of forgery; conviction later reversed**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18940621.2.14); [Society for American Baseball Research biography](https://sabr.org/bioproj/person/erastus-wiman/).  
**Report:** Investor **Erastus Wiman** was convicted in New York of **second-degree forgery**, after a jury deliberated roughly two hours and recommended mercy. The newspaper said sentence had not yet been imposed. A later biographical account records that **the conviction was reversed on appeal**, a fact omitted by accounts stopping at this June verdict.  
**Assessment:** **First-instance guilty verdict with verified subsequent appellate reversal**; it would be false to present the 1894 result as a standing final conviction. **Later outcome partly corroborated.**

**HN-301 — July 27, 1894 — Exploding artillery caisson devastates a Chicago military parade**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18940727.2.7).  
**Report:** An ammunition caisson exploded during a **U.S. Army drill procession in Chicago**, scattering shrapnel, killing **four soldiers** and reportedly **nine horses**, and injuring at least **17 other people**. The report suggested that a loose or defective shell cap *might* have caused the accident, but admitted no mechanism had been established.  
**Assessment:** **Reported military accident with an unconfirmed mechanical hypothesis**, not sabotage or an identified negligent person. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-302 — August 24, 1894 — Nebraska passenger-train plunge prompts an immediate sabotage accusation**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2, August 18 Nebraska dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18940824.2.6).  
**Report:** A **Rock Island** accommodation train fell from a trestle near **Lincoln, Nebraska**, and burning wreckage trapped passengers; the article reported **24 bodies recovered**. Officers arrested **George Davis**, describing circumstantial sightings of a crowbar near the track, and suggested that sabotage had caused the crash. In the same article, an unrelated **Santa Fe** head-on collision was reported separately with **two deaths**.  
**Assessment:** **Two different accidents on one page; no assumption that the first was deliberate or Davis guilty** without subsequent proceedings. Initial casualty counts still need reconciliation. **Follow-up open.**

### 1895

**HN-303 — August 9, 1895 — H. H. Holmes claims a supposed insurance victim killed himself**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18950809.2.26).  
**Report:** **H. H. Holmes**, under investigation for the death of **Benjamin Pitezel**, told police that Pitezel died by suicide and that he disguised the body to support an insurance claim. Investigators, however, suspected murder; reports from Holmes's Chicago property described bones, tanks, and alleged accomplices, but the text also conceded those remains were **not yet proof of homicide**. Police investigated a jailed man with multiple aliases who reportedly offered information in exchange for freedom.  
**Assessment:** **A suspect's self-serving narrative and provisional police case as reported in August**, not a verified account of what occurred or the eventual judicial outcome. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-304 — August 15, 1895 — Two sailors sentenced in the *Hesperia* mate-killing case**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2, news item within a larger report — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18950815.2.58).  
**Report:** A brief San Francisco item said **Hans Hanson** and **Thomas St. Clair** had killed the mate, **Maurice Fitzgerald**, aboard the bark ***Hesperia*** as an initial act of mutiny. Hanson was sentenced to be hanged **October 18**; the same execution date was predicted for St. Clair.  
**Assessment:** **Contemporary report of sentences and an alleged mutiny motive**, not verification that either execution actually occurred on the announced date. Trial and appellate records remain unexamined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-305 — September 12, 1895 — Fatal caisson blast interrupts Louisville military gathering**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2, Louisville dispatch — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18950912.2.33).  
**Report:** During gatherings connected with **Grand Army of the Republic veterans**, an artillery caisson of the Louisville Legion exploded before a planned salute. The headline said **four killed**, but the OCR of the immediate victim list names only **three**, including a Black soldier, **William Adams**, while other soldiers were injured. Speculation that someone had been smoking was directly disputed by the commanding captain.  
**Assessment:** **Confirmed contemporary report of a fatal military accident** with an internally incomplete victim roster; do not fix the figure or assign a smoking-related cause from this page alone. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-306 — September 13, 1895 — Family killed at an Iowa dynamite storehouse while a child target-shoots**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18950913.2.10).  
**Report:** A dynamite store at **Specht's Ferry**, near Dubuque, Iowa, exploded, killing **five people**, including stonework subcontractor **Edward Latshaw**, his wife, and two children. The report described the oldest boy practicing shooting at a nearby post, but explicitly said investigators did not know whether a bullet struck the explosive or firing vibrations triggered it.  
**Assessment:** **The deaths are reported; the proposed causal connection to the child's shooting is unresolved.** It would be unwarranted to blame him on this account. **Follow-up open.**

### 1896

**HN-307 — February 20, 1896 — The Vulcan mine blast leaves rescuers battling toxic gas**  
**Source:** *People's Pilot*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PLPL18960220.2.29); [Colorado mine inspector's contemporary report](https://spl.cde.state.co.us/artemis/nrserials/nr930010internet/nr930010189596internet.pdf).  
**Report:** A February 18 explosion at the **Vulcan coal mine near Newcastle, Colorado**, was said to have trapped and killed **55 miners**. The blast wrecked ventilation equipment and buried parts of the passageway; a miner thrown near the entrance was gravely injured and died. Rescue workers tried alternate shafts and makeshift ventilation while the newspaper speculated that a gas pocket associated with underground fires was responsible.  
**Assessment:** The casualty figure and suggested mechanism are the paper's initial account; the cited state inspector's report is a route to the official investigation, not evidence that the newspaper's theory was confirmed. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-308 — August 7, 1896 — A prison-farm escape sparks a second confrontation inside Leavenworth**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18960807.2.26).  
**Report:** The newspaper said roughly **30 federal prisoners** working outside Fort Leavenworth broke toward a cornfield after a signal attributed to **George East**. Guards fired; East was reported mortally wounded and **Sam Mills** and **S. Dove** seriously injured. Their return to a penitentiary yard containing some 200 prisoners prompted a second, short-lived disturbance that Warden French reportedly quieted before another volley.  
**Assessment:** A report of an attempted mass escape, not proof East died of his wounds or that every named prisoner played the role assigned by the correspondent. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-309 — December 17, 1896 — Newspaper retracts its own claim that a missing girl was found**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18961217.2.6).  
**Report:** After reporting that **Emma DeKoker** of DeMotte had been located at a Chicago hospital and transferred to an Illinois asylum, the paper explicitly **withdrew that claim**. It said she disappeared November 12 while staying with relatives near Pullman after setting out to obtain medicine for a burned arm. Relatives and Chicago police had found no trace. The article speculated about her mental health and possible accident.  
**Assessment:** The important discovery is a **contemporary retraction**: she remained missing as of publication. The article provides **no basis to diagnose illness, declare her dead, or infer a crime**. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-310 — December 31, 1896 — Princeton, Indiana, mine explodes during recovery from an earlier blast**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLRP18961231.2.24).  
**Report:** At the **Maule Coal Company mine** in Princeton, workers were clearing debris from an earlier nonfatal explosion when a second gas blast killed **six men** and injured at least eight. Among the named dead was the company's superintendent **Robert Maule**. The report describes damaged cages, an improvised rescue bucket, and miners initially feared dead who were brought out alive. The state mine inspector was expected to investigate.  
**Assessment:** The newspaper itself distinguished **presumed fatalities from rescues**. Further deaths among the wounded, and the explosion's cause, are not established here. **Follow-up open.**

### 1897

**HN-311 — September 17, 1897 — Five suspects lynched at Versailles; editors condemn mob murder while exposing sectional double standards**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN18970917-01.1.6); [Indiana historical study](https://www.in.gov/history/files/Guenther-S.-Vigilantism-in-Versailles-Examining-the-1897-Lynchings-and-Rural-Perceptions-of-Justice.pdf).  
**Report:** A collection of reprinted editorials discusses a mob that seized **five men jailed for alleged burglary** at **Versailles, Ripley County, Indiana**, and killed them on September 14. Some editors insisted there could be no justification for killing prisoners without trial; others used the crime to advance defensive or racist arguments about Southern lynchings. A contemporaneous editorial described the men as accused of relatively minor property crimes and emphasized the absence of convictions.  
**Assessment:** **Five extrajudicial killings, not court-imposed punishment.** The page comprises divergent **editorial reactions** rather than neutral proof of the original allegations against the men. **Follow-up open** on prosecutions of the mob. 

**HN-312 — November 2, 1897 — A missing Indiana real-estate dealer generates a Chicago police inquiry**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR18971102.2.12).  
**Report:** **George H. Brown**, formerly of Jasper County and later a dealer in **Knox, Indiana**, had vanished while traveling on business. His attorney **C. H. Peters** asked Chicago police to help, noting that Brown was not known to be financially desperate and had possibly intended to collect a debt near Urbana. The article reprinted a report from the *Chicago Inter Ocean* and noted that Brown's family lacked an explanation.  
**Assessment:** **A missing-person investigation only**; the story does not establish murder, fraud, voluntary flight, or his eventual fate. **Follow-up open.**

### 1898

**HN-313 — April 2, 1898 — Newspaper treats the *Maine* inquiry's mine theory as proof of Spanish treachery**  
**Source:** *Democratic Sentinel*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DMST18980402.2.7); [March 21 U.S. naval inquiry](https://www.history.navy.mil/research/publications/documentary-histories/united-states-navy-s/destruction-of-the-m/report-of-the-naval-0.html); [later Naval History assessment](https://www.history.navy.mil/about-us/leadership/director/directors-corner/h-grams/h-gram-077.html).  
**Report:** The paper led with an accusatory headline about Spain after the Navy's court of inquiry attributed the February 15 explosion of the battleship ***Maine*** at Havana to a **submarine mine**. The printed summary discussed the ship's position, magazines, and official findings; the headline converted the board's technical conclusion into a political accusation.  
**Assessment:** The **1898 board did favor an external mine**, but did **not identify Spain as the perpetrator**. Modern Navy historical discussion says the cause was **never conclusively determined**, with later analyses favoring an internal cause. The accusatory headline is **not an established fact**. 

**HN-314 — September 23, 1898 — Early figures after a destructive Lesser Antilles hurricane**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR18980923.2.52).  
**Report:** A news digest counted **531 killed**, **60,000 homeless**, **12,000 buildings destroyed**, and numerous wrecked or missing vessels after a hurricane struck islands of the **Windward group**. It described the sea surge and the damage as information still arriving from a disaster zone scattered across multiple islands.  
**Assessment:** These are **reported early aggregate estimates**, not an audited regional death register. Specific island-level and vessel records remain to be checked. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-315 — October 1, 1898 — Safe blown at the Flora bank; cashier wounded confronting burglars**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC18981001.2.2).  
**Report:** Burglars allegedly broke into a bank at **Flora, Carroll County, Indiana**, dynamited the safe, and fled with an estimated **$10,000–$15,000**. Cashier **W. H. Lenon**, who reportedly fired at the intruders, suffered serious gunshot wounds when they returned fire. The original report does not identify a convicted perpetrator.  
**Assessment:** A contemporary report of a violent safe robbery, with **estimated proceeds and no verified legal outcome**. Do not confuse it with the separately documented **Lima, Ohio, 1898 bank theft** solved in 1905 (HN-349). **Follow-up open.**

**HN-316 — December 3, 1898 — San Francisco “plague discovery” printed before the documented 1900 outbreak**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC18981203.2.75); [CDC historical investigation](https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/7576/cdc_7576_DS1.pdf); [CDC epidemiological study](https://wwwnc.cdc.gov/eid/article/21/1/14-0564_article).  
**Report:** The paper asserted that **“Asiatic cholera, or the bubonic plague”** had appeared in San Francisco's Chinatown and claimed quarantine officials and local newspapers were suppressing information. It gave no case confirmation in this short report and conflated **two distinct infectious diseases**.  
**Assessment:** **Do not repeat the claimed 1898 outbreak as fact**: CDC historical accounts identify **March 1900** as the first documented local human plague case. This 1898 item is a **contemporary scare or unverified rumor**, though later San Francisco plague cases were real. The paper's suppression allegation is not independently substantiated for 1898. 

### 1899

**HN-317 — January 13, 1899 — Youthful train-robbery confessions complicate a case against Jesse James Jr.**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR18990113.2.17).  
**Report:** Authorities at the **Hutchinson, Kansas, reformatory** reportedly said **Seth Rosebrook** and **Chad Stowell** had admitted involvement in a **September 1898 Missouri Pacific express-train hold-up** near Leeds, Missouri. The paper noted that **Jesse James Jr.** was already under indictment for that same robbery, making the purported new confessions especially consequential.  
**Assessment:** The newspaper attributes the claims to police and a deputy sheriff; **an alleged confession is not independently verified guilt**, and James's indictment is not proof that he committed the robbery. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-318 — June 30, 1899 — A Boston bank theft allegedly solved within six hours**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR18990630.2.76).  
**Report:** The *Metropolitan National Bank* in Boston lost **$10,000** to a man who allegedly walked out with the money unobserved. The article said **George Shea**, also called **Philip Lambele**, fled toward New York but was arrested after descriptions were telegraphed to police; all the money was recovered. It further alleged he offered the arresting officer the stolen cash to release him.  
**Assessment:** **Arrest and recovery as reported**, but the colorful bribery allegation and eventual sentence are not verified by court records. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-319 — August 11, 1899 — Caribbean hurricane reports describe devastation in Puerto Rico and Montserrat**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Journal*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ18990811.1.1).  
**Report:** Front-page dispatches from **San Juan**, **St. Thomas**, and **London** describe a hurricane that devastated **Puerto Rico**, damaged barracks holding American troops, and struck **Montserrat** and other Leeward Islands. The paper reported **64 recovered bodies** at **Humacao**, with considerably more deaths feared elsewhere, and separately reproduced the Leeward governor's evolving account of damage on Montserrat.  
**Assessment:** Distinct territories and separate dispatches are **not one verified casualty total**. The paper's numbers are urgent incomplete tallies. **Follow-up open** on each island's official records. 

**HN-320 — December 30, 1899 — Coal-mine death count rises at Braznell, Pennsylvania**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC18991230.2.19).  
**Report:** At the **Braznell mine near Brownsville, Pennsylvania**, the newspaper said **more than 40 deaths** were then known. The violent underground explosion reportedly destroyed the main shaft, hurled timbers out, and blasted a cage upward before it fell back, blocking access for rescuers. The original page notes that the scale appeared worse than first estimated.  
**Assessment:** **Early death tally**, not a final list. The shaft narrative is reported testimony; the precise initiating cause remains unverified here. **Follow-up open.**

### 1900

**HN-321 — March 10, 1900 — Red Ash mine report inflates the fatalities nearly threefold**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19000310.2.42); [West Virginia mine-safety disaster register](https://minesafety.wv.gov/historical-statistical-data/wv-mine-disasters-1884-to-present/); [National Park Service history](https://www.nps.gov/neri/learn/historyculture/mining-and-industrial-disasters.htm).  
**Report:** A dispatch said an explosion at the **Red Ash mine near Thurmond, West Virginia**, had killed **nearly 125 workers**, with none expected to survive. It described wrecked workings and cars hurled by the blast. The article speculated about “firedamp” without a surviving eyewitness account of ignition.  
**Assessment:** **The early death toll was dramatically wrong:** the West Virginia government's historical register and the NPS document **46 deaths** on **March 6, 1900**. Do not preserve the printed **125** as the true toll or treat the paper's proposed ignition as verified. 

**HN-322 — May 5, 1900 — Scofield tragedy: dramatic 250-death report versus later mining history**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19000505.2.89); [Utah state history](https://peoplesonline.history.utah.gov/s/peoples-online/page/winter-quarters); [Utah State Archives mine-inspector records](https://archives.utah.gov/2023/05/01/coal-correspondence-inspector-gomer-thomas-and-the-1900-scofield-mine-disaster/).  
**Report:** The newspaper described the explosion in the **Winter Quarters No. 1 and No. 4 mines** at **Scofield, Utah**, claiming **250 men had died**, with **137 bodies** recovered as of one report. Relatives volunteered for rescue work after a blast that left little firsthand evidence of its cause.  
**Assessment:** The **May 1 disaster was real**, but Utah historical sources commonly document **at least 200 deaths**, rather than accepting the article's **250** as a settled figure. Later counts vary by the records included; ignition theories are not established by this first dispatch. 

**HN-323 — September 14, 1900 — Galveston newspaper count falls far below the catastrophe's later scale**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19000914.2.25); [Galveston Historical Foundation and History Center FAQ](https://www.galvestonhistorycenter.org/research/storm-faqs); [National Park Service history](https://www.nps.gov/articles/galveston-hurricane-of-1900.htm).  
**Report:** After the **September 8 hurricane**, a front-loaded summary reported **2,500 people killed or missing in Galveston** and **2,000 at other Texas locations**, with **37 towns** damaged or destroyed. A witness who escaped by schooner described widespread waterfront destruction; communications failures left the scale uncertain.  
**Assessment:** An early undercount, **not the accepted final loss**. The Galveston history institution says the precise number is unknowable but judges roughly **7,000–8,000** likely; NPS describes approximately **6,000–8,000 in the city**. Distinguish city, island, and wider-region estimates. 

**HN-324 — November 9, 1900 — Handbag containing $650 vanishes inside a guarded bank**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19001109.2.51).  
**Report:** A depositor waiting in the **First National Bank in Chicago** reportedly placed a handbag containing **$650** on a ledge near a teller and turned away briefly. The bag disappeared, despite a crowded lobby and special officers stationed nearby. The correspondent theorized that someone standing directly behind the customer removed it and walked out unnoticed.  
**Assessment:** **A reported theft with an unidentified actor.** The proposed method is an inference, not an eyewitness identification; no recovered funds or arrest is established. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-325 — December 18, 1900 — Editorial coverage of two Black prisoners lynched at Rockport**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 8 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN19001218-01.1.8).  
**Report:** The newspaper discussed the **December lynching of Jim Henderson and Bud Rowland in Rockport, Indiana**, printing a *Chicago Tribune* editorial that simultaneously conceded both men were in secure custody, argued that they should have been tried, and **presumed their guilt while employing racist characterizations**. It compared a small number of Northern lynchings against many more in the South to make a sectional political point.  
**Assessment:** This is evidence of **mob killings and contemporary racialized editorial framing**, **not evidence that either prisoner received a fair trial or was guilty**. The editorial's asserted crime details and racial statistics need independent verification. **Follow-up open.**

### 1901

**HN-326 — September 7, 1901 — Express-train robbers allegedly make an engineer drive off with their stolen cars**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19010907.2.72).  
**Report:** Five armed men reportedly stopped a **Cotton Belt** train near **Eylau, Texas**, disconnected the mail and express cars from the passenger coaches, and forced the engineer to move them about a mile. The gang then blew open an express safe with dynamite and escaped with what the first dispatch called a substantial but imprecise sum.  
**Assessment:** A reported **train hold-up**, not an independently verified inventory of stolen property. The initial page does not identify convicted suspects. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-327 — September 28, 1901 — McKinley assassination case produces a murder conviction amid sensational reporting**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19010928.2.78); [contemporaneous September 15 mourning and succession report](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IJ19010915.1.1).  
**Report:** In Buffalo, a jury found **Leon Czolgosz guilty of murdering President William McKinley**, who had been shot September 6 and died September 14. The newspaper described a short trial, prosecution witnesses, and the defense's decision not to offer testimony. The report's highly charged narrative repeatedly portrayed the accused through moral epithets. The *Indianapolis Journal* also recorded **Theodore Roosevelt taking the presidential oath**.  
**Assessment:** **A reported trial verdict**, not a substitute for the trial transcript. The newspaper's outrage must be distinguished from what witnesses proved; contemporaneous speculation about **poisoned bullets** in other dispatches is not established here. 

**HN-328 — November 14, 1901 — A dozen deaths attributed to one woman remain only police suspicions**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Journal*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJR19011114.2.107).  
**Report:** **Mary Belle Witwer**, arrested at **Dayton, Ohio**, on suspicion of poisoning her sister, had become the subject of claims about **12 earlier deaths** involving relatives and families where she had worked. The unusually cautious short report acknowledged that she denied wrongdoing and said **no incriminating evidence had yet been found**.  
**Assessment:** **A classic dangerous pattern of serial-killer insinuation without proof.** The alleged additional deaths are not established homicides and Witwer is not presented as convicted. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-329 — November 15, 1901 — Leavenworth's mass prison break and the death of an officer**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19011115.2.24); [Federal Bureau of Prisons record of Officer Joseph B. Waldrupe](https://www2.fed.bop.gov/about/history/hero_waldrupe.jsp?i=0).  
**Report:** The paper described an armed breakout from the new **federal prison at Leavenworth**, with **26 convicts escaping** after overpowering guards, seizing arms, and briefly taking civilians hostage. It reported the deaths of prisoner **Ford Quinn** and guard **J. B. Waldrup** and named alleged ringleaders including **Gus Parker** and **Frank Thompson**.  
**Assessment:** The **federal Bureau of Prisons** confirms that Officer **Joseph B. Waldrupe died November 10, 1901**, from wounds incurred during the November 7 escape, and that the surviving fugitives were eventually caught. The original report's description of two people killed during the immediate clash **collapses the officer's later death into the event**. 

### 1902

**HN-330 — January 30, 1902 — Lost Creek mine death toll printed as 50; mine records say 20**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Journal*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJR19020130.2.88); [Iowa mine-inspection history](https://pubs.lib.uiowa.edu/annals-of-iowa/article/13847/galley/122281/view/); [1902 mine report](https://usminedisasters.miningquiz.com/saxsewell/1902_Lost_Creek_Report.pdf).  
**Report:** The newspaper said **50 coal miners** had died near **Oskaloosa, Iowa**, in the **Lost Creek** explosion of **January 24**, and described a misfired shot, flammable gas and coal dust, shattered cages, and desperate rescue attempts.  
**Assessment:** **Incorrect preliminary figure.** Iowa mine-inspection history identifies **20 killed and 14 injured**. The contemporary proposed sequence of ignition should be checked against the full inspector's account; 50 is not the historically documented number of deaths. 

**HN-331 — July 12, 1902 — An elopement report mixes a theft arrest with unproved poisoning allegations**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19020712.2.51).  
**Report:** **Aline O'Malley** and **William Hearin** (the OCR is inconsistent) were reportedly held in Philadelphia on theft charges while authorities in **South Bend, Indiana**, investigated suspicions surrounding the poisoning of **Professor O'Malley**. The item recounted the couple's marriage and separation in an intensely judgmental tone.  
**Assessment:** **Custody for one alleged offense and inquiry into another are not convictions of either.** Names and details require checking against the page image and subsequent case records. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-332 — July 18, 1902 — Johnstown's Rolling Mill mine: feared 200 dead, historically 112**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19020718.2.57); [Heritage Johnstown mining history](https://www.heritagejohnstown.org/attractions/heritage-discovery-center/johnstown-history/history-coal-cambria-county/).  
**Report:** Following a **July 10** blast at the **Cambria Steel Company's Rolling Mill mine** in Johnstown, Pennsylvania, initial estimates ranged from **125 to 200 dead**, while a rumor placed the toll even higher. The report explained that afterdamp obstructed rescuers and that miners who escaped through another entrance had only fragmentary knowledge of colleagues still underground.  
**Assessment:** Later local research documents **112 deaths**, mostly from the ensuing deadly gases. The panic-era range and the **300-death rumor** must not be converted to final counts. 

**HN-333 — August 28, 1902 — Substitute test-takers allegedly buy medical licenses for unqualified clients**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Journal*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RLJR19020828.2.26).  
**Report:** A state board of medical registration reportedly uncovered people taking licensing examinations **under other applicants' names** and transferring the resulting documents to them. To prevent identity substitution, the board proposed requiring applicants to submit a photograph with their signature on its back in addition to the **$25 fee**.  
**Assessment:** An **administrative fraud investigation and proposed safeguard**, not a list of proved impersonators or convictions. The clipping does not identify particular offenders. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-334 — November 29, 1902 — A Rock Island train held up near Davenport; express car blasted apart**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19021129.2.29).  
**Report:** A gang estimated at **12–15 people** reportedly stopped a **Chicago, Rock Island and Pacific** express west of **Davenport, Iowa**, using a red lantern to halt the locomotive. The passenger coaches were detached and the train's engine used to move the express car before the robbers **blew it open with dynamite**, looted safes, and disappeared.  
**Assessment:** Estimates of the gang's size and stolen valuables were made before a verified investigative outcome. **Follow-up open.**

### 1903

**HN-335 — February 21, 1903 — Turf-investment “get rich quick” companies raided across cities**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19030221.2.58).  
**Report:** The paper described coordinated investigations of **E. J. Arnold & Co.** and related **turf-investment promotions**, including a raid on a Chicago branch. Investigators said customers in **51 Western cities and towns** had been drawn in, with losses described in the millions; company agents and managers were named in a mixture of arrests and allegations.  
**Assessment:** **Evidence of a contemporary fraud investigation and alleged geographic scope**, not an audited loss figure, judicial verdict, or verified claim that any named operator escaped with a particular amount. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-336 — May 8, 1903 — Frank Slide blamed on “mine damp”; later geologists identify mountain instability**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19030508.2.56); [Alberta Geological Survey](https://ags.aer.ca/our-science/turtle-mountain/frank-slide); [Alberta historic-place assessment](https://hermis.alberta.ca/ARHP/Details.aspx?DeptID=1&ObjectID=4665-0225).  
**Report:** Reporting the **April 29 rockslide at Frank**, the paper claimed **Turtle Mountain** had been split by an explosion of mine gas, placed the town in **British Columbia**, and estimated **96 deaths** as rock crushed sleeping residents. It warned of flooding from a temporarily dammed river and repeated alarming claims about miners killed in the shaft.  
**Assessment:** Several important corrections: **Frank is in Alberta, not British Columbia**; Alberta's geological survey documents **more than 70 deaths** and says **unstable geological structure**, not a demonstrated mine-gas explosion, was the principal cause. Mining and other factors may have contributed, but this article's single-cause explanation is unsupported. 

**HN-337 — July 4, 1903 — Hanna coal mine report predicts 234 victims; later inspector count is 169**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19030704.2.55); [Wyoming State Mine Inspector's report, reproduced by local historians](https://www.hannahistory.com/1904-state-mine-inspectors-report-for-1903-explosion.html); [Utah Archives inventory of the cross-state investigation](https://archives.utah.gov/research/inventories/series/23008/).  
**Report:** A **June 30** explosion at **Union Pacific's Hanna No. 1 mine**, Wyoming, was initially feared to have killed **234** miners. The same dispatch describes **46 people brought out alive** and repeats workers' speculative explanation involving opening an air chamber.  
**Assessment:** Historical inspector material records **169 deaths**. Inquest language attributed ignition to combustible gas but did **not securely identify the triggering act**; the newspaper's air-chamber hypothesis remains a theory. 

**HN-338 — August 1, 1903 — Explosives magazine wrecks a Lowell neighborhood**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19030801.2.24).  
**Report:** An explosion at a **United States Cartridge Company** magazine in **Lowell, Massachusetts**, destroyed the stone structure, damaged neighboring homes, and caused fires. The article estimated **about 25 dead and 30 injured** and acknowledged that earlier reports had predicted as many as **75 deaths**. The force was reportedly felt in distant communities, where some thought it an earthquake.  
**Assessment:** A well-documented **contemporary correction within one newspaper article**: even its revised count is provisional. A full final victim roster and technical investigation have not been examined. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-339 — December 4, 1903 — Chicago's “car barn bandits” described through arrest-era confessions**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19031204.2.55); [Northwestern University Chicago homicide records](https://homicide.northwestern.edu/searches/capital-punishment/).  
**Report:** A lengthy article catalogued robberies and shootings attributed to **Harvey Van Dine, Peter Niedermeier, Emil Roeski, and Gustave Marx**, including a deadly **August 30 car-barn robbery** and confrontations with railway workers and police. It referred to **police reports and disputed confessions**, and printed a claimed tally of seven killings.  
**Assessment:** Unlike many untested headlines in the ledger, **subsequent judicial records** document convictions and the **April 22, 1904 executions of Marx, Niedermeier, and Van Dine**. Particular acts ascribed to each man in the original confession-era narrative still require charge-by-charge comparison; do not automatically accept every contemporaneous allegation. 

**HN-340 — December 31, 1903 — Iroquois Theatre fire exposed deadly failures of exit and fire protection**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN19031231-01.1.1); [Library of Congress fire-code history](https://blogs.loc.gov/law/2015/12/on-the-shelf-fire-codes/); [Chicago Public Library history](https://www.chipublib.org/blogs/post/absolutely-fireproof-the-iroquois-theater-fire-of-1903/).  
**Report:** The front page reported the **December 30 Chicago Iroquois Theatre fire** during a performance of ***Mr. Bluebeard***, placing the immediate known dead near **580**, with many more still listed missing. It described confusion over how the stage fire started, desperate searches for relatives, and makeshift emergency responses around the morgues.  
**Assessment:** Later histories identify **602 deaths**, **not 580 plus every person initially missing**; the preliminary categories overlap. Chicago and Library of Congress accounts document combustible stage materials, obstructed exits, and the ensuing changes to fire-safety practice. 

### 1904

**HN-341 — January 30, 1904 — Harwick mine explosion leaves Pittsburgh-area families waiting at the shaft**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19040130.2.23); [Carnegie Hero Fund's historical account](https://www.carnegiehero.org/harwick-mine-disaster-victims/).  
**Report:** A dispatch from **Cheswick, Pennsylvania**, described a blast that turned the **Harwick mine** into a chamber of wreckage and toxic gas, claiming **180–190 miners** might be dead. The paper named **Adolph Gunia** as the only miner then brought out alive and reported deadly danger to rescuers. Another column on the same OCR page concerns an investigation into the **Chicago theater fire**; the stories should not be conflated.  
**Assessment:** The Carnegie Hero Fund documents **179 miners and two rescue volunteers killed** on **January 25, 1904**. The newspaper's early uncertainty, and its separate theater-fire legal news, must be kept distinct. 

**HN-342 — April 23, 1904 — An Indiana bank safe blown apart; alleged getaway wagon not identified**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19040423.2.29).  
**Report:** A **2 a.m. explosion** at the **Farmers' and Merchants' Bank of Clay City, Indiana**, reportedly let thieves escape with **$5,281**. The bank said its losses were insured. Witnesses reported **four men driving rapidly away**, but authorities had no confirmed identification. The newspaper also included unrelated local accidents under the same heading.  
**Assessment:** **Amount claimed by the bank and eyewitness suspicion**, not evidence that the four observed men were the perpetrators or that the money was recovered. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-343 — June 24, 1904 — The excursion steamer *General Slocum* burns with families aboard**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19040624.2.19); [Library of Congress newspaper guide](https://guides.loc.gov/chronicling-america-slocum-steamboat-tragedy/selected-articles).  
**Report:** An excursion carrying families and children from **St. Mark's German Lutheran Church** caught fire on the **East River near Hell Gate** on **June 15**. The paper reported **roughly 1,000 deaths**, describing frantic attempts to escape the burning vessel and boat crews trying to rescue passengers as it grounded near **North Brother Island**.  
**Assessment:** The early number is close to the widely documented **1,021 deaths**, but details of crowd behavior and rescues are a dramatic correspondent's telling, not findings from an inquiry. The Library of Congress preserves contemporary articles for source comparison. 

**HN-344 — July 8, 1904 — Search for missing Quaker minister prompts dredging of ponds and rivers**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19040708.1.1).  
**Report:** **Rev. Elwood O. Ellis**, pastor of a Friends congregation on South Eighth Street in **Richmond, Indiana**, had disappeared. Police and friends searched waterways, while relatives speculated that he might have become ill or distressed. The newspaper reports contradictory sightings and says his bicycle was still at home. **His wife and mother were awaiting news**, with no confirmed location or fate.  
**Assessment:** **A missing-person search; suicide, mental illness, and criminal acts were unconfirmed theories.** The newspaper's OCR header misreads the year as **1994**, but the issue date and 1904 political news establish the publication is from **1904**. **Follow-up open.**

### 1905

**HN-345 — March 10, 1905 — Jane Stanford's poisoning death becomes a contested homicide investigation**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19050310.2.72); [Stanford History Department's review](https://history.stanford.edu/publications/who-killed-jane-stanford-gilded-age-tale-murder-deceit-spirits-and-birth-university); [historical reassessment in *Stanford Magazine*](https://stanfordmag.org/contents/who-killed-jane-stanford).  
**Report:** The paper described university cofounder **Jane Leland Stanford** dying in convulsions at a **Honolulu hotel** after drinking bicarbonate of soda, reportedly exclaiming that she had been poisoned. A previous suspected poisoning at her California residence made the story still more alarming. The dispatch treated motive and perpetrator as unknown.  
**Assessment:** Unlike many speculative poison stories, a **Honolulu coroner's jury found strychnine poisoning**, as later Stanford historical scholarship documents. The **killer's identity remains unproved**; allegations against particular associates and competing official explanations must not be made into a judicial verdict. 

**HN-346 — April 8, 1905 — Leiter's Zeigler mine blast: first reports forecast forty or more dead**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19050408.2.25); [U.S. Bureau of Mines historical fatality table](https://digital.library.unt.edu/ark:/67531/metadc12321/m2/1/high_res_d/Bulletin0115.pdf); [contemporary coroner evidence collected locally](https://genealogytrails.com/ill/franklin/1905zeiglermine.html).  
**Report:** After an **April 3 explosion** at **Joseph Leiter's Zeigler mine** in Illinois, the paper said around **50 men had been trapped**, feared **40 or more dead**, and counted **37 bodies recovered** at one point. It confidently suggested gas accumulating during a Sunday shutdown had caused the disaster, although no completed inquiry was quoted.  
**Assessment:** The federal historical mining table records **49 deaths at Leiter/Zeigler**. The paper's **Sunday-gas accumulation theory** was preliminary, not a proved reconstruction; later inquest accounts must be consulted for the full mechanism. 

**HN-347 — July 5, 1905 — Sailors seize the Russian battleship *Potemkin* during revolt**  
**Source:** *Nappanee Advance-News*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=NAN19050705.1.2); [contemporary follow-up in HN-348](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19050715.2.34).  
**Report:** In reports from **Odessa**, sailors took control of the imperial Russian battleship ***Kniaz Potemkin***, killed officers, and raised a red flag while unrest spread ashore. The article relayed **competing versions** of an incident over spoiled food and the killing of a sailor, with confused early reports about how many officers were dead and whether the larger fleet would resist.  
**Assessment:** **The mutiny is real**, but this rapidly transmitted dispatch contains unconfirmed individual killing stories and predictions about the fleet. The following week's separate report establishes that the ship ultimately entered **Romanian** custody, contrary to early surrender scenarios (HN-348). 

**HN-348 — July 15, 1905 — *Potemkin* surrender changes the story from naval siege to extradition dispute**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19050715.2.34).  
**Report:** After cruising the Black Sea, the mutinous ***Potemkin*** entered **Constanța (then often rendered Kustenji), Romania**, and accepted Romanian terms of surrender. The newspaper said the sailors were to be treated as deserters and released at the frontier, while Russia might demand them to stand trial for mutiny and deaths aboard ship. The vessel was handed back to **Admiral Kruger's Russian command**; the accompanying torpedo boat reportedly departed.  
**Assessment:** A **material follow-up to HN-347**: surrender to Romanian officials is documented by the period paper, while threatened extradition and later legal outcomes are **not thereby resolved**. Statements blaming specific sailors for deaths require independent proceedings. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-349 — November 15, 1905 — Woman's account clears two bank officials blamed for a seven-year-old vault theft**  
**Source:** *Nappanee Advance-News*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=NAN19051115.1.6).  
**Report:** The paper reconstructed the **December 1898 theft of approximately $18,332 from the American National Bank at Lima, Ohio**. Its cashier **Gus Kalb** and vice president **N. L. Michael** had been **arrested and indicted**, though their cases were dismissed. By 1905, janitor **Elijah Bowsher** and associate **Thomas K. Wilkins** had allegedly confessed after Wilkins's former wife helped authorities secretly record a conversation. Both men **pleaded guilty** and received sentences of **seven and five years**. The janitor reportedly demonstrated how he had escaped from a locked vault after stealing the money.  
**Assessment:** Unlike a mere arrest, this newspaper account documents **guilty pleas and sentences**, while underscoring the damage done to two wrongly suspected officials. The mechanics and amount are from this report and should be checked against Ohio court files; its original 1898 event date is **not** a new 1905 robbery. 

**HN-350 — November 24, 1905 — Vladivostok mutiny rumors outpace verified casualty information**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19051124.2.36).  
**Report:** Dispatches from **Vladivostok** during Russia's revolutionary unrest claimed that mutinous soldiers and sailors had fought loyalist troops and that much of the city was burning. The newspaper's **headline said more than 800 of the tsar's soldiers killed**, but the article body gave a different figure of **more than 500**, drawing on reports whose communication and geographic details were unclear.  
**Assessment:** The contradiction is **inside the original article**. Neither casualty figure nor the claim that most of the city had burned can be treated as verified on this evidence. **Follow-up open** in Russian and international contemporary records. 

### 1906

**HN-351 — February 2, 1906 — Wreck of the *Valencia* amid inaccessible Vancouver Island cliffs**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19060202.2.60); [Parks Canada historical assessment](https://www.canada.ca/en/parks-canada/news/2017/06/the_sinking_of_thessvalencia.html).  
**Report:** The newspaper said the steamship ***Valencia*** ran onto rocks in fog on the west coast of Vancouver Island, with the number saved initially uncertain. It described wreckage and people struggling to escape high surf below steep cliffs, while repeating survivors' stories about the precise position of the wreck and an alleged misleading light.  
**Assessment:** The shipwreck is **confirmed**. Parks Canada records **136 deaths** and explains that the disaster spurred improvements in Canadian coastal rescue, communications, and lighthouse services. The dispatch's passenger enumeration, location estimates, and dramatic account of a false light **are not established by that official history**; they remain contemporary claims.

**HN-352 — March 23, 1906 — French mine explosion prompts premature claims that rescue is hopeless**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19060323.2.37); [Pas-de-Calais departmental archives](https://www.archivespasdecalais.fr/Recherche-par-commune/Lettre-C/Courrieres/Catastrophe-de-Courrieres).  
**Report:** In the aftermath of the **March 10 Courrières mining disaster**, the paper reported roughly **1,100 victims** in the Pas-de-Calais coalfield, an earlier underground fire, blocked passages, and rescue efforts eventually abandoned because of heat and smoke. It asserted that galleries had collapsed and the trapped miners were doomed.  
**Assessment:** The departmental archives document **1,099 fatalities** and identify a **coal-dust ignition**. The death estimate was remarkably close, but the newspaper's declaration that rescue was impossible should not be treated as a final account of all survivors or the completed inquiry's explanation.

**HN-353 — April 21, 1906 — San Francisco quake coverage swings toward 5,000 estimated dead**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19060421.2.2); [U.S. Geological Survey casualty review](https://earthquake.usgs.gov/earthquakes/events/1906calif/18april/casualties.php).  
**Report:** A sweeping account of the **April 18 earthquake and subsequent fires** relayed destruction in San Francisco and surrounding towns, broken water mains and desperate rescues. One passage estimated that the deaths might reach **5,000**, while immediately acknowledging that only a fraction of the bodies could yet be counted.  
**Assessment:** The catastrophe is confirmed. The USGS explains why contemporary official and later research totals differ; later reconstruction puts the number killed **near or above 3,000**, not the news report's provisional 5,000. Specific claims about every burning hotel, the mint, military control, and nearby towns require local documentation rather than accepting the fast-moving composite dispatch wholesale.

**HN-354 — August 10, 1906 — Mutinies spread among Russian Baltic garrisons and naval crews**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19060810.2.26).  
**Report:** After the 1905 revolutionary upheaval, the paper described mutinies or attempted seizures at **Sveaborg**, **Kronstadt**, and aboard the cruiser ***Pamiat Azova***; it said loyal warships opened fire near Sveaborg and mutineers surrendered, while reports of other naval unrest continued to arrive. The news column bundled military, maritime, and political developments from separate locations.  
**Assessment:** This is evidence of **contemporaneous reports of several distinct revolts**, not proof that one coherent organization controlled them or that every feared seizure took place. The article's internal military casualty details, political negotiations, and transliterated ship names need Russian naval-source checking. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-355 — August 24, 1906 — Valparaíso earthquake reports extend destruction across much of Chile**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19060824.2.62); [USGS historical earthquake magnitude and death register](https://earthquake.usgs.gov/data/comcat/catalog/official/significant_earthquake_magnitudes.pdf).  
**Report:** The paper claimed **Valparaíso, Chile**, and many settlements along the South American Pacific coast had suffered extreme damage from the **August 16 earthquake**, subsequent fires, and what it described as a huge harbor wave. Telegraph failures left enormous gaps in early information; some stories alleged damage from one end of Chile to the other.  
**Assessment:** USGS historical data lists this earthquake at approximately **magnitude 8.2** and **3,882 deaths**. The paper's regional rumors and sweeping claims of a devastating wave at every location **should not be generalized as verified measurements**. More specific Chilean surveys and ship records remain necessary.

**HN-356 — September 28, 1906 — Atlanta mob killings reported through a race-baiting narrative**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19060928.2.53); [Library of Congress historical context](https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/naacp/prelude.html); [Georgia National Guard original records](https://dlg.usg.edu/record/gaarchives_adhoc_1177).  
**Report:** The paper described armed white mobs attacking **Black Atlantans on streets and streetcars**, with deaths, injuries, and the deployment of state troops during **September 22–25**. It framed the violence around widespread allegations that Black men assaulted white women, even as the article conceded that those killed had **no shown connection** to those accusations.  
**Assessment:** The **Atlanta race massacre** is documented independently. The Library of Congress links it to inflammatory political and newspaper campaigns; the underlying sexual-assault allegations **must not be adopted as proven causes or justifications for murder**. Fatality totals varied in the press, and the victims were attacked by mobs, **not convicted in court**.

### 1907

**HN-357 — March 26, 1907 — French battleship *Iéna* blast blamed on a torpedo in first dispatches**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19070326.2.58); [1907 *U.S. Naval Institute Proceedings* initial assessment](https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/1907/april/professional-notes); [follow-up technical report](https://www.usni.org/magazines/proceedings/1907/july/professional-notes).  
**Report:** The paper claimed **200–300 people** had been killed on the French battleship ***Iéna*** at **Toulon**, attributing the March 12 magazine explosion to an **accidental compressed-air torpedo discharge**. It described ammunition detonating and sailors trapped within the docked vessel.  
**Assessment:** A **contemporaneous 1907 U.S. Naval Institute account** put fatalities at **at least 118** and regarded deterioration and spontaneous ignition of French **B powder** as the probable cause; its later technical follow-up traced combustion through the magazines. The newspaper's confident torpedo theory and inflated early casualty range **cannot be retained as findings**.

**HN-358 — November 2, 1907 — Financial panic attributed by one editor to a Rockefeller conspiracy**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19071102.2.14); [Federal Reserve History](https://www.federalreservehistory.org/essays/panic-of-1907).  
**Report:** A local editorial catalogued bank suspensions and receiverships during the **Panic of 1907**, starting with New York's **Knickerbocker Trust**, but alleged that efforts by Rockefeller and Standard Oil financiers to crush **F. Augustus Heinze** had precipitated the crisis. It treated this motive as obvious rather than documenting a particular agreement or court finding.  
**Assessment:** The financial crisis and failure of Knickerbocker Trust are **confirmed**, as are the copper-speculation connection, depositor runs, and resulting pressures for banking reform. A **deliberate Rockefeller plot causing the national panic is the editorial's attribution**, not an established finding in the Federal Reserve historical explanation.

**HN-359 — December 17, 1907 — Monongah explosion reported as entombing 500 miners**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19071217.2.32); [Mine Safety and Health Administration historical disaster table](https://arlweb.msha.gov/MSHAINFO/FactSheets/MSHAFCT8.htm).  
**Report:** A tremendous blast at **Monongah mines Nos. 6 and 8** in West Virginia was said to have trapped about **500 men**. The article detailed the damaged entrances and rescue impediments and offered coal dust as a suspected cause without citing a finished inspection.  
**Assessment:** MSHA's historical disaster table records **362 killed on December 6, 1907**. Other federal narrative accounts use slightly different tallies; **500 reportedly entombed does not mean 500 documented fatalities**. The significance of the accident is not diminished by correcting the initial count.

**HN-360 — December 27, 1907 — Darr mine tragedy initially said to involve 400 trapped workers**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19071227.2.65); [NIOSH historical mining research](https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/5513/cdc_5513_DS1.pdf).  
**Report:** The newspaper said **400 miners** had been entombed by a December 19 blast at the **Darr Mine** near Jacobs Creek, Pennsylvania, while smoke, damaged passages, and fire hindered rescue. It noted the catastrophe followed Monongah and another severe mine incident in a matter of weeks.  
**Assessment:** Federal mining research records **239 deaths** at Darr, not 400. The article said the source of the blast was then uncertain; later research classifies it as a gas-and-dust explosion. The original identifies an early **number feared trapped**, not the definitive death register.

### 1908

**HN-361 — March 7, 1908 — Collinwood school fire exposes fatal evacuation bottlenecks**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19080307.2.48); [Smithsonian National Museum of American History](https://www.americanhistory.si.edu/explore/stories/collinwood-school-disaster-influenced-fire-safety-protocols).  
**Report:** After fire raced through **Lake View School in Collinwood, Ohio**, the paper estimated **160–170 children dead**, described crowded stairwells and inward-opening doors, and alleged a defective furnace ignited the building. It reported hurried identification of victims and inadequate escape capacity.  
**Assessment:** Smithsonian research records **172 children, two teachers, and one rescuer** killed on March 4. The early tally excluded later reconciliations; the decisive broader finding is the inadequate evacuation design and resulting fire-safety reforms. Claims about each door and the precise ignition sequence must be evaluated against investigation records, not only this first narration.

**HN-362 — May 9, 1908 — Bodies found buried on the Belle Gunness farm in Indiana**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19080509.2.23); [LaPorte County Historical Society](https://laportecountyhistory.org/exhibits/belle-gunness/); [Library of Congress newspaper guide](https://guides.loc.gov/chronicling-america-belle-gunness-murder-farm).  
**Report:** Police excavated graves at **Belle Gunness's LaPorte farm** after **Andrew Helgelien's** brother sought news of him. The report described dismembered remains, missing prospective husbands, and a possible financial pattern. It also relayed an extremely speculative theory that trunks delivered to the farm had transported corpses from Chicago.  
**Assessment:** Discovery of victims' remains and the investigation are documented in county historical materials. **The 'Chicago murder clearing house' and specific claims about every husband, victim, or accomplice were investigative suspicions**, not judicially verified facts. The report's body count grows within the story itself; identities and total victims need coroner records.

**HN-363 — May 9, 1908 — Gunness death disputed; an innocent traveler detained as supposed fugitive**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19080509.1.1); [LaPorte County Historical Society background](https://laportecountyhistory.org/exhibits/belle-gunness/).  
**Report:** The front page reported that experts thought the **headless woman's remains** recovered from the burnt Gunness home were **too small to be Belle**, while the prosecutor maintained the remains were hers. In the same rush of speculation, police removed a woman from a train; she identified herself as **Cora Belle Herron** and established that she was not Gunness.  
**Assessment:** This is a valuable example of **contradictory expert and official views printed together**. The traveler was **wrongly suspected**, not evidence that Gunness escaped. Neither a sensational escape claim nor an unequivocal identity finding is established by this page; the historical mystery must remain labeled as such.

**HN-364 — August 19, 1908 — Springfield racial violence; lynchings reported alongside unproved accusations**  
**Source:** *Nappanee Advance-News*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=NAN19080819.1.3); [Library of Congress, NAACP exhibition](https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/naacp/prelude.html); [National Park Service study](https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/upload/1908-Springfield-Race-Riot-SRS-508-2023-0605.pdf).  
**Report:** The newspaper counted **seven violent deaths** after white mobs attacked Black residents, burned homes, and lynched people in **Springfield, Illinois**. Its coverage included troop movements and attacks on Black businesses but framed the upheaval around an alleged rape and also carried demeaning racial descriptions.  
**Assessment:** The attacks and Black victims are confirmed. The Library of Congress records that the accusation against **George Richardson was false**. The original paper's allegation and prejudicial language are evidence of the news climate, **not evidence of a Black suspect's guilt**. The riot helped inspire the formation of the NAACP.

**HN-365 — October 28, 1908 — Tennessee 'night rider' arrests after Reelfoot Lake violence**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19081028.2.24).  
**Report:** Officers reported **61 prisoners** in the investigation of masked **night-rider violence** around **Reelfoot Lake, Tennessee**, after the killing of **Captain Rankin**. The newspaper included a woman's stated intention to testify and fresh accounts of armed masked men demanding food or stopping travelers.  
**Assessment:** These are reports of **arrests, witness claims, and intimidation**, not 61 proven convictions or a verified membership list. The unsigned threats quoted in the dispatch cannot identify their author. The causes and subsequent court outcomes need Tennessee judicial records. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-366 — December 30, 1908 — Messina earthquake news mixes confirmed destruction with exaggerated geography**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19081230.2.14); [USGS seismic historical background](https://earthquake.usgs.gov/product/poster/20120520/us/1481139141593/poster.pdf).  
**Report:** Following the **December 28 earthquake**, dispatches described ruin in **Messina and Reggio Calabria**, a destructive sea wave, fires, interrupted communications, and severe losses across Sicily and southern Italy. Some passages declared other settlements completely destroyed even while conceding that direct reports were unavailable.  
**Assessment:** The earthquake and catastrophic destruction of the Messina Strait region are confirmed. The article's exact claims about **Catania, vanished villages, total deaths, and supposed volcanic connections** should not be adopted without geographic and seismological confirmation. Its phrase that whole towns vanished captures **early uncertainty**, not a mapped survey.

### 1909

**HN-367 — September 4, 1909 — Cook's claimed North Pole discovery becomes instant newspaper satire**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19090904.2.11); [contemporary rejection in HN-370](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19091224.2.42).  
**Report:** The paper said **Frederick Cook** had telegraphed that he reached the North Pole in April 1908, then jokingly claimed he had planted a beer advertisement and returned carrying pieces of the pole in his pocket.  
**Assessment:** **The joke is satirical commentary, not an expedition log.** Its existence shows how quickly the widely publicized announcement entered popular culture. Cook's geographic claim was disputed and was rejected by a scientific committee later the same year (HN-370); this paper does **not** verify that he reached the Pole.

**HN-368 — November 17, 1909 — Cherry mine rescue reports fear 384 dead; later tally lower**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19091117.2.10); [fuller contemporary rescue feature](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19091119.1.6); [NIOSH century-of-mining-research history](https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/5513/cdc_5513_DS1.pdf).  
**Report:** A fire in the **St. Paul coal mine at Cherry, Illinois**, spread after a load of hay or combustible material caught fire underground. The newspaper described sealing shafts, blocked rescue attempts, and uncertainty over how many trapped men remained alive; another paper warned **384** had been lost.  
**Assessment:** NIOSH identifies **259 miners and rescuers killed** in the November 13 disaster. The paper's dramatic counts reflected confusion about men trapped, men rescued, and rescuers killed. It is important to preserve the rescue chronology and **not copy 384 as the final fatality figure**.

**HN-369 — November 17, 1909 — U.S. Supreme Court imposes jail terms after Ed Johnson lynching**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19091117.2.27).  
**Report:** The paper reported the Supreme Court had sentenced former Chattanooga sheriff **Joseph F. Shipp** to **90 days for contempt**, along with jailer **Jeremiah Gibson** and four others receiving terms of **60 or 90 days**. The proceedings stemmed from the 1906 lynching of **Ed Johnson**, despite a stay issued by the Supreme Court.  
**Assessment:** This is **a specific court disposition**, not merely speculation about participants. It is crucial not to confuse the newspaper's **1909 sentencing date** with the **1906 lynching date**. The high court's contempt finding does not make Johnson's lynching lawful or prove the underlying accusation against him.

**HN-370 — December 24, 1909 — Copenhagen reviewers reject Cook's polar-navigation proof**  
**Source:** *Rensselaer Semi-Weekly Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RSWR19091224.2.42); [initial celebration and satire in HN-367](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19090904.2.11).  
**Report:** The newspaper said a **University of Copenhagen** scientific committee had rejected **Frederick Cook's proof** that he reached the North Pole. It went further, calling him a fraud and interpreting his absence from public view as evidence of personal guilt.  
**Assessment:** The **scientific rejection of the submitted polar claim** is distinct from any court finding of fraud. The newspaper's assertions about Cook's motives, income, or hiding are editorial judgments. This is a paired correction to HN-367, **not a determination that every other explorer's rival claim was proved**.

### 1910

**HN-371 — March 5, 1910 — Avalanche hurls two Great Northern trains down a mountainside**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19100305.2.63); [Washington State HistoryLink reconstruction](https://www.historylink.org/file/5127).  
**Report:** Two trains held by snow near **Wellington, Washington**, were swept from the rails into a canyon in the **March 1 avalanche**. The article initially stated **50 killed**, with other passengers missing, described shattered sleeping cars, and worried that recovery might be impossible until warmer weather.  
**Assessment:** Washington's historical record documents **96 deaths**, nearly twice the first newspaper's figure. The incident took place in **Washington State**, despite another contemporary paper mislocating Wellington in **Idaho**. The first count mixed recovered bodies, missing persons, and estimates.

**HN-372 — March 22, 1910 — Detoured Rock Island passenger train wrecks in Iowa**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19100322.2.3).  
**Report:** The newspaper reported **46 killed** and more injured near **Green Mountain, Iowa**, when a Chicago, Rock Island & Pacific train, using Chicago Great Western track because of a blockage, derailed. It said rails spread, then two locomotives and passenger cars piled up, smashing a Pullman and a smoking car.  
**Assessment:** This is a detailed **initial disaster and railroad-explanation report**, but not the completed accident inquiry. Its attribution to **spreading rails** came from early witnesses and officials; no later formal cause or final victim list was established in this pass. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-373 — July 9, 1910 — Ohio mob seizes and hangs a jailed anti-saloon detective**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19100709.2.5); [later arrest report](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19100716.2.47).  
**Report:** In **Newark, Ohio**, a mob forced the jail doors and hanged **Carl Etherington**, a detective hired for Anti-Saloon League raids. The paper said he had acknowledged shooting **William Howard**, who later died; it described anger over an alcohol-related raid and an attempted call for troops after the hanging.
