**Assessment:** The newspaper's account of an **extrajudicial killing** must not be interpreted as a lawful execution. It identifies an alleged confession in an emotionally charged, still-developing investigation. A subsequent article reports multiple arrests, **not convictions of every arrested person**. **Follow-up open** on court dispositions and Etherington's exact legal status.

**HN-374 — July 16, 1910 — Pioneer airman Charles Rolls killed when biplane collapses**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19100716.2.55).  
**Report:** The paper recorded the death of British aviator **Charles Stewart Rolls**, recently celebrated for flying across the English Channel and back, after his **Wright biplane** failed during an aerial display at **Bournemouth**. It said the aircraft collapsed while about forty feet above ground.  
**Assessment:** A specific **reported flight fatality**, not an unsolved crime. The cause-of-failure language is from a short dispatch and **not independently engineering-verified** by this pass. The event illustrates the danger of public aviation demonstrations at the very beginning of powered flight.

**HN-375 — October 8, 1910 — *Los Angeles Times* explosion treated immediately as industrial sabotage**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19101008.2.31); [1911 guilty-plea follow-up HN-378](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19111206.2.15).  
**Report:** An explosion and fire destroyed the ***Los Angeles Times*** building, killing workers and injuring others. The newspaper reported **20 dead and 80 injured** and quoted an editor blaming enemies of the paper's anti-union position, while noting investigators had reportedly found devices near other prominent addresses.  
**Assessment:** The explosion and deaths are documented, and a **later guilty plea by James B. McNamara** supplies a decisive development (HN-378). The editor's instant allegation did **not establish the identities or guilt of every labor organizer**. The newspaper's early death and injury numbers may not match subsequent victim accounting.

### 1911

**HN-376 — March 29, 1911 — Triangle factory fire claimed 141 lives in first Indiana account**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19110329.2.11); [Smithsonian National Museum of American History](https://americanhistory.si.edu/explore/stories/what-you-may-not-know-about-triangle-shirtwaist-factory-fire).  
**Report:** A short account said the fire at New York's **Triangle Waist Company** killed **141 garment workers**, many young women, in the upper floors of a factory building. It blamed a discarded cigarette near accumulated textile waste and inadequate escape measures, including insufficient exterior fire access.  
**Assessment:** The Smithsonian records **146 deaths**, not the dispatch's 141. The article's smoking-material explanation is an **early proposed ignition mechanism**, not a judgment that any specific worker was criminally responsible. The verified disaster helped bring sustained attention to factory fire safety and labor conditions.

**HN-377 — October 21, 1911 — Naval-aviation pioneer Eugene Ely dies during fairground flight**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19111021.2.29).  
**Report:** **Eugene Ely** fell with his biplane while flying at the state fairground in **Macon, Georgia**, and died shortly afterward. The newspaper recalled his earlier feat of landing on a platform aboard a warship and his appearances at aviation exhibitions.  
**Assessment:** The death is reported as an **aviation accident**. Several intimate last words, timings, and the newspaper's tally of **101 global aviation fatalities** are taken from period journalism, not official accident or worldwide aviation registers. Claims about exact mechanics **remain to be checked against contemporary flight investigations**.

**HN-378 — December 6, 1911 — McNamara guilty pleas resolve central issue in *Times* bombing case**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19111206.2.15); [sentencing report](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LPLTWO19111207.1.6).  
**Report:** The newspaper reported that **James B. McNamara pleaded guilty** to dynamiting the ***Los Angeles Times*** building in 1910, while **John J. McNamara** admitted a separate dynamiting of the Llewellyn Iron Works. The local editor angrily criticized the prospect of avoiding the death penalty.  
**Assessment:** These **individual guilty pleas** materially correct the earlier accusation-stage record in HN-375. They do **not** support assigning criminal culpability to unions or workers generally. The contemporaneous sentencing article reports **life imprisonment for James B. McNamara** and a separate sentence for his brother; the editorial's desired punishment was opinion, not the judgment.

### 1912

**HN-379 — January 24, 1912 — Railroad executives killed in private-car collision at Kinmundy**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19120124.2.12).  
**Report:** A following Illinois Central train hit the rear of a stopped train near **Kinmundy, Illinois**. The article named former Illinois Central president **James T. Harahan**, Rock Island vice president **Frank O. Melcher**, legal officer **Edward B. Pierce**, and bridge-company executive **Eldredge Wright** as killed in the private car. It said the train had stopped to take on water.  
**Assessment:** This is a contemporary identification of four victims. The suggestion that **unlit rear signal lamps** or insufficient time for flagging caused the collision was expressly attributed to reports, **not a completed railway inquiry**. **Follow-up open** on railway investigation findings.

**HN-380 — February 7, 1912 — Lawrence mill strike exposes wage realities behind tariff rhetoric**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19120207.2.24); [Smithsonian contextual history](https://americanhistory.si.edu/explore/stories/4-music-inspiring-objects-through-eyes-our-making-american-music-interns).  
**Report:** An editorial discussed the **Lawrence, Massachusetts, textile strike**. Quoting Governor **Eugene Foss** and labor spokesman **Joseph Ettor**, it charged that mill employees faced extremely low wages and employer fines despite tariff protection for the textile industry. It cited statistics on hourly earnings, especially among women.  
**Assessment:** The strike and dispute over wages and hours were real. The article is **an editorial combining quotations and statistical claims**, not a payroll audit of every Lawrence employer or proof that all workers earned a single advertised rate. Any employer-specific misconduct needs wage records or labor-inquiry testimony.

**HN-381 — March 16, 1912 — Hillsville courthouse shootings prompt search for Allen family suspects**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19120316.2.49).  
**Report:** Gunfire broke out in a **Hillsville, Virginia, courtroom**; the report named **Judge Thornton Massie, Commonwealth's Attorney William Foster, and Sheriff Lewis Webb** among the dead and gave an initial toll of **five**. It described pursuit of members of the **Allen family** into the Blue Ridge Mountains and repeated claims that the suspects were a coordinated gang of illicit whiskey distillers.  
**Assessment:** This is a period account of a **real courthouse shooting and manhunt**. Assertions that each named person was a 'moonshiner' or participated in a preplanned murder conspiracy were **not adjudicated in this dispatch**. Its descriptions of imminent gun battles are predictions, not outcomes. **Follow-up open** on trial-by-trial results.

**HN-382 — April 17, 1912 — First *Titanic* sinking reports contradict each other on survivors and deaths**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19120417.2.13); [NOAA historical synthesis](https://www.noaa.gov/office-of-general-counsel/gc-international-section/rms-titanic-history-and-significance).  
**Report:** The paper relayed the news of ***Titanic*** striking an iceberg and sinking during its maiden voyage. It printed a prediction that **more than 1,800 died**, but elsewhere referenced **670–675 saved** and recounted an earlier, incorrect wireless rumor that **all passengers had been rescued**. It also relayed unverified descriptions of suction dragging lifeboats underwater.  
**Assessment:** NOAA's retrospective reports **more than 1,500 deaths**, with exact historical tallies varying by manifest and classification. The ship sank; the early claim that everyone was rescued was **false**. Stories about what the *Carpathia* could see before arriving and how many lifeboats were pulled under were **speculative dispatches**, not eyewitness conclusions of the inquiry.

**HN-383 — April 19, 1912 — Newspaper updates *Titanic* toll but repeats false lifeboat assurances**  
**Source:** *Walkerton Independent*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19120419.1.6); [NOAA Titanic questions and data](https://www.noaa.gov/office-of-general-counsel/gc-international-section/rms-titanic-frequently-asked-questions).  
**Report:** A longer follow-up gave **1,525 reportedly lost**, but reproduced early remarks from the White Star Line asserting that lifeboat capacity was sufficient for all aboard and elsewhere spoke of **50 lifeboats**, while discussing the ***Carpathia***'s recovered survivors.  
**Assessment:** The death estimate moved closer to subsequent accounting, but ***Titanic* carried only 20 lifeboats**, insufficient for all aboard. The page is useful precisely because **a better loss estimate did not mean the remaining operational claims were reliable**. HN-382 and HN-383 together document how information changed during four days of maritime reporting.

### 1913

**HN-384 — March 26, 1913 — Early Dayton flood dispatches incorrectly fear thousands dead**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19130326.1.1); [Ohio History's Dayton historical review](https://www.ohiohistory.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/01/rp-23.pdf).  
**Report:** As the **Great Miami River** flooded Dayton and neighboring Ohio communities, the paper repeated estimates of **5,000 or even 10,000 deaths in Dayton**; another relayed estimate put the Miami Valley total at **6,500**. The same front page printed rescue pleas, eyewitness accounts, and **contradictory claims** about fatalities in nearby Piqua.  
**Assessment:** Ohio History's reconstruction records **361 Dayton flood deaths**, far fewer than these desperate early rumors. Massive destruction and severe danger were real, but the first death forecasts **cannot be treated as counted victims**. This is an unusually clear example of contradictory estimates existing within a single day's coverage.

**HN-385 — March 28, 1913 — Local rescuers bring a more grounded picture of Dayton's devastation**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19130328.1.1); [Ohio History's Dayton assessment](https://www.ohiohistory.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/01/rp-23.pdf).  
**Report:** Richmond residents and reporters traveled toward **Dayton** with automobile loads of food and reported firsthand on broken transport routes, people sheltering in buildings, water shortages, damaged electrical and gas services, and improvised boat rescues. The paper admitted casualty estimates still ranged from **1,000 to 5,000**, even while giving specific descriptions of relief work.  
**Assessment:** This is a **follow-up to HN-384**, not another flood. The firsthand relief details are more grounded than the hearsay death figures; the latter remained guesses. The eventual **361** recorded deaths should replace the inflated numbers when summarizing the disaster.

**HN-386 — November 17, 1913 — Great Lakes storm destroys freighters and strands crews**  
**Source:** *Hammond Times*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LCT19131117.1.4); [earlier account of rescued *L. C. Waldo* crew](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19131112.1.1); [National Weather Service historical study](https://www.weather.gov/news/131107-white?ipid=promo-link-block1).  
**Report:** After days of extraordinary winds and snow, the paper counted **more than 200 dead** and numerous Great Lakes freighters lost. It identified the ***Henry B. Smith*** as missing with **28 aboard** and described debris and crew remains washing ashore. Earlier coverage showed how other vessels such as ***L. C. Waldo*** were wrecked yet yielded survivors.  
**Assessment:** NOAA's National Weather Service characterizes the **November 7–10 'White Hurricane'** as causing **roughly 250 deaths** and multiple major wrecks, with individual NOAA summaries using somewhat different totals. **Every reported vessel wreck was not necessarily a total crew loss**; the paired source about rescued sailors underscores that distinction.

**HN-387 — December 29, 1913 — Federal Reserve plan explained as answer to recurring bank instability**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19131229.1.4); [Federal Reserve historical explanation](https://www.federalreservehistory.org/essays/panic-of-1907).  
**Report:** A newspaper summary explained the newly approved **Federal Reserve legislation**, regional reserve banks, the proposed reserve board, bank subscriptions, and the power to rediscount eligible commercial paper. It described an **earlier Senate vote** and the organization work required before banks could open.  
**Assessment:** The Federal Reserve Act **became law on December 23, 1913**. The original article's Senate passage date should **not** be mistaken for the enactment date. The banking reorganization followed long debate, including the weaknesses exposed during the **1907 panic** (HN-358); it was not a single editor's direct response to a proven Rockefeller conspiracy.

### 1914

**HN-388 — January 21, 1914 — Newly inaugurated Terre Haute mayor charged with election manipulation**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19140121.2.12); [later verdict report HN-395](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19150414.2.19).  
**Report:** A special grand jury indicted new **Terre Haute mayor Donn M. Roberts** for alleged bribery, fraudulent voter registration, and ballot-box manipulation. The newspaper said investigators alleged payments to precinct workers for fictitious voter names; Roberts posted bond and declined comment.  
**Assessment:** These are **indictments and alleged particulars**, not a January conviction. The follow-up in **1915** reports an actual election-fraud verdict (HN-395); the intervening courtroom record would be necessary to assign responsibility for each charged count.

**HN-389 — April 22, 1914 — U.S. sailors and Marines occupy Veracruz amid conflicting war reports**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium (Daily)*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19140422.1.1); [U.S. Navy historical reconstruction](https://www.history.navy.mil/content/history/nhhc/browse-by-topic/wars-conflicts-and-operations/early-20th-century-conflicts/veracruz-1914.html).  
**Report:** The newspaper reported the landing of U.S. sailors and Marines at **Veracruz, Mexico**, fighting near the waterfront and customs house, reinforcements from American warships, and increasingly grim speculation about U.S.–Mexican war. Some reports treated a much wider conflict as inevitable.  
**Assessment:** The Navy confirms **landings and the seizure of the customs house on April 21–22**, amid fighting with Mexican defenders. **An imminent general war was a prediction**, not a settled fact. The specific reported number of killed or captured combatants should be reconciled with military records.

**HN-390 — April 23, 1914 — Labor newspaper describes deadly attack on Ludlow tent colony**  
**Source:** *Marion Labor Sentinel*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=PKMLS19140423.1.1); [National Park Service historical account](https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/war-in-the-coalfields-the-ludlow-massacre-and-its-impact-on-the-eight-hour-workday.htm).  
**Report:** In strongly partisan, sometimes damaged OCR, a labor newspaper described **Colorado National Guard troops and mine guards clashing with striking coal miners** near **Ludlow**, tents burning, and women and children being caught in the violence. The paper printed demands for federal intervention and charged that miners' families were being deliberately slaughtered.  
**Assessment:** The **April 20 Ludlow Massacre** is corroborated by the National Park Service, which records **21 killed, including 11 children**. The paper offers a contemporaneous **labor movement perspective**. Particular battlefield decisions, intentionality of each death, and quoted accusations against named people require more than one partisan report; the historical deaths and strike context are not in doubt.

**HN-391 — June 5, 1914 — *Empress of Ireland* sinks in collision on the St. Lawrence**  
**Source:** *Walkerton Independent*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19140605.1.2); [Parks Canada's official maritime history](https://parks.canada.ca/lhn-nhs/qc/pointeaupere/culture/histoire-history/services).  
**Report:** The Canadian Pacific liner ***Empress of Ireland*** sank in fog after collision with the ***Storstad*** near Pointe-au-Père. The newspaper gave **969 dead and 418 saved**, included troubling differences in the number of surviving crew, women, and children, and described how abruptly the vessel listed after the collision.  
**Assessment:** Parks Canada documents **1,012 deaths** and says the vessel sank in **about 14 minutes**. The paper's exact numbers and accusatory reconstruction of the other ship backing away were preliminary, **not a completed adjudication of navigational fault**.

**HN-392 — July 3, 1914 — Assassination at Sarajevo reported with elaborate, unproved plot details**  
**Source:** *Walkerton Independent*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19140703.1.2); [contemporary front-page coverage](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DT19140629.1.1); [U.S. Navy's First World War context](https://www.history.navy.mil/content/history/museums/nmusn/explore/photography/wwi/wwi-entering-the-war/german-uboat-attacks-1915-17.html).  
**Report:** The paper described the **June 28 assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand and Duchess Sophie** in **Sarajevo**: a failed bomb attack and subsequent pistol shots. It identified **Gavrilo Princip** among those arrested and asserted additional details about bulletproof clothing, advance planning, thrown-away bombs, and official knowledge that required more checking.  
**Assessment:** The murders and their place in the prelude to **World War I** are established. The report's conjectures about each suspect's knowledge, every accomplice, and the purpose of each weapon **are not proved by the breaking dispatch**. Anti-Serb sentiment and the paper's generalizations must be distinguished from individual criminal evidence.

**HN-393 — July 22, 1914 — *Komagata Maru* passengers blocked from entering Canada**  
**Source:** *South Bend News-Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19140722.1.2); [Parks Canada national historic event description](https://parks.canada.ca/culture/clmhc-hsmbc/~/~/~/link.aspx?_id=2BD417C19474481DB85572425EA71CB3&_z=z).  
**Report:** The newspaper said passengers aboard the ***Komagata Maru*** at **Vancouver** faced deportation after weeks confined aboard ship; it described a government naval vessel and threatened force if the passengers resisted. Its account broadly and inaccurately labeled the South Asian passengers as **'Hindus'**.  
**Assessment:** Parks Canada records **376 passengers—340 Sikhs, 24 Muslims, and 12 Hindus**—who challenged **racially discriminatory immigration restrictions** and were prevented from disembarking. The source's religious label and suggestion that coercion simply represented orderly immigration enforcement are misleading. The episode is a documented **civil-rights injustice**, not a mutiny conviction.

### 1915

**HN-394 — January 16, 1915 — Avezzano quake described as leaving up to 50,000 dead and injured**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19150116.2.11).  
**Report:** Following the **January 13 earthquake** in Italy's **Marsica/Avezzano** region, the newspaper estimated **25,000–50,000 dead and injured combined**, with other passages listing roughly **15,000 deaths** in particular districts. It described collapsed dwellings, shattered communications and King Victor Emmanuel visiting the emergency zone.  
**Assessment:** **Crucial distinction:** the large headline estimate **combined fatalities and injuries**, and the story's local sub-totals were preliminary. This research pass has not reconciled them with a complete Italian civil-protection death register. The earthquake is real, but **do not repeat '50,000 killed'** as the newspaper's finding or as a verified final count. **Follow-up open.**

**HN-395 — April 14, 1915 — Terre Haute election-fraud case moves from indictment to guilty verdict**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19150414.2.19); [1914 charging-stage report HN-388](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19140121.2.12).  
**Report:** After a contested political trial, the paper discussed a **guilty verdict** against **Donn M. Roberts and associated election operatives**, including evidence from witnesses and defendants who had admitted fraudulent conduct. Its commentary strongly denounced the defendants and the town's political machinery.  
**Assessment:** **This is a verdict-stage story**, materially different from the 1914 indictments. The paper's hostility is editorial, so it cannot establish the guilt of anyone **outside the actual verdict** or give the sentence or appeals for each individual. Official judgments remain the proper source for count-by-count dispositions.

**HN-396 — May 8, 1915 — *Lusitania* sinking triggers conflicting first estimates and diplomatic alarms**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19150508.2.11); [U.S. Naval History and Heritage Command study](https://www.history.navy.mil/content/history/nhhc/browse-by-topic/wars-conflicts-and-operations/world-war-i/history/ww1-freedom-of-seas.html).  
**Report:** The paper reported a German submarine attack on the British passenger liner ***Lusitania*** off Ireland. Initial accounts alternated among **700** and **900 feared dead**, while surviving passenger numbers and lifeboat stories were still contradictory. It discussed advance German warnings and the possibility of a diplomatic crisis with the still-neutral United States.  
**Assessment:** The Navy records that **U-20 torpedoed the ship on May 7** and **1,198 people died**, including **128 U.S. citizens**. The newspaper's estimated losses were far too low. Its further assertion that the vessel was **flying an American flag at the time** requires separate support and must not change the British ship's established nationality.

**HN-397 — July 28, 1915 — *Eastland* capsizes beside Chicago dock; early toll nearly doubles reality**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19150728.2.14); [National Park Service account](https://www.nps.gov/people/jun-fujita.htm).  
**Report:** The excursion steamer ***Eastland*** rolled over in the **Chicago River** while embarking **Western Electric workers** bound for a company outing. A dramatic account said **about 1,500 died**, described trapped people and improvised rescue crews, and offered contradictory allegations about ballast and how many passengers were aboard.  
**Assessment:** The National Park Service gives **844 deaths**, not 1,500. The ship's loss was real and catastrophic, but claims blaming a sudden one-sided rush of passengers were **disputed even within the period article**. Arrests or proposed manslaughter charges described in breaking news **are not proof of convictions**.

**HN-398 — August 17, 1915 — Leo Frank abducted from prison and murdered by mob**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19150817.2.10); [New Georgia Encyclopedia case history](https://www.georgiaencyclopedia.org/articles/history-archaeology/leo-frank-case/).  
**Report:** The paper stated that **Leo Frank**, convicted of murdering **Mary Phagan**, had been seized from the state prison at **Milledgeville** and found hanged near **Marietta, Georgia**. It relayed rumors about exactly how he was killed and speculated about what the kidnappers meant to do with his body.  
**Assessment:** The abduction and **lynching** are established; none of the mob's members acquired judicial authority to execute him. Georgia's **1986 posthumous pardon** expressly addressed the state's failures to protect Frank and bring his killers to justice **without deciding his guilt or innocence**. The original report's sensational and anti-Semitic context demands care.

**HN-399 — September 25, 1915 — Wartime newspapers report Armenian deportations and mass killing**  
**Source:** *Daily Tribune* (Terre Haute), p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DT19150925.1.1); [U.S. Holocaust Memorial Museum historical synthesis](https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/article/the-armenian-genocide-1915-16-in-depth).  
**Report:** A dispatch citing British newspaper correspondents and American missionaries described **Ottoman deportations of Armenians**, forced journeys toward arid regions, robbery, killings, deaths through hunger and thirst, and separation of families. The report recorded contemporaneous accusations of systematic destruction while specific refugee and casualty reports were difficult to verify in wartime.  
**Assessment:** The Holocaust Memorial Museum confirms the **Armenian genocide in 1915–1916**, with estimates ranging from **at least 664,000 to as many as 1.2 million deaths across the period**. This independent historical finding establishes the overall systematic persecution; it does **not automatically authenticate every individually narrated atrocity or exact 1915 newspaper figure**. The event must not be mischaracterized as merely an unverified wartime rumor.

### 1916

**HN-400 — May 4, 1916 — Dublin Easter Rising described as broken by unconditional surrender**  
**Source:** *Syracuse and Lake Wawasee Journal*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TSYLWWJ19160504.1.1).  
**Report:** The Indiana weekly reproduced a London communiqué claiming that Irish insurgent leaders had surrendered, the Four Courts area was retaken, and **707 prisoners** were in custody. Its language adopted the British command's view of a rebellion successfully suppressed.  
**Assessment:** The **Easter Rising of April 1916**, British military suppression, arrests, and executions are established history. This is a **belligerent government's contemporary statement**, not an independent accounting of all rebels, civilian deaths, or the political consequences. The OCR even reads “Iceland” where the report plainly concerns **Ireland**. **Follow-up open** on individual prisoners and changing tallies.

**HN-401 — July 13, 1916 — Fatal shark attacks set off searches in New Jersey waterways**  
**Source:** *Daily Tribune* (Terre Haute), p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DT19160713.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper described hundreds searching New Jersey bays and tidal waterways after attacks on swimmers, with **two deaths reported in a creek** and others injured. Armed parties in boats were attempting to find the shark or sharks involved.  
**Assessment:** The 1916 New Jersey shark-attack sequence was a real public emergency, but the reporting **did not identify an individual shark beyond doubt**; a single culprit, species identity, and later sensational retellings should not be treated as forensic findings from this article.

**HN-402 — July 23, 1916 — Preparedness Day parade bombing triggers immediate radical-plot theories**  
**Source:** *Daily Tribune* (Terre Haute), p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DT19160723.1.1).  
**Report:** A bomb exploded during a **San Francisco preparedness parade**, killing and injuring spectators. The headlines linked the attack to earlier threats of direct action against the pro-military demonstration; later papers circulated suspect names and rewards.  
**Assessment:** The attack occurred, but the first attribution of responsibility to political radicals was **an investigative theory**, not a verdict. The subsequent, deeply contested **Tom Mooney case** requires separate court and pardon records; this entry does **not** identify the bomb's perpetrator. **Follow-up open**.

**HN-403 — July 31, 1916 — Black Tom ammunition depot explodes near New York Harbor**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19160731.2.7); [National Park Service history](https://www.nps.gov/articles/black-tom-wwi.htm).  
**Report:** Explosions destroyed ammunition stores on **Black Tom Island near Jersey City**, smashed windows for miles, and caused fires. The first dispatch guessed **$25 million in damage**, reported **three confirmed deaths and at least five missing**, and acknowledged that casualty lists could not yet be checked.  
**Assessment:** The National Park Service identifies the event as **deliberate wartime sabotage** of munitions bound for the Allies. The initial loss estimates, missing-person counts, and speculation in the paper should **not** be mistaken for later verified totals or an identification of individual saboteurs in this first report.

### 1917

**HN-404 — March 1, 1917 — German proposal to Mexico emerges in the Zimmermann Telegram**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN19170301-01.1.1); [U.S. State Department history](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1914-1920/wwi).  
**Report:** An extra-edition headline said Washington was startled by a German effort to involve **Mexico and Japan in a conflict with the United States**; it described shifting congressional support for President Wilson. Some subheadings amplified the episode into a broad plan for world domination.  
**Assessment:** The State Department documents the **authentic January 1917 Zimmermann Telegram**, intercepted and decrypted by Britain. The proposal sought a **German-Mexican alliance if the United States entered the war**; sensational assertions about Germany's entire imagined world-conquest program should be distinguished from the message's actual terms.

**HN-405 — July 4, 1917 — East St. Louis killings reported with an uncertain death toll**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19170704.2.20); [Library of Congress NAACP exhibition](https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/naacp/founding-and-early-years.html).  
**Report:** A short notice said violent attacks and fires devastated Black neighborhoods in **East St. Louis, Illinois**, offering **100 or more deaths as conjecture** because burned structures could conceal victims. Its opening framed the event around a Black suspect's alleged killing of a white officer.  
**Assessment:** The event was a **racist massacre**, not simply a balanced altercation. The Library of Congress documents **48 killed** in its account, while contemporary activists and other studies offer higher disputed estimates. The article itself labels the larger immediate figure conjectural; it does **not** establish the ultimate total or justify attacks on Black residents.

**HN-406 — August 24, 1917 — Houston military violence reported through prejudicial dispatches**  
**Source:** *Hammond Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LCT19170824.1.1); [U.S. Army's 2023 case review](https://www.army.mil/article/271614/army_sets_aside_convictions_of_110_black_soldiers_convicted_in_1917_houston_riots).  
**Report:** A United Press dispatch reported deadly clashes involving Black soldiers of the **24th Infantry** and civilians in **Houston**, with the preliminary toll reaching **15**. The language characterized the soldiers as rioters and gave relatively little space to preceding assaults and racial provocations.  
**Assessment:** The Army's later review states **19 people died** after troops faced months of racist abuse, including violent arrests of two Black soldiers. It found that subsequent trials were **fundamentally unfair** and in **2023 set aside the convictions of 110 soldiers**. The first dispatch's framing is **not** a neutral finding on culpability.

**HN-407 — December 8, 1917 — Halifax explosion: deaths and even the second ship misreported**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19171208.2.17); [Nova Scotia Archives](https://archives.novascotia.ca/halifax-explosion/).  
**Report:** The newspaper estimated **2,000 dead and 3,000 injured** after the French munitions ship ***Mont-Blanc*** exploded in **Halifax Harbor**. Its account incorrectly identified the colliding vessel as a Belgian relief ship named ***Ionia*** and located and timed parts of the collision inconsistently.  
**Assessment:** The other vessel was the ***Imo***, a **Norwegian** ship. Nova Scotia Archives maintains a **name-based record of 1,782 known victims**; broader historical estimates near 2,000 are common, so the newspaper's rounded toll is not necessarily a categorical falsehood. The vessel's identification, however, is demonstrably wrong.

**HN-408 — December 15, 1917 — Thirteen Houston soldiers hanged after military courts-martial**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19171215.2.33); [U.S. Army review](https://www.army.mil/article/271614/army_sets_aside_convictions_of_110_black_soldiers_convicted_in_1917_houston_riots).  
**Report:** A national news digest announced that **13 Black soldiers** convicted over the August Houston events had been **hanged at Fort Sam Houston**, with officials present.  
**Assessment:** The executions occurred, but **execution is not evidence of a fair adjudication**. The Army says **19 soldiers were ultimately executed**, the first 13 in secrecy without higher review; it then changed execution-review rules. The **2023 decision vacated all 110 related convictions**, explicitly citing racial injustice and unfair military trials.

### 1918

**HN-409 — July 9, 1918 — Two passenger trains collide head-on near Nashville**  
**Source:** *Daily Tribune* (Terre Haute), p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DT19180709.1.1); [Tennessee legislative historical resolution](https://capitol.tn.gov/Bills/106/Bill/SJR0135.pdf).  
**Report:** An urgent dispatch said **about 100 people were killed** and dozens injured when two **Nashville, Chattanooga and St. Louis Railway** passenger trains collided near Belle Meade. It described crushed coaches, fire, and overwhelmed rescue teams.  
**Assessment:** The **Dutchman's Curve** wreck happened. Tennessee's commemorative record cites **101 dead**, a count it also notes has been challenged over time. The newspaper's figure is **preliminary but close**; purported causes in first dispatches are not substitutes for complete railroad investigations.

**HN-410 — October 4, 1918 — Public venues close as influenza overwhelms hospitals**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 27 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN19181004-01.1.27); [CDC pandemic history](https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/flu/pandemic-resources/1918-commemoration/1918-pandemic-history.htm).  
**Report:** Under a headline about closing public places, the paper described **crowded hospitals, shortages of medical staff, and thousands of workers absent** at the Hog Island shipyard as influenza spread. Restricting gatherings was becoming an emergency public-health tool.  
**Assessment:** This is an authentic report of the **1918 influenza pandemic**, later estimated by CDC to have caused roughly **675,000 U.S. deaths and at least 50 million worldwide**. The specific shipyard absence figure is a **contemporary operational report**, not a verified census of infections. There was no influenza vaccine then.

**HN-411 — October 14, 1918 — Cloquet forest fire described as possible enemy sabotage**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19181014.2.10); [Minnesota Historical Society's MNopedia collection](https://www.mnhs.org/mnopedia/search/index/thing/forest-fires).  
**Report:** The account feared **500 dead**, described towns and rail routes consumed by the **Cloquet–Moose Lake fires**, and repeated a wartime rumor that **enemy agents** had caused the devastation. It explicitly conceded **no confirmation** of the sabotage rumor.  
**Assessment:** Minnesota Historical Society gives **at least 453 deaths**. The wartime spy explanation was **speculation**, not a demonstrated origin. Drought, combustible landscapes, and railroad-related ignitions are central to later historical accounts. **Do not promote the paper's unconfirmed incendiary allegation to fact.**

**HN-412 — December 27, 1918 — Mexican influenza tragedy filtered through border travelers' accounts**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19181227.2.28); [CDC global 1918 pandemic background](https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/flu/pandemic-resources/1918-commemoration/1918-pandemic-history.htm).  
**Report:** Travelers reaching **Laredo, Texas**, described overwhelmed undertakers in Mexico and alleged **40,000 influenza cases in Mexico City by mid-October**, with pneumonia and deaths among poor households. The headline called it the **most fatal epidemic in Mexico's history**.  
**Assessment:** The pandemic and severe social disruption are credible in context. This Indiana paper reprinted **secondhand traveler testimony**, not a Mexican national mortality register. Its **40,000 figure counts alleged cases, not deaths**, and the sweeping claim that this was the country's worst epidemic remains **unverified by this source**.

### 1919

**HN-413 — January 16, 1919 — Boston molasses tank ruptures, engulfing streets**  
**Source:** *South Bend News-Times*, p. 10 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19190116.1.10); [Smithsonian history](https://www.smithsonianmag.com/history/without-warning-molasses-january-surged-over-boston-180971251/).  
**Report:** A waterfront storage tank belonging to Purity Distilling burst, releasing an estimated **two million gallons of molasses** into Boston's North End. The first account said **perhaps a dozen died**, identified only a few victims, and described shattered buildings, damaged rail tracks, and a trolley car thrown off its tracks.  
**Assessment:** The **Great Molasses Flood** was real; the established final toll was **21 dead and about 150 injured**. The initial count was incomplete. The disaster arose from industrial tank failure, not a mysterious natural wave or unexplained explosion independent of the storage system.

**HN-414 — February 6, 1919 — Seattle general strike halts much of the city's work**  
**Source:** *South Bend News-Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19190206.1.1); [University of Washington labor-history project](https://depts.washington.edu/labhist/strike/).  
**Report:** As shipyard workers sought higher wages, Seattle unions began a **citywide sympathy strike**. The dispatch described stopped streetcars, curtailed power and other services, while essential functions and some newspaper work continued.  
**Assessment:** The five-day **Seattle General Strike** involved roughly **60,000 workers**, according to the University of Washington's historical project. Contemporary suggestions that it must be an attempted Bolshevik overthrow **are political interpretations**, not what the existence of a mass labor strike alone proves.

**HN-415 — July 30, 1919 — Chicago race violence reaches a provisional toll of 27**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19190730.2.9); [Illinois State Archives coroner report](https://www.ilsos.gov/departments/archives/online-exhibits/100-documents/1919-coroner-race-riot.html).  
**Report:** A dispatch on violent attacks and retaliation in Chicago said **27 people had died**, described serious injuries, troops standing by, and disturbances in Black and other neighborhoods. Its headline portrayed the entire city as descending into disorder.  
**Assessment:** The **1919 Chicago race riot** followed the death of **Eugene Williams**, a Black teenager attacked at a segregated Lake Michigan swimming area. The later accepted total is **38 dead**, with **23 Black and 15 white victims**. Much of the violence targeted Black Chicagoans; the first report's 27 was provisional.

**HN-416 — August 1, 1919 — Chicago officials pronounce riot ended as toll rises to 32**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium and Sun-Telegram*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19190801.1.2); [Illinois State Archives coroner report](https://www.ilsos.gov/departments/archives/online-exhibits/100-documents/1919-coroner-race-riot.html).  
**Report:** This later Associated Press account placed the toll at **32 dead—18 Black and 14 white—and more than 300 injured** after troops took control. It said officials considered the violence over.  
**Assessment:** This is a useful **same-event correction** to HN-415, not a second riot. The ultimate **38 deaths** demonstrate that even the later official-looking figure was not final. A government declaration that order had returned does not resolve the responsibility for the attacks.

**HN-417 — September 19, 1919 — Wilson tries to avert nationwide steel strike**  
**Source:** *Hammond Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=LCT19190919.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper said President **Woodrow Wilson** had urged U.S. Steel chairman **Elbert H. Gary** to meet workers' representatives. The company refused as the large steel-industry stoppage approached; the local paper also discussed union strike benefits.  
**Assessment:** This captures negotiations **before the September 22 national steel strike**, not a completed settlement or proof that one party alone caused the conflict. The subsequent strike and bitter suppression are historical events; company and union assertions in a breaking labor dispatch require attribution.

**HN-418 — November 8, 1919 — Justice Department raids alleged radicals in 18 cities**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis News*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INN19191108-01.1.1).  
**Report:** The article relayed Attorney General **A. Mitchell Palmer's** announcement that **more than 200 people** were held following coordinated federal raids in **18 cities**. Officials claimed to have seized rifles, flags, pamphlets, and bomb-making materials and sought deportations.  
**Assessment:** Arrest totals, seized items, and official claims of radical conspiracy **do not establish each detainee's guilt**. These were part of the **First Red Scare and Palmer Raids**; the article primarily reproduces the prosecuting government's perspective, with civil-liberties and warrant issues requiring further review.

**HN-419 — December 3, 1919 — Senator Newberry and campaign workers indicted**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19191203.2.9); [Supreme Court decision, *Newberry v. United States*](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/256/232/).  
**Report:** A wire digest reported a federal grand jury indicting Michigan senator **Truman H. Newberry**, **14 leading campaign workers**, and **120 others** on alleged 1918 election-spending, conspiracy, and mail-law violations.  
**Assessment:** This is a record of **indictment, not conviction**. A jury did subsequently convict Newberry (HN-420), but the Supreme Court **reversed his conviction in 1921** (HN-425). Treating the 1919 accusation as his final legal status would be wrong.

### 1920

**HN-420 — March 24, 1920 — Newberry receives prison term and fine after election trial**  
**Source:** *Jasper County Democrat*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JCDC19200324.2.14); [*Newberry v. United States*](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/256/232/).  
**Report:** Following a March 20 jury verdict, Newberry was sentenced to **two years in federal prison and a $10,000 fine** over campaign spending; his lawyers sought new proceedings. The report also described convictions of other defendants.  
**Assessment:** The verdict and sentence occurred, **but were not the final judgment**. The U.S. Supreme Court overturned the federal conviction in **May 1921**, principally over limits on federal regulation of primary-election expenditures. This entry must always be read with HN-425.

**HN-421 — August 18, 1920 — Tennessee ratifies Nineteenth Amendment**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19200818.1.1); [National Archives history](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/19th-amendment).  
**Report:** A front-page headline announced that the **Tennessee legislature had ratified women's suffrage**, completing the required three-fourths of state approvals.  
**Assessment:** **August 18** was the decisive ratification date; the federal proclamation followed on **August 26** (HN-422). The constitutional prohibition on sex-based voting exclusion did not by itself remove **racial disenfranchisement, citizenship exclusions, and other barriers** women faced in practice.

**HN-422 — August 27, 1920 — Newspaper reports federal certification of women's suffrage**  
**Source:** *Evening Republican*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=EVRP19200827.2.8); [National Archives history](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/19th-amendment).  
**Report:** A dispatch said Secretary of State **Bainbridge Colby** signed the proclamation giving legal effect to the **Nineteenth Amendment**, after Tennessee's certificate arrived in Washington. It claimed the change extended the franchise to **27 million women**.  
**Assessment:** The National Archives records certification on **August 26, 1920**; the item was **printed August 27**. The headline's sweeping language did not mean that all American women could actually cast ballots without discrimination, nor does the 27-million estimate establish newly enfranchised voters.

**HN-423 — September 16, 1920 — Wall Street blast initially mistaken for possible vehicle accident**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium and Sun-Telegram*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19200916.1.1); [FBI case history](https://www.fbi.gov/history/cases-and-criminals/wall-street-bombing-1920).  
**Report:** A noon explosion shattered New York's financial district, damaged the **J. P. Morgan building**, and left many dead and wounded. The first large headline called the cause **mysterious** and reported a police theory that **a dynamite-laden truck had collided**; a federal investigator reportedly discounted a bombing theory.  
**Assessment:** The FBI identifies this as the deliberate **Wall Street bombing of 1920**. The accident theory was a **rapidly discarded initial hypothesis**. The perpetrator was **never conclusively identified**; later theories do not make any named group judicially proven responsible.

**HN-424 — September 17, 1920 — Wall Street coverage revises the toll and identifies a bomb**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19200917.1.1); [FBI case history](https://www.fbi.gov/history/cases-and-criminals/wall-street-bombing-1920).  
**Report:** The follow-up headline put the **bomb death toll at 38** and damage at **$2.5 million**, a change from the previous day's accident speculation.  
**Assessment:** This is a **revision of HN-423, not a separate explosion**. The FBI confirms a deadly bomb attack but describes the case as **unsolved**. Preliminary casualty and financial damage figures should remain labeled as newspaper figures, not independently audited final totals.

### 1921

**HN-425 — May 2, 1921 — Supreme Court sets aside Newberry conviction**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19210502.1.1); [*Newberry v. United States*, 256 U.S. 232](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/256/232/).  
**Report:** The front page announced that the U.S. Supreme Court had cleared Senator **Truman H. Newberry** of his federal election conviction, declaring the statute underpinning the prosecution invalid as applied.  
**Assessment:** The judgment **reversed the conviction and sentence** described in HN-420. It does not mean every allegation about campaign finance was affirmatively disproved, only that **this criminal conviction did not stand**. Such distinctions are essential when following sensational criminal-political reporting.

**HN-426 — June 2, 1921 — Tulsa attack reported as 'race riot' with nearly 100 dead**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19210602.1.1); [National Park Service historical account](https://www.nps.gov/people/olivia-hooker.htm).  
**Report:** A dispatch from **Tulsa, Oklahoma**, announced martial law and a grand-jury investigation after an alleged **nearly 100 deaths** and massive property damage. The newspaper used the contemporary **'race riot'** terminology, describing authorities attempting to assign blame.  
**Assessment:** This was the **Tulsa Race Massacre**: white attackers destroyed much of **Greenwood**, an affluent Black district, and killed Black residents. Casualty estimates remain contested and no first-day figure is definitive. The NPS recounts both the early low official count and later higher estimates, and the underlying attack must not be presented as two equally situated mobs.

**HN-427 — June 9, 1921 — Later Tulsa syndication alleges 75–150 deaths**  
**Source:** *Walkerton Independent*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19210609.1.2); [National Park Service historical account](https://www.nps.gov/people/olivia-hooker.htm).  
**Report:** A reprinted dispatch dated June 3 estimated **75 to 150 dead**, **84 blocks burned**, and **thousands of Black residents homeless**, attributing the start to a confrontation over a Black youth's arrest.  
**Assessment:** The estimates demonstrate how **uncertain and politically mediated** the first reports were. This is **not an independently verified count**, and the arrests narrative cannot justify destruction of Greenwood. Modern investigations distinguish **established mass violence** from uncertain individual casualty tallies.

**HN-428 — July 15, 1921 — Sacco and Vanzetti found guilty of Massachusetts murders**  
**Source:** *South Bend News-Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19210715.1.1).  
**Report:** A brief wire item said a jury had convicted **Nicola Sacco and Bartolomeo Vanzetti** of first-degree murder in the 1920 **South Braintree payroll robbery**, following about five hours of deliberation.  
**Assessment:** The **1921 convictions** and **1927 executions** are historical facts, but evidence and fairness of the proceedings have been vigorously disputed. Neither the headline nor later political mythology, on either side, constitutes independent forensic proof of who killed the victims. **Follow-up open** for trial and appellate records.

**HN-429 — September 1, 1921 — Armed coal miners cross toward Blair Mountain**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium and Sun-Telegram*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19210901.1.4); [National Park Service history](https://home.nps.gov/articles/000/the-battle-of-blair-mountain.htm/index.htm).  
**Report:** The report describes **armed miners** advancing near **Blair Mountain and Crooked Creek, West Virginia**, while opposing patrols moved to block them and both sides reported casualties. Vehicles carried reinforcements and ammunition along the Logan–Blair road.  
**Assessment:** The **Battle of Blair Mountain** was a real peak of the West Virginia Mine Wars, involving unionizing coal workers and armed mine/company-aligned forces. Federal troops helped end the conflict. Broad-brush portrayals of the miners simply as criminal 'insurrectionists' obscure the coalfield labor struggle; competing battle casualty claims need individual corroboration.

### 1922

**HN-430 — January 30, 1922 — Knickerbocker Theatre roof collapse reported as 107 dead**  
**Source:** *South Bend News-Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=SBNT19220130.1.1); [National Weather Service retrospective](https://www.weather.gov/media/lwx/reporter/winter2003.pdf).  
**Report:** Following a heavy Washington, D.C., snowfall, the roof of the **Knickerbocker Theatre** collapsed onto its audience. The newspaper led with **107 on the casualty list**, while reports from the wreckage described ongoing rescues, including children pulled out alive.  
**Assessment:** The federal weather retrospective records **98 dead and 158 injured**. The original headline reflects an **unstable emergency tally**, not evidence for 107 confirmed deaths. A heavy snow load and structural failure explain the disaster without the need for sabotage theories.

**HN-431 — May 1, 1922 — Senate opens inquiry into Fall's naval oil-reserve leases**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19220501.1.7).  
**Report:** The paper reported a **59–0 Senate vote** authorizing inquiry into Interior Secretary **Albert B. Fall's** leasing of U.S. naval petroleum reserves to private firms, highlighting controversy around **Teapot Dome**.  
**Assessment:** This is evidence of an **investigation starting**, not a corruption verdict already established in May 1922. Later trials and Fall's bribery conviction transformed the investigation into an established major scandal. His specific criminal outcome and lease history merit their own court-document check; see also HN-436.

**HN-432 — June 23, 1922 — Herrin mining massacre reported amid conflicting death counts**  
**Source:** *Richmond Palladium and Sun-Telegram*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19220623.1.1).  
**Report:** An Associated Press report from **Herrin, Illinois**, described the killing of strikebreaking mine workers during violent confrontations involving thousands of armed miners and supporters. Its headline called the killings a **massacre**, and its dispatch said **at least 27 dead** while noting no immediate formal investigation or arrests.  
**Assessment:** The **Herrin Massacre** is a documented labor-violence atrocity; accounts vary on the final count and identities of participants. The reporter's figure, quoted admissions, and accusations against particular people are **contemporary claims** unless confirmed in subsequent proceedings. **Follow-up open** on the verified victim register and court outcomes.

### 1923

**HN-433 — January 5, 1923 — Reports from Rosewood give incompatible early death tolls**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19230105.1.1); *Richmond Palladium and Sun-Telegram*, p. 1 — [contemporaneous account](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=RPD19230105.1.1); [Florida State Archives survivor testimony](https://floridamemory.com/items/show/350844).  
**Report:** One first-day wire report asserted **22 Black and white people had been killed** in 'race riots' at **Rosewood, Florida**. Another, citing reports from the nearby town of Sumner, said a mob had **burned almost the entire village**. Both accounts were composed while violence and flight continued.  
**Assessment:** The Florida State Archives documents a **racially motivated white-mob massacre and destruction of Rosewood's Black community**. The **22 figure was an early, unverified estimate**, not a verified victim list. Labeling the event a generic, symmetrical 'race riot' conceals who was targeted; later survivor accounts are essential.

**HN-434 — August 3, 1923 — Harding dies; Coolidge becomes president**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19230803.1.1).  
**Report:** The newspaper announced **'Coolidge President; Harding Mourned'** following President **Warren G. Harding's** unexpected death in **San Francisco**. A wire story suggested a sudden stroke after an illness earlier described in terms of food poisoning, indigestion, and pneumonia.  
**Assessment:** Harding **died August 2, 1923** and Vice President **Calvin Coolidge** succeeded him. The new president's accession was constitutional, not evidence that political rumor or later controversy over Harding's administration caused his death. The dispatch's shifting medical explanation is **contemporary reporting**, not a final independent pathology finding.

**HN-435 — September 6, 1923 — Japan earthquake dispatch claims 250,000 deaths**  
**Source:** *Walkerton Independent*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=WLKI19230906.1.1); [Government of Japan's disaster review](https://www.bousai.go.jp/en/documentation/white_paper/pdf/2023/SF1-1.pdf).  
**Report:** The paper repeated dispatches from Osaka and Shanghai claiming **250,000 Japanese people dead**, with devastated **Tokyo and Yokohama**, firestorms, collapsed buildings, stories of refugees trapped, and additional uncorroborated reports of vanished islands or military facilities.  
**Assessment:** The **Great Kantō earthquake occurred September 1, 1923**. Japan's Cabinet Office now reports about **105,000 dead or missing**, not the dispatch's 250,000 deaths. This is an especially stark illustration of wartime-style disaster rumor and premature mass-casualty extrapolation; the fires were indeed a major killer, but not every extraordinary eyewitness anecdote was verified.

### 1924

**HN-436 — January 26, 1924 — Teapot Dome loan revelation contradicts Fall's denial**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19240126.1.4).  
**Report:** An editorial juxtaposed Interior Secretary **Albert B. Fall's** earlier statement that he received no money connected with the oil leases against **E. L. Doheny's testimony** that he had advanced Fall **$100,000** in 1921, supposedly as a ranch loan.  
**Assessment:** The paper exposed a **concrete inconsistency in sworn and written accounts**, but an editorial juxtaposition is not itself a criminal judgment. Later Teapot Dome proceedings established serious corruption, and Fall was convicted of taking a bribe; distinguishing the 1924 testimony from those later legal findings matters. This follows the investigation first reported in HN-431.

**HN-437 — May 26, 1924 — Restrictive immigration bill signed into law**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19240526.1.1); [National Archives immigration history](https://www.archives.gov/research/aapi/immigration).  
**Report:** The front-page headline **'Japanese Exclusion Act Is Law'** announced President **Calvin Coolidge's** signature of a sweeping new immigration restriction, particularly emphasizing exclusion of Japanese immigrants. The text includes **Coolidge's public objection to the Japanese exclusion clause** while signing the broader bill for its other provisions; the exclusion was set to take effect **July 1**.  
**Assessment:** The **Immigration Act of 1924** established a national-origins quota based on **2% of each nationality's U.S. population in the 1890 census** and excluded immigrants ineligible for naturalization, including Japanese citizens. Coolidge's stated regret **does not undo the exclusionary statute he signed**. The period's anti-immigrant claims are not established by their appearance in news coverage.

**HN-438 — June 4, 1924 — Leopold and Loeb murder case cast as evidence of a 'Jazz Age' pathology**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19240604.1.6).  
**Report:** The paper described **Nathan Leopold Jr. and Richard Loeb** as confessed killers of **14-year-old Robert 'Bobby' Franks**, while an illustrated feature blamed or psychoanalyzed the pair as 'products of the Jazz Age.'  
**Assessment:** The **Franks murder** and the defendants' admissions were real, but broad sociological theories about modern youth **are journalistic interpretation, not findings of clinical causation**. The later court disposition is recorded in HN-439; claims of additional planned crimes would require independent corroboration.

**HN-439 — September 10, 1924 — Leopold and Loeb avoid the death penalty**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19240910.1.1).  
**Report:** A prominent front-page headline declared **'Leopold and Loeb Get Life'** as the Chicago court sentenced the defendants following the Franks murder proceedings.  
**Assessment:** The judge imposed **life imprisonment for murder plus a 99-year sentence for kidnapping**, rather than death. The verdict is an actual **court outcome**, unlike earlier speculation about motive or psychiatric causation (HN-438). This entry is not a blanket endorsement of the contemporaneous sensational coverage.

### 1925

**HN-440 — February 3, 1925 — Nome antitoxin arrival turns sled dog Balto into a hero**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19250203.1.2).  
**Report:** A dispatch said **Gunnar Kaasen's team**, led by **Balto**, brought **300,000 units of diphtheria antitoxin** to **Nome, Alaska**, despite dangerous winter conditions. It celebrated Balto as leader of the 'race of mercy.'  
**Assessment:** The emergency **1925 serum relay** happened and helped address a diphtheria threat. Crucially, **multiple mushers and dog teams carried the antitoxin in successive stages**; Balto led the last team but did not make the entire journey alone. The first victory narrative is incomplete without the relay's other participants.

**HN-441 — March 19, 1925 — Tri-State Tornado early reports exceed 1,000 deaths**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19250319.1.1); *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [second original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19250319.1.1); [National Weather Service study](https://www.weather.gov/pah/1925tornado).  
**Report:** Within a day of the massive storm, one newspaper predicted **1,000 deaths** and another claimed **more than 1,000** overall with **nearly 200 killed in southern Indiana**. The dispatches described destroyed schools, homes, towns, and fires among the wreckage.  
**Assessment:** The **March 18 Tri-State Tornado**, passing through **Missouri, Illinois, and Indiana**, killed **695 people**, of whom **71 died in Indiana**, according to the National Weather Service. The contemporaneous projections significantly **overstated both totals**. The disaster itself and its extraordinary magnitude are firmly documented.

**HN-442 — April 18, 1925 — Indiana former Klan leader charged in Madge Oberholtzer case**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19250418.1.1); [Indiana Historical Bureau](https://www.in.gov/history/state-historical-markers/find-a-marker/indianapolis-times/).  
**Report:** The front page announced **D. C. Stephenson was charged with murder** in the death of **Madge Oberholtzer**; it discussed the ex-Klan leader's house, related fire inquiry, other defendants, and court pleas.  
**Assessment:** On **April 18**, these were **criminal charges**, not yet a verdict. The later historical and court record establishes Stephenson's **second-degree murder conviction** and **life sentence in November** (HN-445). Reporting this sequence separately prevents the subsequent outcome from being falsely read back into the indictment.

**HN-443 — July 21, 1925 — Scopes convicted and fined in evolution-teaching trial**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19250721.1.1); [Tennessee Encyclopedia case history](https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/the-scopes-trial/).  
**Report:** The paper's headline said **John T. Scopes was found guilty and fined $100** for teaching human evolution contrary to Tennessee's Butler Act. The prosecution and defense turned the case into a highly publicized debate over education and religious orthodoxy.  
**Assessment:** The **1925 conviction** happened; the Tennessee Supreme Court later **reversed the fine on a sentencing technicality**, while leaving the underlying statute standing at that time. The case did **not** establish that evolution was scientifically false or constitutionally unteachable nationwide.

**HN-444 — September 3, 1925 — Naval airship *Shenandoah* tears apart in storm**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19250903.1.1); [U.S. Navy loss history](https://www.history.navy.mil/research/library/online-reading-room/title-list-alphabetically/u/us-navy-ships-lost-in-selected-storm-weather-related-incidents.html).  
**Report:** The main headline reported **'Shenandoah Splits in Sky; 14 Die'** after the U.S. Navy rigid airship came apart over **Ohio**. Early dispatches and photographs emphasized the extraordinary destruction and crew casualties.  
**Assessment:** Navy records confirm **14 crew killed and two injured** in the **September 3 storm-related loss**. Questions about command decisions and the flight route were subjects for a separate inquiry; nothing in this breaking report demonstrates sabotage.

**HN-445 — November 16, 1925 — Stephenson sentenced to life in Oberholtzer murder**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19251116.1.1); [Indiana Historical Bureau](https://www.in.gov/history/state-historical-markers/find-a-marker/indianapolis-times/).  
**Report:** The newspaper reported that **D. C. Stephenson**, previously a powerful Indiana Ku Klux Klan leader, had been **found guilty by a jury of second-degree murder** and **sentenced to life imprisonment** for the killing of **Madge Oberholtzer**. Related coverage described proceedings involving his associates.  
**Assessment:** Unlike April's **charge** (HN-442), this was an **established conviction and sentence**, confirmed by Indiana's official historical records. The *Indianapolis Times*' investigation of Klan-linked influence later became part of the political scandal's aftermath; the trial verdict does not establish every accusation against every person in the surrounding reports.

### 1926

**HN-446 — June 23, 1926 — Aimee Semple McPherson resurfaces after disappearance**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19260623.1.1); [PBS *American Experience* case history](https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/americanexperience/features/sister-mcpherson-trial/).  
**Report:** The front page announced that evangelist **Aimee Semple McPherson**, missing for weeks, had been found alive near the Mexican border. Her mother recounted McPherson's telephone explanation that kidnappers had held her in Mexico before she escaped.  
**Assessment:** Her **return was real**; the alleged kidnapping was a **contested account**, not a fact established by the headline. Investigators later alleged the disappearance was staged, but the prosecution did not prove that theory and dropped its case. Neither the kidnapping story nor its denial can be certified from this first report.

**HN-447 — September 17, 1926 — Prosecutors allege McPherson kidnapping was a hoax**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19260917.1.1); [PBS *American Experience* case history](https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/americanexperience/features/sister-mcpherson-trial/).  
**Report:** The paper described a judge's alleged connection with a supposed **McPherson kidnapping hoax** as authorities sought to build a criminal case against the evangelist and associates.  
**Assessment:** The reporting documents the **prosecution's allegations and investigative stage**, not a criminal finding. Charges were later **dropped**, leaving the competing accounts unresolved. Pair with the appearance report in **HN-446** rather than letting either sensational headline supply the final answer.

**HN-448 — September 20, 1926 — Miami hurricane devastation spreads beyond initial reports**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19260920.1.1); [National Weather Service historical report](https://www.weather.gov/mfl/miami_hurricane).  
**Report:** The newspaper called the Florida hurricane a growing catastrophe as communications and transport failed, and relatives in Indiana waited for news from people in the affected region.  
**Assessment:** The **September 18 Great Miami Hurricane** caused widespread destruction. The National Weather Service cites the **October 9 Red Cross total of 372 deaths and over 6,000 injuries**, a more grounded retrospective count than the rapidly changing first dispatches. The storm did not occur on the paper's September 20 publication date.

**HN-449 — September 22, 1926 — Hurricane toll printed as 370**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19260922.1.1); [National Weather Service historical report](https://www.weather.gov/mfl/miami_hurricane).  
**Report:** Four days after the Miami landfall, the paper announced that the hurricane death toll **stood at 370**, while the region continued recovering survivors and accounting for the missing.  
**Assessment:** Unlike some wildly inflated catastrophe dispatches, this interim number was **close to the later Red Cross report of 372**. This comparison still does not certify that every person had been identified or that all estimates across affected places used the same geographic boundary.

**HN-450 — December 24, 1926 — Southern Railway trains collide near Rockmart**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19261224.1.1).  
**Report:** A collision between Southern Railway trains ***Royal Palm* and *Ponce de Leon*** near **Rockmart, Georgia**, produced a headline claiming **17 killed and 36 injured**, while an official quoted in the same report initially gave **16 killed and 29 injured**.  
**Assessment:** The article itself gives **conflicting early counts**, a warning not to treat the most prominent figure as the finalized toll. A definitive independent casualty roster and the official cause investigation have **not been established in this pass**; follow-up open.

### 1927

**HN-451 — April 18, 1927 — Mississippi floods already displace thousands**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19270418.1.1); [National Weather Service Mississippi River flood history](https://www.weather.gov/safety/flood-states-ms).  
**Report:** During the expanding Mississippi River floods, an early dispatch put the homeless at roughly **22,000** and emphasized uncertainty about deaths as levees and tributaries failed.  
**Assessment:** The **Great Mississippi Flood of 1927** became a much larger catastrophe: the National Weather Service records **246 deaths and more than 700,000 people left homeless**. The April 18 article captures an early stage of a months-long crisis, not its full toll or the displacement and racial inequities in relief that followed.

**HN-452 — May 18, 1927 — Bath school bombing first reaches the front page**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19270518.1.1); [Bath School Museum historical account](https://bathschoolmuseum.org/bombing/).  
**Report:** The newspaper announced that a **school building had been dynamited** in **Bath Township, Michigan**, as rescue crews searched wreckage and details about the attack emerged.  
**Assessment:** This was a deliberate bombing carried out on **May 18 by Andrew Kehoe**, not an accidental explosion. The breaking story precedes more complete casualty reporting; the museum documents **38 pupils, four school employees, and one bystander killed in the bombing**, a total of **43 victims** apart from deaths counted differently in wider descriptions of Kehoe's crimes.

**HN-453 — May 19, 1927 — Bath death toll reported as 43**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19270519.1.1); [Bath School Museum historical account](https://bathschoolmuseum.org/bombing/).  
**Report:** Follow-up coverage said the death toll had **mounted to 43**, identifying school official **Andrew Kehoe** as responsible as families mourned and investigators inspected the site.  
**Assessment:** The museum's **43 bombing victims** matches this widely cited count; some other totals **include Kehoe or his wife**, whom he had killed separately, and thus use different counting rules. Those numbers should not be presented as direct contradictions without stating the population counted. See **HN-452**.

**HN-454 — May 23, 1927 — Lindbergh honored after solo Atlantic flight**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19270523.1.1); [Smithsonian National Air and Space Museum collection](https://airandspace.si.edu/collection-objects/spirit-st-louis/nasm_A19280021000).  
**Report:** Paris honored **Charles Lindbergh** with the French **Legion of Honor** following his nonstop transatlantic arrival in the ***Spirit of St. Louis***.  
**Assessment:** His **May 20–21 New York-to-Paris journey** was the first **solo nonstop transatlantic flight**, not the first nonstop aerial crossing of the Atlantic by anyone. Contemporary celebration sometimes compresses this distinction; **Alcock and Brown** flew nonstop in **1919**.

**HN-455 — August 23, 1927 — Sacco and Vanzetti executed after years of appeals**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 5 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19270823.1.5).  
**Report:** The paper reported the executions of **Nicola Sacco and Bartolomeo Vanzetti** in Massachusetts after their murder convictions and prolonged worldwide campaign over the fairness of their trials.  
**Assessment:** The **executions occurred**, but the coverage should not erase the **enduring disputes about trial fairness, prejudice, and the underlying evidence**. A criminal judgment and a later historical evaluation are different kinds of evidence. This closes the execution chronology of the **1921 conviction reported at HN-428**, not the debate about guilt.

### 1928

**HN-456 — March 13, 1928 — St. Francis Dam collapse inundates California valley**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19280313.1.1); [U.S. Geological Survey history](https://www.usgs.gov/centers/california-water-science-center/californias-history-large-storms-floods).  
**Report:** The newspaper headlined **scores drowned when a dam broke**, describing catastrophic floodwater racing downriver after the **St. Francis Dam** failed north of Los Angeles.  
**Assessment:** The **March 12–13 collapse** is firmly documented. The U.S. Geological Survey cites **at least 431 deaths**; early vague or smaller casualty claims undercounted victims. The disaster prompted examination of dam design, geology, and construction conditions rather than supplying evidence of a deliberate attack.

**HN-457 — March 16, 1928 — Dam disaster dispatch alleges looting amid recovery**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19280316.1.3); [U.S. Geological Survey history](https://www.usgs.gov/centers/california-water-science-center/californias-history-large-storms-floods).  
**Report:** As crews buried flood victims, the paper described **275–300 bodies** and printed claims of **stolen possessions**, disease fears, and orders to shoot suspected grave robbers on sight.  
**Assessment:** The initial **275–300 count was incomplete** compared with **at least 431 deaths** later documented. The story's **looting allegations and claimed security orders** are evidence of what was reported during the crisis, not proof that each alleged act occurred or that any killing under such an order was justified.

**HN-458 — September 19, 1928 — Okeechobee flooding overwhelms communities**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19280919.1.2); [National Weather Service historical analysis](https://www.weather.gov/mfl/okeechobee).  
**Report:** Reports described **several hundred people feared dead** after hurricane-driven water overtopped parts of the **Lake Okeechobee** perimeter, with bodies, wreckage, and urgent medical needs reported from southern Florida.  
**Assessment:** This was an early and serious **undercount**, not a minor local flood. The National Weather Service's later historical estimate is **at least 2,500 killed**, with disproportionate suffering among Black agricultural workers and settlements around the lake. The early dispatch could not yet account for isolated communities.

**HN-459 — September 24, 1928 — Newspaper estimates 2,200 Okeechobee deaths**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19280924.1.1); [National Weather Service historical analysis](https://www.weather.gov/mfl/okeechobee).
