The **Soviet declaration and attack** are another simultaneous military development, not an effect of this individual bomb. Contemporary “obliterated” language is a **sensational damage estimate**.

**HN-553 — August 14, 1945 — Japanese surrender reported before official note arrives**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19450814.1.1); [U.S. National Archives, Japanese surrender](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/surrender-of-japan).  
**Report:** The day's **fifth edition** led **“Japs Say They Give Up—But Note Fails to Arrive,”** explaining that Tokyo radio had signaled acceptance but **Washington had not yet received the awaited definitive diplomatic communication**.  
**Assessment:** The **August 14 U.S.-date announcement** (August 15 in Japan) and **September 2 signed instrument** are distinct milestones. The printed uncertainty was **real-time diplomatic lag**, not proof Japan secretly continued a settled war after formal surrender. The period newspaper's ethnic slur is preserved solely to identify the historical headline.

**HN-554 — September 3, 1945 — Paper commemorates formal Japanese surrender aboard *Missouri***  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19450903.1.7); [U.S. National Archives, Japanese Instrument of Surrender](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/surrender-of-japan).  
**Report:** The paper printed Truman's remarks and described the ceremony aboard the **USS *Missouri*** in Tokyo Bay, where Japanese representatives **signed the instrument of surrender**.  
**Assessment:** The formal ceremony took place **September 2, 1945**, even though this issue was printed September 3. The report helps distinguish **initial acceptance** (**HN-553**) from the formal instrument; surrender did not instantly resolve occupation, prisoner repatriation, or postwar justice.

**HN-555 — October 1, 1945 — Correspondent reports lingering Nagasaki radiation illness**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 8 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19451001.1.8); [National Archives, atomic bombing and aftermath](https://visit.archives.gov/whats-on/explore-exhibits/atomic-bombing-hiroshima-and-nagasaki).  
**Report:** A delayed September 24 dispatch from Nagasaki described **new cases of illness and death continuing weeks after the bomb**, citing U.S. Navy physicians and contemporary medical observations.  
**Assessment:** The article is evidence of **early recognition of delayed radiation injuries**, challenging the August 9 headline's implication of a single completed instantaneous event (**HN-552**). Its stated case-fatality percentage came from a small wartime clinical observation and must **not** be treated as a representative death rate for all exposed residents.

**HN-556 — November 30, 1945 — Jewish paper publishes scenes from Nuremberg trials**  
**Source:** *Jewish Post*, p. 40 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPOST19451130-01.1.40); [U.S. Holocaust Memorial Museum, International Military Tribunal](https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/article/international-military-tribunal-at-nuremberg).  
**Report:** The page featured **“Scenes as Top Nazis Tried for War Crimes at Nuremberg”** shortly after the principal international trial opened, alongside other reports of continuing anti-Jewish agitation.  
**Assessment:** The **International Military Tribunal began November 20, 1945**, but **judgments were not delivered until 1946**. Published courtroom images and prosecutorial accusations are not themselves final verdicts; the subsequent case outcomes and the experiences of victims require the trial record and survivor testimony.

### 1946

**HN-557 — July 1, 1946 — Atomic test targets a fleet at Bikini Atoll**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19460701-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Marshall Islands and nuclear testing](https://home.nps.gov/articles/000/marshall-islands.htm).  
**Report:** Under **“Bikini Blasted by Atom Bomb; Results Awaited,”** a dispatch reported the U.S. military's first **Operation Crossroads** detonation against a deliberately assembled target fleet, including initial estimates of three ships sunk and at least twenty damaged.  
**Assessment:** This was the **air-dropped Able test**; the separate underwater Baker test followed on July 25. First-day ship-damage claims were provisional. The testing also involved **displacement of Marshallese communities and enduring environmental consequences** largely absent from this fleet-centered article.

**HN-558 — July 25, 1946 — Underwater Bikini explosion prompts new ship-loss estimates**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19460725.1.1); [National Park Service, Marshall Islands](https://home.nps.gov/articles/000/marshall-islands.htm).  
**Report:** A headline announced an **atomic-bomb toll on ships at Bikini**, reporting destruction associated with the second test. The same front page also covered Indiana's strained mental-hospital capacity, a reminder that a page's main story is not its only news.  
**Assessment:** Baker was detonated **underwater on July 25**, unlike the July 1 airburst (**HN-557**), and spread radioactive contamination across vessels. Ship sinkings and structural damage alone do **not** describe the scale of radiation exposure, cleanup hazards, or Marshallese displacement.

**HN-559 — October 11, 1946 — Jewish press examines a limitation in the Nuremberg judgment**  
**Source:** *National Jewish Post*, p. 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPOST19461011-01.1.7); [U.S. Holocaust Memorial Museum, tribunal verdicts](https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/timeline-event/holocaust/after-1945/verdicts-international-military-tribunal).  
**Report:** The article **“Nuremberg Decision Means Just Don't Do It In Time Of War”** criticized how the tribunal's decision treated persecution of Jews, discussing the relationship between crimes against humanity and wartime acts.  
**Assessment:** On **October 1, 1946**, the International Military Tribunal convicted nineteen defendants and imposed twelve death sentences. The Jewish correspondent was making a **contemporary legal critique**, not reporting that prewar persecution was harmless or that genocide did not happen. The tribunal's jurisdictional reasoning and the subsequent evolution of international criminal law are distinct matters.

**HN-560 — December 7, 1946 — Winecoff Hotel blaze reported with an inflated first death toll**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19461207.1.1); [U.S. Fire Administration, hotel-fire history](https://www.usfa.fema.gov/downloads/pdf/statistics/v19i4.pdf).  
**Report:** **“122 Killed in Fire at Atlanta Hotel; Toll Mounts Hourly”** described trapped occupants at the Winecoff, fatal jumps from windows, and an emergency response still in progress.  
**Assessment:** Federal fire-history material records **119 deaths**, not the paper's immediate **122**. The building lacked sprinklers and had only one exit stairway. A shifting body count is a documented emergency-reporting error, not evidence that an unidentified additional three victims must have existed.

### 1947

**HN-561 — April 10, 1947 — Tornado's first regional death estimates race ahead of surveys**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19470410.1.1); [National Weather Service, Woodward tornado history](https://www.weather.gov/oun/events-19470409).  
**Report:** The front page led **“Over 100 Dead in Southwest Tornado”** following the April 9 devastation across the Texas Panhandle and northwestern Oklahoma.  
**Assessment:** Weather Bureau historical material reported **169 deaths across the larger path**, but subsequent research has distinguished **multiple tornadoes in the same storm sequence**; the NWS records **at least 107 killed in and around Woodward**, not all deaths of the larger outbreak. Geography and changing storm-track classification matter when comparing totals.

**HN-562 — April 17, 1947 — Texas City disaster proclaimed to have killed 700**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19470417.1.1); [Texas State Historical Association, Texas City disaster](https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/texas-city-disaster).  
**Report:** **“Texas City Explosion Death Toll 700”** presented a sweeping casualty estimate after the explosion of the ammonium-nitrate-laden **SS *Grandcamp*** and the subsequent devastation of the port.  
**Assessment:** The contemporary **700** headline was not a verified final toll. The Texas State Historical Association explains that the **anchor monument records 576 known dead, including 178 listed as missing**, and that an exact count may never be known; other retrospective sources give somewhat different totals. A second ship, ***High Flyer***, exploded about sixteen hours after *Grandcamp*, further complicating initial reports.

**HN-563 — April 17, 1947 — Science dispatch probes a familiar fertilizer's explosive danger**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19470417.1.2); [Texas State Historical Association, cargo and sequence](https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/texas-city-disaster).  
**Report:** **“Usually Harmless Fertilizer Touches Off Texas Disaster”** identified **ammonium nitrate** in the French ship's hold and explained how material sold for agriculture can also support extremely powerful explosions.  
**Assessment:** Identification of the cargo was a meaningful early lead; it does **not** mean all causes, cargo-handling decisions, ignition circumstances, and legal responsibilities were settled on April 17. The official historical chronology records subsequent fires, the second ship explosion, and prolonged compensation litigation.

**HN-564 — July 5, 1947 — Scientists push back on spreading 'flying saucer' stories**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19470705.1.1); [National Archives, Air Force UFO investigations](https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos).  
**Report:** **“Science Turns Jaundiced Eye on 'Flying Saucer' Stories”** described proliferating eyewitness claims and skeptical suggestions involving perceptual errors, ordinary objects, and public anxiety.  
**Assessment:** These are **competing interpretations in contemporary reporting**. Neither the sensational sightings nor the scientists' immediate generalizations independently establish the identity of each reported object. The Air Force subsequently investigated subsets of reports through formal projects; this article does not resolve them all.

**HN-565 — July 9, 1947 — Roswell wreckage reported as balloon material amid disc frenzy**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19470709.1.3); [National Archives, Roswell and Air Force reports](https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos).  
**Report:** In a roundup of supposed flying-disc recoveries and sightings, the paper carried the **Roswell military-base retraction**, describing a foil-covered object associated with a **weather-observation balloon**.  
**Assessment:** That account followed a brief, widely distributed military announcement suggesting a **flying disc** had been recovered. Later Air Force research identified **Project Mogul balloon-related material** as its explanation; this is the documented official conclusion, not blanket proof about unrelated sightings or all subsequent witness narratives.

**HN-566 — December 5, 1947 — Jewish newspaper reacts to UN partition vote amid violence**  
**Source:** *National Jewish Post*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=JPOST19471205-01.1.1); [UN General Assembly Resolution 181 (II)](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/671195?ln=en).  
**Report:** The newspaper described **celebrations after the partition tally** and reported escalating clashes in Palestine, alongside speculation about leadership of a future Jewish state.  
**Assessment:** The **November 29, 1947** UN General Assembly vote approved a **partition plan**, not the already completed creation of two states. The plan's reception was sharply contested, and violence and displacement followed. The selected **Jewish press perspective** is important primary evidence but cannot stand alone for all Palestinian and Arab experiences of the period.

### 1948

**HN-567 — January 30, 1948 — Gandhi's assassination reported in breaking bulletins**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19480130-01.1.1).  
**Report:** **“Gandhi Slain by Assassin; Bulletins India Seethes”** announced the shooting of **Mohandas K. Gandhi** in New Delhi and conveyed the immediate uncertainty and public emotion.  
**Assessment:** Gandhi was assassinated on **January 30** by **Nathuram Godse**. The initial dispatch's mood and fast-arriving claims do not establish collective guilt on the part of any group. Godse's later trial and conviction belong to a separate evidentiary record.

**HN-568 — February 3, 1948 — Hundreds arrested after assassination; implication is not conviction**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19480203.1.1).  
**Report:** **“300 Arrested for Link With Gandhi Murder”** described Indian authorities' roundup of Hindu extremist organizers and suspects after the killing.  
**Assessment:** The **300 figure is the government's reported arrest total**, not 300 people adjudicated guilty of participating in the murder. The article combines a named assassination investigation with wider security action amid post-partition communal conflict; legal guilt requires individual evidence.

**HN-569 — July 3, 1948 — Berlin blockade puts postwar Allied access in jeopardy**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19480703.1.1); [U.S. State Department, Berlin Airlift](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1945-1952/berlin-airlift).  
**Report:** The newspaper led with pressure for a **“Berlin Blockade Showdown”** while the Western powers responded to Soviet land-route restrictions and began supplying West Berlin by air.  
**Assessment:** This was a genuine Cold War access crisis, **not a conventional armed battle in Berlin**. The blockade ended **May 12, 1949**; the airlift continued beyond that point to rebuild reserves and demonstrate continued access. Early diplomatic threats must be read against the eventual timeline.

**HN-570 — October 20, 1948 — Column treats a Dewey presidency as an assumption**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 12 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19481020.1.12); [Harry S. Truman Presidential Library, 1948 election](https://www.trumanlibrary.gov/education/presidential-inquiries/election-1948).  
**Report:** A Scripps-Howard political survey began, **“The polls say Gov. Thomas E. Dewey is 'In',”** then forecast Senate contests and the legislative challenges **Dewey would face as president**.  
**Assessment:** **Harry Truman defeated Dewey in November**, demonstrating a polling/interpretive failure. This is a **particular October prediction**; it should not be confused with the separate famous *Chicago Daily Tribune* headline, **“Dewey Defeats Truman,”** which was not printed on this cited page.

**HN-571 — October 31, 1948 — Donora smog labeled mysterious after 18 reported deaths**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19481031.1.1); [U.S. EPA, historical air-pollution episodes](https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=940025VB.TXT).  
**Report:** **“Smog Fatal to 18 in Pennsylvania”** described overwhelmed hospitals, a declared emergency, and four days of dense polluted fog in the Monongahela Valley. Contemporary doctors suspected an industrial pollution component.  
**Assessment:** The later established immediate toll was **20 deaths**, with thousands made ill. The word **“mysterious”** reflects incomplete early medical and atmospheric information, not proof of an unknown exotic agent. Coal combustion, industrial emissions, and stagnant air are central to later analyses.

**HN-572 — November 10, 1948 — Donora toll reaches twenty as investigation proceeds**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19481110-01.1.6); [U.S. EPA, air pollution history](https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=940025VB.TXT).  
**Report:** A small report headed **“Authorities Study Victims of Smog Death as Toll Reaches 20”** updated the fatality count and noted ongoing medical inquiry.  
**Assessment:** This correction directly supersedes the earlier **18-death emergency dispatch (HN-571)**. A 1955 federal air-pollution law followed years later, but one should not treat every subsequent pollution-control measure as caused solely by this one incident.

### 1949

**HN-573 — April 9, 1949 — Workers pursue trapped three-year-old in abandoned well**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19490409.1.1).  
**Report:** A live dispatch from San Marino, California, described a parallel-shaft rescue for **Kathy Fiscus**, trapped in a narrow abandoned well casing. A rescuer thought he saw **pink fabric** deep underground but said **he was unsure**.  
**Assessment:** The reported sighting was an **uncorroborated visual clue**, not confirmation the child was alive or that rescuers had reached her. This became a national live-media rescue effort, with a tragic outcome verified in the subsequent paper.

**HN-574 — April 11, 1949 — Kathy Fiscus recovery confirms rescue failed**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19490411.1.1).  
**Report:** The issue reported that **Kathy's body had been brought to the surface** following attempts to reach her through an adjacent shaft.  
**Assessment:** This documented outcome corrects the unresolved rescue hopes in **HN-573**. Dramatic contemporaneous speculation about faint sounds, clothing, or imminent extraction must not be retold as proof of survival at those moments; the chain of reporting reflects what searchers could and could not know.

**HN-575 — September 23, 1949 — Truman publicly announces a Soviet atomic explosion**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19490923.1.1); [U.S. State Department, records of announcement](https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1949v01/d194).  
**Report:** The banner said the United States would act to make more atomic bombs **as Russia fired an atomic explosion**, and Truman's statement that Soviet testing had recently occurred broke the perceived American monopoly.  
**Assessment:** The Soviet **RDS-1 test happened August 29, 1949**; **September 23 was the U.S. announcement date**, not the detonation date. The paper conveys the contemporaneous intelligence and policy shock; descriptions of the Soviet program's scope remained preliminary.

**HN-576 — October 1, 1949 — Proclamation of People's Republic reaches U.S. newspapers**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19491001.1.2); [U.S. State Department, Chinese Revolution of 1949](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1945-1952/chinese-rev).  
**Report:** **“'People's Republic' Proclaimed in China as Thousands Cheer”** described Mao Zedong's declaration in Beijing, a new central government, and a mass rally.  
**Assessment:** The PRC was proclaimed **October 1**. This did **not** make the civil war's military and political consequences instantly disappear: the Republic of China government continued on Taiwan. A declaration and complete consolidation of rule are different historical events.

### 1950

**HN-577 — January 29, 1950 — Street interviews capture fear of a possible hydrogen bomb**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19500129.1.1); [Truman Presidential Library, January 31 policy statement](https://www.trumanlibrary.gov/library/public-papers/26/statement-president-hydrogen-bomb).  
**Report:** **“Hoosiers Dread Idea of Hydrogen Bomb but They Want Weapon in U.S. Arsenal”** quoted residents fearing that the USSR would build the weapon if America did not.  
**Assessment:** On **January 31**, Truman publicly directed continued development of thermonuclear weapons. That decision was **not evidence an operational American hydrogen bomb already existed**; the first full-scale U.S. thermonuclear test came in **1952**. The newspaper quotes a small set of interviewees, not a scientific opinion poll.

**HN-578 — March 18, 1950 — Air Force denies existence of 'flying saucers'**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19500318.1.1); [National Archives, Air Force UFO records](https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos).  
**Report:** An editor's note announced an Air Force **“flat denial”** of flying saucers and of an alleged government cover-up, while noting persistent reports from New Mexico.  
**Assessment:** This documents the **Air Force's public position on the date printed**. It does not show there were no later unidentified observations or prove the government withheld extraterrestrial technology. Official research programs and public statements changed over time; compare the 1947 sighting wave (**HN-564–HN-565**) and the 1952 radar episode below.

**HN-579 — June 28, 1950 — Seoul's fall reveals escalating Korean invasion**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19500628.1.1); [U.S. State Department, contemporary Korea cables](https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1950v07/d67).  
**Report:** **“Capital of South Korea Falls to Reds”** reported the capture of Seoul during rapid North Korean advances and U.S. mobilization discussions.  
**Assessment:** **North Korean forces crossed into South Korea June 25**, three days before this paper. An early report of the capital falling is neither the war's start date nor a final judgment about the entire campaign; shifting battlefronts and military censorship made early claims especially time-sensitive.

**HN-580 — September 11, 1950 — Guard troop train wreck raises urgent safety questions**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 2 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19500911.1.2).  
**Report:** The paper covered a serious **Ohio collision involving a troop train** transporting 28th Division servicemen to training at **Camp Atterbury**, reporting deaths, **44 injuries**, and eyewitness accounts of warning flares before impact.  
**Assessment:** The witnesses' statements provide evidence of their perceptions, not a completed finding of responsibility. The first casualty and signal accounts should be reconciled with railroad-investigation records before assigning fault; **follow-up open**.

**HN-581 — November 23, 1950 — Thanksgiving Eve commuter collision kills 78**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19501123.1.1).  
**Report:** Officials ordered an investigation after **two Long Island Rail Road electric commuter trains** collided near **Kew Gardens, Queens**. The newspaper reported **78 dead** and almost 300 hurt, describing a following train striking a stalled rush-hour local.  
**Assessment:** This was a documented catastrophic rear-end crash on **November 22**; the **November 23** date is publication. The article's quoted speed estimate is eyewitness description, not an instrument reading or completed technical investigation. Detailed cause allocation is **follow-up open**.

### 1951

**HN-582 — January 12, 1951 — Nevada nuclear-test plans revealed without a test date**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19510112.1.1); [Department of Energy, Nevada proving ground history](https://www.energy.gov/em/manhattan-project-department-energy-formation-1939-1977).  
**Report:** **“Atomic Explosions Planned in Nevada”** stated the Atomic Energy Commission intended to use a military range near Las Vegas, while **declining to announce the first detonation date**.  
**Assessment:** The first Nevada Proving Grounds nuclear test, **Able**, occurred **January 27, 1951**. The newspaper describes a **plan**, not a blast already witnessed on January 12. Long-term downwind exposure and public-health consequences require separate technical records rather than the early military statement.

**HN-583 — March 30, 1951 — Rosenberg espionage conviction photographed before sentence**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19510330-01.1.1); [National Archives, Rosenberg trial records](https://www.archives.gov/press/press-releases/2008-53).  
**Report:** A court photograph showed **Julius Rosenberg, Ethel Rosenberg, and Morton Sobell** after a guilty verdict, saying the principals **faced possible death sentences** and were scheduled for sentencing April 5.  
**Assessment:** The convictions were returned **March 29**. Julius and Ethel were sentenced to death **April 5** and executed **June 19, 1953**; Sobell was **not executed**. These are separate procedural outcomes. Subsequent archival revelations and disputes about evidence must not be reduced either to a mere photograph or to guilt by association.

**HN-584 — April 11, 1951 — Truman fires MacArthur amid Korean War strategy dispute**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19510411.1.1); [Harry S. Truman Presidential Library, MacArthur dismissal](https://www.trumanlibrary.gov/education/presidential-inquiries/firing-macarthur).  
**Report:** The front-page headline **“President Truman Fires MacArthur”** announced the dismissal of the general commanding UN forces during the Korean War; follow-up editions reported the ensuing political controversy.  
**Assessment:** President Truman relieved General Douglas MacArthur on **April 11** after disagreements involving military policy and civilian authority. The firing did not end the war, and an opponent's interpretation of the controversy is not the sole historical record.

**HN-585 — July 14, 1951 — Flood and fires strike Kansas City**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19510714.1.1).  
**Report:** **“Fire and Flood Hit Kansas City”** accompanied scenes of evacuees, including children at a refugee center, as river flooding damaged industrial and residential areas.  
**Assessment:** This is a contemporaneous glimpse of the **1951 Kansas–Missouri flood emergency**, not a survey of all inundated communities or final damage totals. Local crests and losses evolved over several days; **follow-up on official basin-wide counts remains open**.

**HN-586 — October 30, 1951 — Nevada bomb cloud celebrated as a perfect test**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19511030.1.1); [Department of Energy, atmospheric-test program history](https://www.energy.gov/em/manhattan-project-department-energy-formation-1939-1977).  
**Report:** **“Atom Test Blast Rocks Nevada Desert”** described the visible mushroom cloud and quoted pleased scientists who called its outline **“classical”** and the test **“perfect.”**  
**Assessment:** The description concerns **military/test performance and visual appearance**, not a finding that the detonation was safe for civilians, workers, or downwind populations. It follows the proposed program in **HN-582**; environmental and dosimetry records are a different evidence base.

### 1952

**HN-587 — February 6, 1952 — Princess Elizabeth becomes queen while abroad**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19520206.1.1); [Royal Household, accession and coronation](https://www.royal.uk/queen-elizabeth-iis-accession-and-coronation).  
**Report:** **“Lilibet Flying Home From Africa; King's Death Makes Her Queen”** reported George VI's death and Elizabeth's immediate accession while she was in Kenya.  
**Assessment:** Elizabeth became monarch **on February 6, 1952**, upon her father's death. The solemn **coronation ceremony was June 2, 1953**, more than a year later (**HN-592**); the page should not be described as reporting the coronation itself.

**HN-588 — July 18, 1952 — Polio epidemic grows across nine reported states**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19520718.1.6); [CDC Museum, polio history](https://www.cdc.gov/museum/online/story-of-cdc/polio/index.html).  
**Report:** **“Polio at Epidemic Stage in 9 States”** described local outbreaks that had crossed a contemporary incidence threshold and worried that 1952 might exceed earlier case records.  
**Assessment:** The article was a **midyear projection**, not a final annual count. The national epidemic intensified, helping frame the urgency of later vaccine trials. An incidence threshold applied to particular localities cannot be treated as the infection rate throughout each entire state.

**HN-589 — July 28, 1952 — Radar reports prompt jets over Washington**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 3 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19520728.1.3); [National Archives, Air Force UFO case files](https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos).  
**Report:** **“Air Force Jets Give Chase to 'Flying Saucers' Over Capital”** paired reports of unusual radar returns near Washington, D.C., with pilots being sent to investigate.  
**Assessment:** **Radar observations and intercept sorties are real reported events**; the newspaper label **“flying saucers” is an interpretation**, not physical evidence of extraterrestrial craft. Records of weather conditions, sensor behavior, air traffic, and individual pilot observations must be separated case by case.

**HN-590 — July 31, 1952 — Cool-air explanation enters Washington saucer coverage**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Times*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=IPT19520731.1.4); [National Archives, Air Force UFO investigations](https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos).  
**Report:** **“Air Force Hopes Cool Air Will End 'Saucers'”** discussed atmospheric conditions as a possible explanation for the capital's recent radar phenomena and noted the official desire for a solution.  
**Assessment:** **Temperature inversions and anomalous radar propagation** were part of the official explanation; that does not automatically account for every sighting mentioned in the earlier story (**HN-589**). This second report is evidence of how a public explanation developed over days.

### 1953

**HN-591 — March 6, 1953 — Stalin's death prompts speculation about Soviet succession**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19530306.1.1).  
**Report:** **“Ruler of Russia Dead”** announced Joseph Stalin's death while also relaying reports that the Eisenhower administration was contemplating psychological operations against Soviet communist leadership.  
**Assessment:** Stalin died **March 5, 1953**; March 6 was a common U.S. newspaper date of publication. The claim of a **“top secret”** U.S. propaganda campaign is an attributed press account, not a declassified operational finding established by this article alone.

**HN-592 — June 2, 1953 — Elizabeth II crowned in elaborate televised ceremony**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19530602.1.1); [Royal Household, accession and coronation](https://www.royal.uk/queen-elizabeth-iis-accession-and-coronation).  
**Report:** **“Elizabeth II Is Crowned Queen”** described the Westminster Abbey ceremony in nearly fairy-tale imagery, emphasizing pageantry and historical continuity.  
**Assessment:** The **June 2 coronation** was ceremonial confirmation of a reign that began **February 6, 1952 (HN-587)**. These dates are often conflated. The newspaper's romantic descriptive style is cultural framing, not a comprehensive account of British imperial politics.

**HN-593 — June 10, 1953 — New England tornado toll initially given as 82**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19530610.1.1); [NOAA/National Weather Service, historic damaging tornadoes](https://www.weather.gov/media/owp/oh/hdsc/docs/TP20.pdf).  
**Report:** The headline **“New England Struck by Area's Worst Tornadoes in History: 82 Are Dead”** described violent destruction in and around **Worcester, Massachusetts**, on June 9.  
**Assessment:** The **82** figure was an early count. Retrospective accounts commonly report **94 fatalities** for the Worcester tornado, while some historic Weather Bureau summaries use about **90**. Counts must be paired with source, date, and whether they mean this tornado or the broader outbreak.

**HN-594 — June 11, 1953 — Tornado damage estimate and death count rise**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19530611.1.1); [NOAA/National Weather Service, historical review](https://www.weather.gov/media/owp/oh/hdsc/docs/TP20.pdf).  
**Report:** The follow-up raised the **Worcester-area toll to 89**, estimated tens of millions of dollars in damage, and reported a federal disaster declaration.  
**Assessment:** The revised **89** corrects **HN-593's 82**, but should not automatically be treated as the final disaster total. The newspaper was reporting ongoing rescue and damage assessments, not explaining tornado formation or adjudicating contemporaneous rumors that nuclear tests had caused the storm.

**HN-595 — June 20, 1953 — Rosenberg executions reported after failed clemency efforts**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19530620.1.1); [National Archives, Rosenberg case history](https://www.archives.gov/press/press-releases/2008-53).  
**Report:** **“Atom Spies Executed”** recounted protests outside the White House after **Julius and Ethel Rosenberg** were put to death; the same issue discussed an unrelated Korean truce crisis.  
**Assessment:** The executions occurred **June 19, 1953**, a day before this report. The procedural sequence began with the **March 1951 convictions (HN-583)**. Calling them **“atom spies”** is the newspaper's legal/political shorthand; later documentary debates over espionage evidence and the punishment's proportionality should not be erased.

### 1954

**HN-596 — March 2, 1954 — Capitol shooting report miscounts attackers**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19540302.1.1); [U.S. House Historian, 1954 chamber shooting](https://history.house.gov/Blog/2024/March/3-1-Looking-Back-1954/).  
**Report:** The caption identified **five wounded congressmen** but attributed the March 1 House-gallery gunfire to **“three Puerto Rican terrorists.”** It described Representative Alvin Bentley as critically injured.  
**Assessment:** House records establish **four Puerto Rican Nationalist Party attackers**, with **five Representatives wounded and none killed**. The newspaper's **three** was an incorrect contemporary account or caption. Modern historical description must distinguish an **independence movement's armed faction** from Puerto Ricans generally.

**HN-597 — March 16, 1954 — Fishing crew reports burns from Pacific nuclear fallout**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19540316.1.1); [Department of Energy, historic fallout research](https://ehss.energy.gov/ohre/roadmap/roadmap/part2.html).  
**Report:** **“23 Jap Fishermen Claim Atom Burns”** described swollen hands, hair loss, and darkened skin aboard a Japanese vessel after **radioactive dust** descended from a March 1 U.S. test near the Marshall Islands.  
**Assessment:** The fishermen aboard the ***Daigo Fukuryū Maru* (Lucky Dragon No. 5)** were indeed exposed to fallout from the **Castle Bravo thermonuclear test**, whose yield and fallout exceeded planning expectations. The period headline's term for Japanese people is derogatory and is reproduced here only for source identification; the medical symptoms and official government requests are materially relevant.

**HN-598 — April 22, 1954 — Army–McCarthy hearings set committee roles and procedures**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19540422.1.1); [U.S. Senate, McCarthy and Army–McCarthy hearings](https://www.senate.gov/about/powers-procedures/investigations/mccarthy-and-army-mccarthy-hearings.htm).  
**Report:** A photograph showed **Senator Karl Mundt** welcoming a replacement member to the investigating subcommittee as **Joseph McCarthy** stepped back from membership for the hearings over his dispute with Army leaders.  
**Assessment:** The televised **Army–McCarthy hearings began April 22**. A committee photograph is not a determination that McCarthy's accusations were true; the scope, membership, testimony, and subsequent **December 1954 Senate censure** are separate events.

**HN-599 — May 22, 1954 — Black newspaper explains five cases behind school ruling**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 7 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19540522-01.1.7); [National Archives, *Brown v. Board of Education*](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/brown-v-board-of-education).  
**Report:** **“Background of 5 School Cases”** reviewed the plaintiffs' challenges and courts' divergent actions following the Supreme Court's landmark ruling on racial segregation in public education.  
**Assessment:** The Court decided ***Brown* on May 17, 1954**, declaring state-imposed public-school segregation unconstitutional. The **May 31, 1955 *Brown II*** decision addressed implementation; neither judgment caused every segregated school to integrate immediately. The *Recorder* offers essential contemporary Black press context too often missed in general newspaper indexes.

**HN-600 — June 10, 1954 — Welch's rebuke dominates Army–McCarthy coverage**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 6 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19540610.1.6); [U.S. Senate, Welch's rebuke](https://www.senate.gov/about/powers-procedures/investigations/mccarthy-hearings/have-you-no-sense-of-decency.htm).  
**Report:** **“Welch Scores Cruel Charge by McCarthy”** recounted Army counsel **Joseph N. Welch's** June 9 objection to McCarthy's attack on a young lawyer, followed by applause despite hearing-room rules.  
**Assessment:** The moment became a powerful televised public confrontation, but it did **not immediately remove McCarthy from the Senate**. His formal censure came in **December 1954**. The article documents public and legal reaction, not a judicial verdict against any individual named during the hearing.

**HN-601 — September 23, 1954 — Death of Lucky Dragon crewman follows fallout exposure**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19540923.1.1); [Department of Energy, Castle Bravo fallout history](https://www.energy.gov/management/articles/history-atomic-energy-commission?nrg_redirect=408579).  
**Report:** **“Japanese Fisherman Dies of Radio Ash”** reported the death of **Aikichi Kuboyama**, one of twenty-three fishermen exposed during the March test, and anticipated renewed Japanese protest. The dispatch also mentioned pneumonia in the immediate medical history.  
**Assessment:** His death on **September 23** followed the confirmed exposure in **HN-597**. The article's phrase **“first fatality from hydrogen bomb test”** is a narrow, contemporary claim, **not proof that no Marshallese people suffered radiation injuries or that no other deaths were later attributed to the test**. Medical causation and long-term effects require clinical and exposure records.

### 1955

**HN-602 — April 12, 1955 — Salk polio-vaccine field trial proclaimed successful**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19550412.1.1); [CDC Museum, development of polio vaccines](https://www.cdc.gov/museum/online/story-of-cdc/polio/index.html).  
**Report:** **“Salk Polio Vaccine Is Success”** marked public release of the major trial evaluation of Jonas Salk's inactivated-virus vaccine, accompanied by a photograph of vaccine vials.  
**Assessment:** The **April 12 efficacy announcement** was a breakthrough, but it was **not proof that all manufactured lots were safe or that polio had been eliminated**. The subsequent Cutter manufacturing failure became public within weeks; the positive clinical trial and a specific production defect must be distinguished.

**HN-603 — June 13, 1955 — Le Mans race disaster appears with 78 dead**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19550613.1.1); [24 Hours of Le Mans, history of the 1955 accident](https://www.24h-lemans.com/en/news/24-hours-of-le-mans-10-key-dates-1923-1967-40401).  
**Report:** A graphic photograph labeled **“Death at the Races”** showed rescue workers, while the report gave a contemporary toll of **78 killed** and described driver **Pierre Levegh's** fatal high-speed crash on June 11.  
**Assessment:** Later commonly cited totals are **83 spectators plus Levegh, or 84 deaths overall**, rather than the early 78. The horrifying accident is documented; liability, mechanical sequence, and safety reforms cannot be established merely from a dramatic still photograph and first-day wire count.

**HN-604 — June 23, 1955 — Polio panel backs vaccination after manufacturing crisis**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19550623.1.1); [CDC, the Cutter Incident](https://www.cdc.gov/vaccine-safety/historical-concerns/index.html).  
**Report:** **“Polio Experts' Panel Favors Continuing of Salk Vaccine Program”** documented official debate about resuming or continuing immunization after questions over vaccine manufacturing and safety.  
**Assessment:** Some **Cutter Laboratories** vaccine lots contained live poliovirus despite release testing, causing cases of paralytic polio. The panel's willingness to continue a controlled program did **not** erase that failure or discredit the Salk vaccine's overall efficacy (**HN-602**). These are separate judgments about **product design, production quality, and oversight**.

**HN-605 — September 17, 1955 — Black press shows Emmett Till's open-casket evidence**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19550917-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Emmett Till history](https://www.nps.gov/people/emmett-till.htm).  
**Report:** The *Recorder* printed **a photograph of Emmett Till's severely battered face** as his body lay in state in Chicago, alongside portraits of his mother and two accused white men before the murder trial.  
**Assessment:** Fourteen-year-old Till was **abducted and murdered in Mississippi in August 1955**. The photograph became vital evidence for the broader public, made possible by **Mamie Till-Mobley's insistence that the public see the violence**. Period labels about the accusation against Till are not evidence that he provoked or deserved the crime.

**HN-606 — September 24, 1955 — *Recorder* reports murder trial as jury hears evidence**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19550924-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Till trial site and legal history](https://www.nps.gov/till/learn/management/presidential-proclamation.htm).  
**Report:** **“Lynch Trial Takes New Turn”** displayed photographs of witnesses, relatives, and the defendants as the Mississippi prosecution of **Roy Bryant and J. W. Milam** unfolded.  
**Assessment:** The **all-white jury acquitted both men on September 23**, during the week of this issue; a paper's production time and dated front-page coverage should not be presumed to include every event of its publication day. Their acquittal **was a court result, not historical exoneration**; later admissions and investigations make that distinction essential.

**HN-607 — November 12, 1955 — Grand jury declines further Till kidnapping charges**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19551112-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Till national monument record](https://www.nps.gov/till/learn/management/presidential-proclamation.htm).  
**Report:** **“Miss. Grand Jury Fails to Indict Till Kidnappers”** reported another failure of Mississippi's legal process to pursue the case after the murder-trial acquittal.  
**Assessment:** This is a **separate grand-jury nonindictment**, not a second jury acquittal on the murder charge. The sequence illustrates repeated failures of accountability, rather than credible proof that the abduction or killing never occurred.

**HN-608 — December 10, 1955 — Montgomery residents organize bus boycott**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19551210-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Rosa Parks and boycott](https://www.nps.gov/semo/learn/historyculture/rosa-parks.htm).  
**Report:** **“Angry Citizens Stage Bus Boycott in Ala. City”** described Black Montgomery residents refusing segregated city bus service, with community leaders expressing anger at discriminatory treatment.  
**Assessment:** **Rosa Parks was arrested December 1**, and the organized boycott began **December 5**. It was the work of a broader network of Black residents and organizers, **not a spontaneous one-person protest**. In December 1955 its eventual duration and court outcome were still unknown.

**HN-609 — December 24, 1955 — Montgomery boycott organizers weigh their own transport network**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 13 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19551224-01.1.13); [National Park Service, Montgomery Bus Boycott](https://www.nps.gov/articles/montgomery-bus-boycott.htm).  
**Report:** **“Plan Own Bus Service as Boycott Continues”** outlined an idea for **Black-owned bus or taxi relays** after authorities resisted changing segregated bus operations and hiring practices.  
**Assessment:** This article documents **an organizing proposal**, not proof that an independent bus system had already been licensed or started. Carpools, walking, funding, and legal challenge sustained the **381-day boycott through 1956**. The proposal is unusually revealing evidence of the logistical work behind the movement, and deserves follow-up in the next decade.

### 1956

**HN-610 — March 31, 1956 — King convicted as Montgomery bus boycott continues**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19560331-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Montgomery Bus Boycott](https://www.nps.gov/articles/montgomery-bus-boycott.htm).  
**Report:** A photograph of **Martin Luther King Jr. and Coretta Scott King** accompanied news of his conviction for conspiring to boycott segregated buses. The caption said he was appealing a fine and hard-labor sentence, while other organizers sustained the campaign.  
**Assessment:** This was a **prosecution of the boycott's leadership**, not a finding that segregated public transportation was lawful or just. The boycott continued; federal court action in *Browder v. Gayle* ultimately made Montgomery bus integration effective **December 20, 1956**. Compare HN-608–HN-609 and HN-615.

**HN-611 — July 3, 1956 — Investigators find evidence of Grand Canyon midair collision**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19560703.1.1).  
**Report:** **“Find Evidence of Collision of Airliners”** relayed statements from investigators at the wreckage of two passenger aircraft. The dispatch referred to **128 people aboard** the planes and described a developing, not yet completed, inquiry.  
**Assessment:** The June 30 collision of **United Air Lines Flight 718 and TWA Flight 2** killed all **128** aboard and accelerated demands for better air-traffic control. Evidence found at a crash site and a final account of operational causes are different stages of investigation; the article records the former, not the latter.

**HN-612 — July 26, 1956 — *Andrea Doria* collision and rescue unfold at sea**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19560726.1.1); [U.S. Coast Guard, historical chronology](https://www.history.uscg.mil/research/chronology/).  
**Report:** The page carried developing reports on the Italian liner ***Andrea Doria*** after its collision with the Swedish ***Stockholm*** near Nantucket, as rescue efforts and the vessel's fate were still being established.  
**Assessment:** The *Andrea Doria* sank on **July 26**. Early passenger and casualty figures changed; even published retrospectives differ slightly over the final death count. The rescue itself was real, but **initial accounts of who maneuvered wrongly or what radar showed should not be presented as settled navigational findings**.

**HN-613 — October 30, 1956 — Fighting erupts around the Suez Canal**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19561030.1.1); [U.S. State Department, Suez Crisis history](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1953-1960/suez).  
**Report:** A map titled **“Trouble Erupts Near Suez”** showed Israeli troops advancing into Egypt and reported diplomatic and military responses.  
**Assessment:** This page captured the **opening Israeli advance**, before the full Anglo-French intervention and ensuing international pressure had played out. The Suez Crisis involved Egyptian nationalization of the canal, British and French intervention, Israel's attack, and a U.S.-backed demand for a ceasefire; none is adequately explained by the map's front-line arrows alone.

**HN-614 — November 3, 1956 — Soviet armor closes around Budapest**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19561103.1.1); [U.S. State Department, Hungary in 1956](https://2001-2009.state.gov/r/pa/ho/time/lw/107186.htm).  
**Report:** **“Soviets Close Armored Ring at Budapest”** described Soviet forces around Hungary's capital and reports that the Austrian frontier was being closed, while another part of the same page covered the simultaneous fighting in Egypt.  
**Assessment:** The dispatch described the **immediate prelude** to the renewed Soviet assault on **November 4** and the suppression of the Hungarian uprising. Reports relayed through Vienna were not direct verification of every troop number; the simultaneous Suez crisis also shaped international diplomatic responses.

**HN-615 — December 29, 1956 — Integrated Montgomery bus service faces sniper violence**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19561229.1.1); [National Park Service, bus integration decision](https://www.nps.gov/places/quote-from-montgomery-bus-boycott-speech.htm).  
**Report:** The story reported a sniper firing into a newly integrated Montgomery bus, wounding passenger **Rosa Jordan** and temporarily halting city bus operations.  
**Assessment:** The **381-day boycott had formally ended** following the court order, but **legal desegregation did not end white-supremacist violence**. The report should not be mistaken for a second beginning of the boycott; it is an early account of retaliation after a significant civil-rights victory.

### 1957

**HN-616 — September 14, 1957 — Black newspaper interrogates Little Rock governor's defiance**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19570914-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Little Rock crisis timeline](https://home.nps.gov/chsc/learn/historyculture/timeline.htm).  
**Report:** Amid the desegregation crisis, the *Recorder* featured Little Rock Mayor **Woodrow Mann's** criticism of Governor **Orval Faubus** and cited an FBI assessment in dispute with the governor's asserted fears of violence.  
**Assessment:** The page is important because it records **opposition to state obstruction**, not just images of hostile crowds. The claim that a governor was keeping order must be assessed against his National Guard's actual obstruction of **nine Black students' access to Central High School**.

**HN-617 — September 24, 1957 — Little Rock violence prompts federal intervention**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19570924-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Little Rock Nine](https://www.nps.gov/people/the-little-rock-nine.htm).  
**Report:** **“Violence Flares in Little Rock”** described public disorder as the effort to integrate Central High School entered a critical stage.  
**Assessment:** Eisenhower sent federal troops to enforce the students' access; the **Little Rock Nine entered under military protection on September 25**. Violence against the students and enforcement of their federal rights are not morally or legally equivalent “sides” merely because they occurred at the same time.

**HN-618 — October 24, 1957 — Asian-influenza death reports approach 300**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19571024.1.1); [CDC, 1957–58 H2N2 pandemic](https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/flu/pandemic-resources/1957-1958-pandemic.html).  
**Report:** **“Flu Fatalities in Nation Near to 300”** tallied deaths and described outbreaks in institutions during the **Asian influenza** pandemic.  
**Assessment:** **Nearly 300** was a **point-in-time reported toll**, not the total U.S. mortality of the **1957–58 H2N2 pandemic**. The CDC retrospectively gives an estimated **116,000 U.S. deaths** over the pandemic; those are estimates using a broader time frame and attribution method than a day's news tally.

**HN-619 — November 12, 1957 — The fate of Sputnik's dog remains uncertain to readers**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19571112.1.1); [NASA, animals in space history](https://www.nasa.gov/history/a-brief-history-of-animals-in-space/).  
**Report:** The page reported amid uncertainty about the fate of the dog aboard **Sputnik 2**, launched November 3, as the Soviet space mission generated contradictory or incomplete information.  
**Assessment:** **Laika did not return**; the spacecraft had no recovery system, and later evidence showed she died **within hours from heat stress**, much earlier than the public Soviet presentation suggested. A newspaper's uncertainty is genuine evidence of **information asymmetry**, not proof that the animal remained alive or that the flight was uncrewed.

### 1958

**HN-620 — February 1, 1958 — Explorer 1 becomes the first U.S. satellite**  
**Source:** *The Daily Banner*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=TDB19580201-01.1.1); [NASA, Explorer 1 history](https://www.nasa.gov/missions/explorer/explorer-1-fast-facts/).  
**Report:** **“‘Moon’ Placed in Orbit by Army Experts”** announced the successful launch of the first American artificial satellite.  
**Assessment:** **Explorer 1 launched January 31** (U.S. time), while this issue appeared the next day. Its particle measurements led to the identification of the **Van Allen radiation belts**. “Moon” was period shorthand for a man-made satellite, not an actual lunar probe.

**HN-621 — March 6, 1958 — No signal arrives from attempted Explorer 2**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19580306.1.1); [NASA, Sputnik–Explorer launch chronology](https://www.nasa.gov/history/sputnik/chronology.html).  
**Report:** **“No Sound Is Received From Explorer II”** described tracking stations listening for a satellite signal with declining hope after a Jupiter-C launch.  
**Assessment:** **Explorer 2 did not reach orbit** because the final stage failed. The absence of radio contact was an observed fact; on the newspaper date, details of why the attempt failed were still being determined. It should not be listed as a second successful American satellite.

**HN-622 — March 13, 1958 — Accidentally dropped nuclear weapon detonates conventionally**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19580313.1.1).  
**Report:** A photograph captioned **“A-Bomb Crater”** showed damage near **Florence, South Carolina**, and said a military inquiry was under way after an aircraft accidentally released a nuclear weapon.  
**Assessment:** Its **conventional high explosives detonated**, but there was **no nuclear detonation**. “Atomic bomb explosion” in a sensational caption would be technically misleading; the distinction between a dropped nuclear weapon, conventional detonation, radiological contamination, and nuclear yield is essential.

**HN-623 — July 11, 1958 — Alaska's giant wave reported with missing boats**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 8 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19580711.1.8); [National Park Service, Lituya Bay tsunami research](https://www.nps.gov/articles/aps-18-1-4.htm).  
**Report:** **“At Least 3 Killed by Huge Tidal Wave”** described fishing vessels missing after earthquake-driven waves near **Yakutat and Lituya Bay**, with confused early reports of deaths and boats lost.  
**Assessment:** The **July 9 Lituya Bay rockslide tsunami** generated a documented **524-metre (1,719-foot) maximum run-up** on the opposite slope—**not** an open-ocean wave traveling at that height. Initial accounts mixed locations and missing-person reports. The modern reconstruction identifies the landslide and its localized, exceptional run-up.

**HN-624 — December 3, 1958 — Chicago school-fire toll rises as investigators question building safety**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19581203.1.1); [National Fire Protection Association, historical fire record](https://files.nfpa.org/nfpaorg/NFPA/index.html).  
**Report:** A photograph described **87 children killed** at Our Lady of the Angels school, while an accompanying story weighed whether closed doors might have reduced the rapidly growing number of deaths.  
**Assessment:** The **December 1** disaster ultimately killed **92 children and three nuns (95 people)**. The 87-children figure is **an interim count**, and the article's discussion of doors is a preliminary fire-safety hypothesis rather than a completed cause-and-liability determination.

### 1959

**HN-625 — January 3, 1959 — Alaska joins the Union amid Cold War headlines**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19590103.1.1).  
**Report:** **“Alaska Added to Union, New Flag Unfurled”** marked statehood as the paper also discussed Soviet lunar-launch developments and the collapse of Fulgencio Batista's Cuban government.  
**Assessment:** Alaska became the **49th U.S. state on January 3**. The juxtaposed headlines are useful evidence of the day's global agenda; they do not imply that statehood had any causal connection to the simultaneous Cuban or space-race events.

**HN-626 — July 4, 1959 — *Recorder* records Mahala Ashley Dickerson's admission to Alaska bar**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 13 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19590704-01.1.13).  
**Report:** **“M. A. Dickerson Can Now Practice Law in Alaska”** reported that **Mahala Ashley Dickerson**, already licensed in other states, had gained admission after a procedural delay connected with statehood and opened an Anchorage law office.  
**Assessment:** This is a significant **first-person-era record of a Black woman's professional achievement**, often overlooked in national headline surveys. The item specifically documents **Alaska bar admission**, not a general claim that she was the first woman lawyer in the United States or that every later biographical superlative has been separately verified here.

**HN-627 — September 23, 1959 — Steel strike's economic consequences reach household wages**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19590923.1.1).  
**Report:** The paper connected the prolonged **United Steelworkers strike** and factory changeovers to falling manufacturing wages, citing a reduced weekly take-home figure for a family worker.  
**Assessment:** A current wage index and a national steel labor dispute are related but **not proof that every factory worker lost the reported amount because of the strike**. The article is useful for tracing the conflict's broader economic reporting, with attribution divided among strike layoffs and seasonal auto-model changes.

**HN-628 — October 5, 1959 — Soviet Lunik 3 launch intensifies lunar-race reporting**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19591005.1.1); [NASA, early spaceflight chronology](https://www.nasa.gov/history/dawn-of-the-space-age/).  
**Report:** A story on proposed U.S. moon launches noted that **the Soviet Union had just launched Lunik III**, frustrating hopes that an American spacecraft would make the next lunar breakthrough.  
**Assessment:** **Luna 3** later returned the **first photographs of the Moon's far side**. That later achievement should not be backdated to October 5 as if this paper had already seen and verified the images; it reported a launch and plans, not the completed photographic result.

### 1960

**HN-629 — March 22, 1960 — Sharpeville violence reported through police framing**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19600322.1.1).  
**Report:** **“New Violence by African Rioters”** counted an early **67 people dead across several locations** and described police opening fire on a crowd protesting South Africa's identity-pass laws.  
**Assessment:** The **Sharpeville massacre on March 21** killed **69 people at Sharpeville**, overwhelmingly Black demonstrators shot by police. The dispatch's language casts protesters as the initiating menace and its 67 figure combines incidents; neither should override the later investigation of state violence or be confused with Sharpeville's own documented toll.

**HN-630 — May 11, 1960 — Eisenhower publicly acknowledges U-2 intelligence flights**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19600511.1.1); [U.S. State Department, U-2 incident history](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1953-1960/u2-incident).  
**Report:** **“President Eisenhower Defends Intelligence Flights over Russia”** quoted the president calling such operations unpleasant but necessary to guard against surprise attack after a U.S. plane came down inside the Soviet Union.  
**Assessment:** This **acknowledgment contradicted the administration's initial weather-research-plane cover story** after pilot **Francis Gary Powers** survived. The later admission and the earlier explanation cannot both be treated as equally reliable descriptions of the aircraft's mission.

**HN-631 — May 19, 1960 — Failed Paris summit blamed on U-2 confrontation**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19600519.1.1); [U.S. State Department, U-2 incident history](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1953-1960/u2-incident).  
**Report:** Coverage of NATO reaction and Eisenhower's visit to Portugal described a summit in disarray after Nikita Khrushchev's confrontation over American reconnaissance flights.  
**Assessment:** The diplomatic breakdown is documented, but **the newspaper's loaded description of Soviet “torpedo tactics” is a political framing**, not an impartial finding that the U.S. reconnaissance mission caused no part of the dispute. Read beside HN-630 and the diplomatic record.

**HN-632 — May 24, 1960 — Tsunami from Chile sends casualty reports across Pacific**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19600524.1.1); [U.S. Geological Survey, 1960 Chile tsunami](https://pubs.usgs.gov/circ/c1187/).  
**Report:** **“Tidal Waves Pound at Pacific Areas; 139 Are Dead and Many Missing”** summarized unfolding reports of deadly waves in **Japan and Hawaii** after the Chilean disaster.  
**Assessment:** The generating earthquake struck southern Chile on **May 22**, with a retrospectively measured **magnitude of 9.5**, the largest instrumentally recorded. The newspaper's 139 was **an early, geographically mixed tsunami count**, not a final overall earthquake-and-tsunami death toll; updates arrived over different time zones and shorelines.

### 1961

**HN-633 — April 12, 1961 — Yuri Gagarin completes first human orbit**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19610412.1.1); [NASA, Gagarin biography](https://starchild.gsfc.nasa.gov/docs/StarChild/whos_who_level2/gagarin.html).  
**Report:** **“Russia Sends Man Around World in Space Ship and Returns Him Unharmed”** described Soviet pilot **Yuri Gagarin's** single orbit aboard **Vostok 1** and the speed of the international announcement.  
**Assessment:** Gagarin was indeed the **first human to orbit Earth**. A significant detail not evident from heroic capsule-return language is that **he ejected during descent and landed separately by parachute**, as planned. The distinction matters when interpreting contemporary claims about landing procedures.

**HN-634 — April 20, 1961 — Bay of Pigs invasion collapses**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19610420.1.1); [U.S. State Department, Bay of Pigs invasion](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1961-1968/bay-of-pigs).  
**Report:** **“Fidel Castro Proclaims Destruction of Invasion Force”** carried the Cuban government's declaration that the armed landing had been defeated.  
**Assessment:** The **CIA-backed exile invasion failed**. The contemporaneous Cuban government's proclamation was a partisan wartime source even where the ultimate outcome was correct; individual claims of prisoners, executions, or battlefield strength still require independent records.

**HN-635 — May 27, 1961 — Freedom Riders continue after attacks and a burned bus**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19610527-01.1.1); [National Park Service, Freedom Riders](https://www.nps.gov/articles/freedom-riders.htm).  
**Report:** The Black weekly printed an image of an **Anniston, Alabama, bus burned by a white mob**, described federal official **John Seigenthaler's** injuries during attacks in Montgomery, and reported riders continuing despite arrests.  
**Assessment:** The attacks on the buses began **May 14**, before this May 27 issue; the issue documents **subsequent resistance and consequences**, not one simultaneous event. The Freedom Rides challenged segregation in interstate travel, and violence against the riders should not be relabeled as mutual disorder.

**HN-636 — November 8, 1961 — Berlin Wall strengthened to prevent contact and escapes**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 4 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19611108.1.4); [U.S. State Department, Berlin Wall history](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1961-1968/berlin-wall).  
**Report:** **“Erect Screens to Seal Off East Berlin”** described new barriers intended to stop people across the divided city from even signaling to one another and summarized earlier window and street closures.  
**Assessment:** The **border closure began August 13, 1961**. November's screens were **additional measures**, not the initial erection of the wall. Their purpose is understandable only in the context of East German restrictions on movement and persistent attempts to flee to the West.

### 1962

**HN-637 — February 20, 1962 — John Glenn launched into Earth orbit**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19620220.1.1); [NASA, John Glenn mission](https://www.nasa.gov/people/john-glenn/).  
**Report:** **“United States Fires Astronaut Glenn into Three-Orbit Flight”** reported the launch of **Friendship 7** while Glenn was still on his historic mission.  
**Assessment:** Glenn completed **three orbits** and became the **first American to orbit Earth**—not the first human in orbit (Gagarin in 1961), nor the first American in space (Alan Shepard in 1961). The morning paper's planned three-orbit wording was **prospective at publication**, with the successful splashdown established afterward.

**HN-638 — July 10, 1962 — Telstar 1 enters orbit for experimental communications**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19620710.1.1); [NASA, milestone chronology](https://www.nasa.gov/specials/timeline/).  
**Report:** **“Telstar Is Successfully Rocketed into Space at Cape Canaveral Today”** described launch of the privately developed active communications satellite.  
**Assessment:** **Telstar 1** did demonstrate transatlantic communications, but launch-day publicity should not be read as an assertion of already-routine, continuous satellite television. It had **limited orbital visibility windows** and was an experimental system, not the modern global communications network.

**HN-639 — July 28, 1962 — Thalidomide scandal prompts scrutiny of experimental drug testing**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19620728.1.1); [FDA, historical drug-safety reforms](https://www.fda.gov/about-fda/fda-history-exhibits/brief-history-center-drug-evaluation-and-research).  
**Report:** A UPI explainer noted that **thalidomide**, previously supplied experimentally, had been associated with severe birth defects and raised questions about whether patients knew they were receiving unapproved drugs.  
**Assessment:** The U.S. **did not approve thalidomide for general marketing** at the time because FDA reviewer **Frances Kelsey** declined to clear the application, although investigational distribution occurred. The disaster helped build support for the **1962 Kefauver–Harris Amendments**. Neither “not approved” nor “tested experimentally” means **no U.S. patient was exposed**.

**HN-640 — October 23, 1962 — Aerial photographs disclose Soviet missile installations in Cuba**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19621023.1.1); [U.S. State Department, Cuban Missile Crisis](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1961-1968/cuban-missile-crisis).  
**Report:** **“Blockade of Cuba Is Underway”** displayed claims about launch pads seen in U.S. aerial reconnaissance photographs; the Pentagon spokesman explicitly said the government had **not positively confirmed nuclear warheads on the island**.  
**Assessment:** The photographs established **Soviet missile-site construction**, but the government's **public uncertainty on the presence of warheads** is a meaningful contemporaneous distinction. The encounter involved a U.S. naval **“quarantine”**, Soviet forces, and a real danger of nuclear escalation; a suspected warhead and a directly verified one are not interchangeable.

**HN-641 — October 29, 1962 — Soviet press celebrates plan to remove Cuban missile bases**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19621029.1.1); [U.S. State Department, crisis settlement](https://history.state.gov/milestones/1961-1968/cuban-missile-crisis).  
**Report:** The paper quoted **Pravda's** favorable account of Khrushchev's decision to dismantle missile installations, alongside the continued naval interception and other crisis news.  
**Assessment:** The **public removal announcement** was a turning point, not immediate proof every missile had already been shipped away. The settlement also entailed a **U.S. pledge not to invade Cuba and a privately communicated plan to remove Jupiter missiles from Turkey**—the latter absent from much immediate public reporting.

### 1963

**HN-642 — August 31, 1963 — *Recorder* publishes King's message after March on Washington**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19630831-01.1.1); [National Archives, March on Washington program](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/official-program-for-the-march-on-washington).  
**Report:** A striking front page hailed roughly **200,000 freedom marchers** and printed a lengthy version of **Martin Luther King Jr.'s** address delivered at the August 28 March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom.  
**Assessment:** The National Archives describes **more than 250,000 attendees**; the *Recorder's* rounded **200,000** demonstrates differing estimates rather than a different protest. The march advocated **jobs as well as civil rights**, and the Black press's extensive treatment gives a perspective omitted by some headline-only recaps.

**HN-643 — September 21, 1963 — Bombed Birmingham church pictured amid accusations of state culpability**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19630921-01.1.1); [National Park Service, 16th Street Baptist Church bombing](https://www.nps.gov/articles/16thstreetbaptist.htm).  
**Report:** The front page showed the **damaged altar at Birmingham's 16th Street Baptist Church**, grieving families, and the death of **four Black girls**, while a headline blamed Alabama Governor Wallace's political climate for the bloodshed.  
**Assessment:** The **September 15 bombing was carried out by Ku Klux Klan members**. The headline about Wallace is a **political attribution of responsibility**, not an announcement that he planted the explosives or a completed criminal verdict. The direct perpetrators and the wider climate of racist terror require distinct treatment.

**HN-644 — November 22, 1963 — Kennedy assassination reported within hours**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19631122.1.1); [National Archives, Warren Commission records](https://www.archives.gov/research/jfk/warren-commission-report/intro).  
**Report:** **“Pres. Kennedy Is Killed by Assassin's Bullets; Governor of Texas Is Also Shot”** announced the death of President **John F. Kennedy** and the wounding of Texas Governor **John Connally** in Dallas.  
**Assessment:** The assassination itself is established; **the identity, motive, number of attackers, and sequence of shots were still being investigated on November 22**. Subsequent official inquiries include the **Warren Commission** and later congressional review; a breaking-news page cannot serve as a final investigative ruling.

**HN-645 — November 23, 1963 — Oswald charged, not convicted, in Kennedy's murder**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19631123.1.1); [National Archives, Warren Commission report](https://www.archives.gov/research/jfk/warren-commission-report/chapter-1).  
**Report:** **“Charge Oswald with Murder”** identified **Lee Harvey Oswald** as the accused man, described the formal murder charge, and noted his denial.  
**Assessment:** A **charge is not a conviction**. Oswald was shot and killed by **Jack Ruby on November 24** before trial. Later commissions reached findings about the assassination, but they cannot retroactively make Oswald a criminal defendant who was convicted in open court; the article's hostile character labels also are not evidence.

### 1964

**HN-646 — March 28, 1964 — Good Friday Alaska earthquake produces fires and tidal waves**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19640328.1.1); [U.S. Geological Survey, 1964 Alaska earthquake](https://earthquake.usgs.gov/earthquakes/events/alaska1964/).  
**Report:** **“Alaska Is Rocked by Violent Earthquake”** described shaking, coastal waves, fires, and disrupted communities in the immediate aftermath.  
**Assessment:** The earthquake occurred on **March 27 Alaska local time** (March 28 UTC) and measured **magnitude 9.2**, the most powerful recorded in U.S. history. The newspaper dated the next day; **its earliest casualty reports are not final** and immediate tsunami damage must be distinguished from direct shaking damage.

**HN-647 — July 4, 1964 — Civil Rights Act signed; Black weekly reports the long legislative fight**  
**Source:** *Indianapolis Recorder*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=INR19640704-01.1.1); [National Archives, signed Civil Rights Act](https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/civil-rights-act).  
**Report:** **“Johnson Signs Rights Bill”** detailed passage of the landmark legislation proposed under Kennedy and enacted by **President Lyndon Johnson** after a prolonged struggle in Congress.  
**Assessment:** The act was signed **July 2**, before the July 4 issue. Its protections against discrimination were an enormous **legal change**, not proof that exclusion, racist intimidation, or discriminatory employment practices instantly ended. It also did **not** remove the need for the 1965 Voting Rights Act.

**HN-648 — August 4, 1964 — *Maddox* confrontation portrayed as an unprovoked attack**  
**Source:** *Decatur Daily Democrat*, p. 1 — [original page](https://newspapers.library.in.gov/?a=d&d=DCDD19640804.1.1); [National Security Agency, declassified Gulf of Tonkin records](https://www.nsa.gov/helpful-links/nsa-foia/declassification-transparency-initiatives/historical-releases/gulf-of-tonkin/).
